### Tracking Representations in LLM Reasoning Traces for Iterated Games
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23403) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23403) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23403) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23403)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Large language model (LLM) agents achieve state-of-the-art performance across domains through chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. Despite their ubiquitous use, little is understood about the relationship between the externalized trace and the model's actual computation. We address this by investigating how LLM agents make decisions in iterative games with incomplete information. Using a series of mechanistic interpretability techniques, we find that the model forms and iteratively revises an internal representation of the game throughout the CoT. Once the model prematurely commits to an incorrect belief, it rarely recovers. This failure is manifested in both the literal trace and the layer activations. Ultimately, as multi-agent systems are more prevalent, our work opens the door to a more focused diagnosis of their strategic reasoning errors. For illustrative purposes, we offer an interactive visualizer of our methodology on selected sample traces at https://interp-self.github.io/gametrace/

### Modeling LLM Reasoning Dynamics with Structural Representations
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23749) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23749) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23749) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23749)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Language-model reasoning alternates between sustained activities and local operations. We study how this temporal structure predicts continuation and the computation needed to analyze it. T/E/S combines text, local events, and spans of activity. Across reasoning and information-seeking tasks, events and spans improve next-outcome prediction in 70 of 76 evaluation groups for a Transformer and 74 of 76 for Qwen with LoRA. Span duration and preceding events are associated with transitions, and retaining event history across span boundaries improves prediction. We formulate budget-allocation counting and action-conditional continuation queries, prove counting hardness under explicit encodings, and derive conditions for affordable decision certification. In finite-process experiments, bounds that cancel shared continuation mass recover the same optimal actions as full dynamic programming while reducing mean inference CPU time by 77.7%. On 71 held-out HotpotQA and MuSiQue questions, a compact structural observer improves prediction of whether a fixed inference procedure finishes within a CPU allowance: Brier loss falls from 0.0836 to 0.0756. An annotation audit identifies substantial errors in that observer, separating this computational result from semantic validation of its features.

### Depth Before Breadth: Reverse-to-Forward Curriculum with Advantage Decomposition for LLM Agent
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24697) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24697) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24697) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24697)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a common approach for training large language model (LLM) agents on long-horizon tasks. However, learning from sparse terminal rewards makes credit assignment and exploration difficult. We approach these challenges through a policy-induced capability region, the set of states from which the current policy can reliably complete a task. Near its boundary, an agent may need only a few actions to reach a more solvable state, even when the goal remains distant. This reduces long-horizon learning to two coupled subproblems of reaching that state and completing the task from there. We address them with paired advantages and a progressive reverse-to-forward curriculum. The advantages provide separate signals for reaching more solvable states and completing the task from them, while the curriculum expands the capability region backward along successful reference trajectories and then into off-reference states through on-policy exploration. On ALFWorld and WebShop, our method outperforms competitive agentic RL methods at both model scales, achieving success rates of 90.1%/96.1% and 80.2%/82.6% with Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B-Instruct, respectively.

### GUARD: Stabilizing Recursive Self-Improvement with Regret Guidance
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23476) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23476) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23476) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23476)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Autonomous AI agents pair a frozen base model with an editable harness of prompts, skills, tool rules, and memory. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) adapts this harness from experience without weight updates, yet self-editing without external graders often backfires: reflection and scalar scores conflate what an edit newly broke with what remains unsolved, causing local regressions, skill forgetting, and memory bloat. We introduce GUARD (Grader-free Unsupervised Environment Design [UED] with Adaptive Rule Distillation), a closed-loop framework for reference-free continual harness evolution. Our key insight is to decompose each task into pre-execution self-checks and guide harness updates via directional regret. Each round grades trajectories against these checks, pinning mastered rules while prioritizing newly broken behaviors, and selects among parallel candidate edits by critic-free group-relative fitness. A Soft-HURT gate then promotes an edit only when gains outweigh regressions, scoping specialized fixes to prevent over-triggering and guaranteeing non-regression on its gated buffer. At test time, one frozen harness runs in a single pass. Across benchmarks on memory, skill evolution, continual task streams, and domain reasoning, GUARD consistently improves performance without catastrophic forgetting or memory pollution while substantially cutting deployment token usage.

### MINT: Meeting-time Indicators for Truncation in Multi-Step Off-Policy RL
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24413) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24413) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24413) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24413)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Many multi-step off-policy temporal-difference methods handle policy mismatch through products of per-step trace coefficients, leading to a trade-off between variance control and long-horizon signal. The maximum trace length consequently becomes a sensitive hyperparameter. We propose MINT (Meeting-time INdicators for Truncation), which replaces these products with a binary stopping indicator: one until coupled behavior and target trajectories first meet, zero thereafter. At the true value function, the indicator's idempotence yields a variance bound governed by meeting-time survival. For practical implementation, a single sampled action provides a model-free surrogate for the stopping rule, requiring no transition model or mixing-time estimate. We derive separate sufficient conditions for contraction of the state-coupled operator and its practical surrogate under fixed-policy evaluation. On MuJoCo and DeepMind Control tasks, MINT outperforms the reported multi-step baselines; the main MuJoCo comparison uses a common cap without per-task cap-length tuning. We also demonstrate the effectiveness of MINT on Atari 100k, RWRL, and OGBench experiments; MINT adapts the backup horizon to reduce target variability relative to long fixed-horizon backups across tasks and dynamics.

### Which Tokens, in What Order, and How Fast? A Theoretical Analysis of On-Policy Distillation Objectives
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23397) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23397) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23397) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23397)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student language model to match a teacher model based on its own generations. When using OPD, one must choose whether to apply it at the sequence or token level and which divergence to minimize. However, despite the wide adoption of OPD in practice, the formal understanding of which OPD objective is appropriate for a given setting is limited. Toward addressing this gap, we theoretically analyze how the choice of OPD objective governs which tokens are prioritized, in what order, and how efficiently. Our analysis reveals that sequence-level OPD exhibits a future agreement bias, which myopic token-level objectives avoid. This bias induces a reverse learning order, in which sequence-level OPD first matches the teacher at later token positions, while at earlier positions it can favor tokens that are improbable under the teacher, and thus often undesirable. As for the choice of divergence, we prove that it strongly influences the amenability of OPD to gradient-based optimization. In particular, while the forward KL divergence enjoys a well-behaved objective landscape, other $f$-divergences, such as reverse KL, can suffer from vanishing gradients even when the student and teacher are far apart. Across language model post-training settings, we demonstrate the implications of these theoretical findings for which OPD objective is preferable, highlighting the importance of accounting for the interplay between the student, teacher, and task at hand.

### Replay, Don't Resample: Off-Policy Consolidation of Long-Horizon RL Experts
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24059) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24059) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24059) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24059)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Frontier post-training follows a common recipe: train one expert per domain with reinforcement learning (RL), then consolidate the experts into one model via multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD). Such consolidation is expensive: the student samples in every environment again, an expert scores every sampled token, and the student inherits the drift of the final experts. Yet we argue that the experts' RL runs have already produced what consolidation needs: rollouts with their sampling probabilities and rewards, in the order the experts learned from them. We propose multi-expert replay policy distillation (MRPD), which replays these training logs through the student in the experts' order. At each step, the policy gradient is recomputed on these scored trajectories, and the final experts' distributions are distilled at the logged prefixes. Following the experts' own trajectory reduces the training-inference mismatch and keeps the student within the clip range, so the replay is off-policy in origin but quasi-on-policy throughout consolidation. MRPD samples nothing and calls no environment or reward model: the trainer is the sole process, and consolidating the experts costs only about a seventh of the compute used to train them. On three long-horizon agentic domains (reasoning, search and SQL), MRPD yields the best student model, substantially outperforming online RL baselines, and surpassing MOPD from the same experts at a fraction of MOPD's cost. Our analysis on the same logs shows that the replayed trajectories carry most of the gain, and that both the policy gradient and the distillation term add to it. Most interestingly, the order of the logs matters more than their amount or rewards, suggesting that expert training logs are a curriculum, not a data mixture.

### Rethinking Fixed-Horizon Latent Reasoning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24001) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24001) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24001) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24001)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Existing latent reasoners typically run for a preset latent horizon during inference. Intuitively, a longer horizon should provide more opportunity for reasoning. However, in this work, we find that extending latent Chain-of-Thought (CoT) to the full preset horizon may in fact degrade reasoning performance. We observe that latent CoT evolves through distinct functional stages, with earlier computation forming frontier reasoning states, while later computation mainly reorganizes existing candidates and can weaken gold candidate support. These findings motivate a rethink of fixed-horizon latent inference. To address this, we characterize the reasoning benefits of latent CoT by realized and remaining constructive progress. Empirically, we find that higher realized constructive progress corresponds to higher current reasoning accuracy, while more remaining progress corresponds to larger future gains from continued latent CoT. To this end, we introduce a lightweight method that estimates the remaining reasoning progress and adaptively adjusts the latent horizon during inference. Across representative latent CoT reasoners and multiple arithmetic benchmarks, our method improves reasoning accuracy by up to 7.2 percentage points while reducing latent computation by up to 29.0%, without retraining the LLM.

### Self-Rewarded Reinforcement Learning for Better-Structured Medical Explanations in Small Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24650v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24650v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24650v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24650v1)

2026/11/18 15:52 GTM

Small language models (SLMs) can run on commodity hardware and support privacy-preserving, on-premises clinical deployment, but their medical explanations are often short and poorly structured compared with large language models. Recent supervised approaches such as Meerkat transfer chain-of-thought reasoning into 7B-8B backbones, yet their training objective does not directly target the style or organization of the generated rationales. Building on the instruction-tuned Mistral-7B backbone, we investigate whether a lightweight reinforcement learning (RL) step can improve explanation structure without human preference labels.We apply Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with LoRA adaptation to 2,000 Meerkat-derived medical prompts, guided by a simple self-supervised reward that favors longer, lexically diverse, and well-punctuated responses while penalizing very short ones. Training for about 170 PPO steps on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU yields a stable increase in the average reward. Quantitative and qualitative comparisons show that the RL-tuned model produces longer, more organized, multi-point explanations while preserving the underlying medical content.We discuss the promise and the limitations of self-rewarded RL for shaping the style of medical explanations.

### Communicative On-Policy Distillation: Distilling Black-Box Teacher Capabilities through Communication for Long-Horizon Tasks
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23673) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23673) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23673) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23673)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

On-policy distillation (OPD) is a powerful paradigm for improving language-model agents on long-horizon tasks, yet its conventional formulation relies on teacher logits that text-only black- box models do not expose. To address this limitation, we introduce Communicative On-Policy Distillation (COPD), which uses natural-language communication as a medium for distilling a stronger model's capabilities into a smaller student. During task execution, the student au- tonomously decides when to consult a black-box teacher and formulates open-ended natural- language requests without predefined question templates or intervention schedules. Successful student-teacher interactions are retained as privileged references to supervise fresh adviser-free student rollouts through on-policy self-distillation, jointly with task-reward reinforcement learning. On CAMG-II, COPD achieves 2.46 times the gain of the strongest black-box OPD baseline. The capabilities it learns through communication generalize to Terminal-Bench 2.1 and SWE-Bench Pro V2, improving task success without adviser access.

### LProp: Propagating Latent Uncertainty through Evidence-Conditioned Belief Revision in LLM Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23564) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23564) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23564) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23564)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

When LLM agents pass intermediate judgments downstream without uncertainty, later decisions may treat tentative assumptions as facts. Effective uncertainty propagation must preserve information for correction and update inherited beliefs as new evidence arrives. Yet scalar scores omit information needed for revision, while detailed descriptions and reflection incur additional costs. We introduce Latent Uncertainty, a latent-space estimator that preserves uncertainty-relevant information without lengthy descriptions. Building on this, we propose LProp (Latent Uncertainty Propagation), which revises inherited answer distributions using latent representations and new evidence without additional trajectory sampling. We evaluate both methods across multiple-choice, yes/no, open-ended question answering, and mathematical reasoning tasks. Our approach outperforms baselines on most metrics, while LProp achieves the lowest mean inference-token usage in all eight propagation settings. Matched feature ablations further demonstrate that inherited latent content improves error recovery beyond answer probabilities or model capacity alone.

### Idempotency Is Not Recovery: A Fault-Injection Study of Language-Model Agent Tool Actions
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23353) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23353) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23353) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23353)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

When a language-model agent's tool call is interrupted, the action may already have changed an external system without confirmation. Retrying risks duplication; assuming success may conceal an action that never occurred. We study these failure modes in a controlled agent runtime combining authorization, an action ledger, target-side idempotency, reconciliation, and evidence sealing. We inject ten single-host fault classes and compare four configurations and three ablations using a target-side oracle separate from the runtime ledger. On each of Windows and Linux, 7,513 trials used 96 captured model responses from 24 constructed cases. In 200 known original crash and replay pairs, the full configuration recorded 0/200 repeated effects versus 117/200 for each simpler baseline. A ledger-related ablation had 170/170 orphaned effects versus 0/170 and 200/300 recovery successes versus 300/300 for the full configuration. Disabling reconciliation left 100/100 known post-effect crash outcomes ambiguous despite no observed duplicates. A separate supplement designed after the initial results and reusing the same inputs found a similar pattern under response loss or delay. The full runtime had about one-seventh of fail-open throughput, while injected evidence-write faults left 75/100 trials per platform unresolved. These findings distinguish duplicate suppression from recovery and identify their measured reliability and performance tradeoffs within the tested benchmark.

### Chain-of-Thought Hidden States Reveal Task-Solving Directions for Direct Answers
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24598v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24598v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24598v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24598v1)

2026/11/18 15:25 GTM

Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting improves the performance of Large Language Models by eliciting intermediate reasoning steps. However, CoT is far from perfect: on some tasks, models perform worse than with direct-answer prompting, and on others they generate a few hundred tokens to reach the correct answer. We start by labelling each CoT hidden state as recovering if patching it into the direct prompt yields the correct answer, and failed otherwise. A linear probe shows that the model encodes these two classes differently. We then show that the representation space of the direct prompt holds a direction for each question that leads the model, in a short generation, to the correct answer more often than CoT does. Specifically, we rotate the direct prompt's last hidden state once, before generation, towards a direction built for each question from its own recovering and failed states. Across 25 tasks, these directions raise the direct prompt's mean accuracy from $46\%$ to $85\%$ on LLaMA 3.1 8B (CoT: $69\%$) and from $52\%$ to $86\%$ on Qwen 2.5 7B (CoT: $77\%$), with a tenth of CoT's tokens. CoT is thus not the shortest route to this accuracy, and these directions can be used in future work to train models to align their states with them.

### A Mechanistic Analysis of In-Context Learning and Out-of-Context Hallucination
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24000) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24000) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24000) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24000)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Large language models can infer and follow specific rules through in-context learning (ICL), yet may fail to remain faithful to provided evidence, giving rise to out-of-context (OOC) hallucination. Existing work has largely investigated the underlying mechanisms of ICL and OOC hallucination along separate lines. However, this separation obscures the mechanistic basis for these seemingly opposing outcomes. In this work, we study this contrast through the unified view of functional roles in dynamic context. The key observation is that role information can be internally represented without reliably constraining output. We derive a first-order decomposition to characterize the role effect, showing that its substantial influence depends on two necessary conditions, namely sufficient contribution magnitude and coherent signed aggregation. Causal analysis further shows that role span contributions affect key token choices involving response structure, content selection, and factual values. We further show that role-consistent choices depend on sufficient role span support at these key decisions, while failing choices can be reversed when this support overcomes the deficit. These findings support a common mechanistic view of ICL and OOC hallucination: each functional role defines the pattern of output decisions over which role span contributions must remain effective, and differences in this organization account for the seemingly opposing outcomes.

### RePlan-VLA: Progress-Aware Replanning for Long-Horizon Vision-Language-Action Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23843) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23843) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23843) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23843)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as a promising paradigm for general-purpose robotic manipulation, yet their performance often degrades on long-horizon tasks that require multiple interdependent manipulations. Instead of burdening the VLA policies with long-horizon reasoning, memory, action generation, progress tracking, and error recovery, we argue for leveraging their short-horizon manipulation capabilities within an execution-aware framework. To this end, we introduce \textnormal{\textbf{RePlan-VLA}}, a VLA-agnostic framework that translates a VLA's short-horizon capabilities into reliable long-horizon execution. RePlan-VLA explicitly monitors the robot's motion for progress evaluation, and adaptively revises the future plan based on observed outcomes. We preserve the underlying VLA policy entirely, requiring no additional fine-tuning. Experiments on RoboCasa365 and CALVIN benchmarks show an improvement of 21.9% and 10.4% in success rate over the next strongest baseline. We also validate our approach for real-world deployment on a Franka Research 3 robot.

### JEPA as a Transferable Optimizer: Learning and Understanding Black-Box Optimization from Reward-Agnostic Pretraining
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23608) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23608) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23608) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23608)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Black-box optimization evaluates candidates through simulations, rollouts, or experiments, often without gradients. Learned optimizers are usually judged by whether they outperform existing methods. We ask a complementary question: what information does a learned optimizer use from its search history? We introduce JEPA-PS (JEPA Policy Search), based on a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture. It is pretrained on random rollouts using parameter vectors and scalar returns. Its latent-prediction objective predicts the next point in an optimization trajectory rather than its reward or gradient, allowing us to examine what was tried and how well it performed. At test time, JEPA-PS reads a short evaluation history and proposes a candidate in one forward pass, without task descriptions, reward shaping, or test-time gradients. We evaluate it on 15 control environments against 12 optimizers, including evolutionary methods, Bayesian optimization, CMA-ES, and an LLM optimizer. JEPA-PS is competitive with tuned classical baselines and outperforms the LLM optimizer by more than 2x on Swimmer at 71x lower per-step cost. It also transfers zero-shot to environments excluded from pretraining. Null-model comparisons and decoder-training ablations indicate that its latent representation carries information beyond simple history statistics; every trained decoder variant performs worse than the default frozen representation. Our main finding is that the history JEPA-PS uses depends on the task. On smooth landscapes it uses the full trajectory, whereas on rugged landscapes the best-seen point carries nearly all the signal. This memory requirement has a weak positive correlation with fitness-distance correlation (FDC; r=0.112, n=7), while an independent reward-ordering sensitivity probe does not corroborate the relationship (r=-0.024, n=7). Code: https://github.com/mdebnath22/jepa-props.

### Probing agent overeagerness in realistic settings with continuation audits
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24491) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24491) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24491) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24491)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Frontier coding agents increasingly exhibit overeagerness, taking surprising, sometimes reckless actions beyond a user's mandate --- reported in recent system cards from frontier model developers. These cases are surfaced through internal deployment monitoring, which requires private traffic and is out of reach for open-weight models and independent evaluators. Automated alignment audits are the natural alternative, but capable models increasingly recognise the synthetic setup and adjust their behaviour. We introduce Continuation Audits, a pipeline that grounds the auditor on a prefix drawn from a real coding-agent transcript and directs it to continue the trajectory toward a seeded behaviour. Because the prefix is drawn from a real session, the auditor only needs to maintain a realistic setting rather than manufacturing it. We validate the method on SWE-Chat prefixes: on a paired subset of 90 audits a judge cannot reliably distinguish continuations from their source sessions (realism win rate 0.42 vs.\ 0.43), and across all 900 audits verbalized eval-awareness does not exceed the rate observed in the unaudited real sessions. Applying the pipeline to three recent models across three seeds, we find each model leads a different seed --- DeepSeek~V4~Pro on colleague-credentials misuse, GPT-5.6~Terra on file-permission bypasses, and Kimi~K3 on treating diagnostic questions as instructions. Kimi~K3 additionally continues to bypass file-permission protections after an explicit CLAUDE.md prohibition while the other two stop entirely, corroborating Moonshot's own report of excessive proactiveness.

### Think Offline, Act in Real Time: Self-Improving Visual Game Agents through Code
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24590) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24590) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24590) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24590)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Pre-trained models can read screens and reason about games, yet on an unfamiliar game, an agent still has to learn to play. We ask how an agent can self-improve on such a game from a few of its own attempts, in real-time visual games where the agent sees only the screen and the game keeps running while the agent thinks. We study two kinds of policy that can carry this improvement. In model as policy, a model selects actions during play; we adapt real-time chunking so that committed actions keep executing while the model reasons, and train a 2B controller for this interface. In code as policy, a model writes, tests, and revises a program between attempts, and the program acts during play. Across 170 GameWorld tasks, with at most eight one-minute executions per run, code as policy reaches 47.80% mean progress, against 30.90% for our trained controller, and leads in all five game categories. Improving the controller's weights on a single game, by contrast, yields only modest gains. These results favor letting a model think offline and storing what it learns in a program that acts in real time. Project repository: https://github.com/TabibitoQZP/SIGame

### BaseRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement of a Base Vision-Language Model via Training-Free Harness Internalization
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24578v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24578v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24578v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24578v1)

2026/11/18 15:10 GTM

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is moving from the harness of prompts and tools around a base model toward the model itself. However, existing RSI still faces key challenges, including harness-bound gains that stay outside the base model, black-box supervision that reads only the model's outputs, and imprecise edits whose site and strength are fixed in advance. To address these challenges, we propose BaseRSI, a new paradigm of Recursive Harness Internalization: the base model improves its harness, then writes what the harness adds into its own weights through training-free edits, round after round on its own responses. To support this paradigm, we introduce ReGap, a mechanism that supervises every transformer block with the high-dimensional activation difference that the harness makes at that block alone, and updates the block's weights along this signal in a low-dimensional subspace. Building on this mechanism, we further propose ReKey, an algorithm that writes this signal by regression into a few key-value neurons of every block, and measures the site and strength of each edit on the model's own responses. Moreover, the base model fills every role of this recursion with forward passes alone, and its edits, written at different sites, compose in one checkpoint without refitting. Experimental results on ten visual reasoning benchmarks show that BaseRSI consistently improves a base vision-language model both in distribution and on held-out benchmarks. Our code is available at https://github.com/beita6969/BaseRSI.

### Metrics-in-the-Loop: Complexity Feedback Reshapes Agent-Written Code
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23817) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23817) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23817) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23817)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Coding agents now write whole codebases in long autonomous runs, guided mainly by whether their tests pass. Yet passing tests alone does not reveal accumulating complexity or gaps in test coverage, which then persist as agents extend their own code. We introduce metrics-in-the-loop, an inference-time intervention that gives agents in-flight feedback: cheap, automatically computed code metrics injected into the agent's context during the trajectory. After each code-changing agent turn, we insert short feedback messages naming changed functions whose cyclomatic complexity exceeds a limit (complexity feedback) or changed lines that are skipped by tests (coverage feedback), giving the agent the opportunity to refactor code or strengthen tests before continuing. We run extensive experiments and ablations comparing several interventions across three long-horizon coding benchmarks and four strong models. We find that complexity feedback significantly reduces erosion, a measure of complexity concentration of code, without affecting held-out correctness of the code. Broadly, our experiments investigate a practical form of agent "re-railing", where timely, actionable measurements redirect agents before weaknesses become the foundation they keep building on. As agents scale to codebases with millions of lines, such interventions become critical to sustaining effective development and long-term code maintainability.

### Continual Tool-Space Extension Learning for Multimodal Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23810) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23810) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23810) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23810)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Multimodal agents largely rely on generic tools such as cropping, search, and code execution, leaving specialist vision models outside their tool space. However, bringing such a model into an already trained agent is not straightforward. The challenge is to learn each new tool efficiently while retaining the ability to use existing ones. This work proposes COTEX, continual tool-space extension learning, that adds new tools at efficient adapter-level cost. COTEX locates two sets of parameter directions; one triggers a tool call and thereby opens a multi-turn reasoning trajectory, while the other determines which tool is chosen. The adapter of a new tool is restricted to the calling directions that leave earlier tool choices unaffected by orthogonal constraint. We further introduce a protocol that converts specialist models' existing task-specific annotations into verified tool-use trajectories. Experiments on two representative agents and three specialist tools show gains on ten vision-language benchmarks, with at most a 0.3-point drop on existing tools.

### Learning through Discernment: when a small language model learns from user feedback on the job, and when it decays
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23716) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23716) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23716) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23716)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Small language models on devices could keep improving by learning, overnight, from their own daily work. Without a true outcome, however, something must decide which attempts are worth learning from, and both the model's own judgment and the user's reaction can be wrong. We study a small model (Qwen3 0.6B and 1.7B) with a LoRA adapter, fine-tuned each night on the attempts a judge accepts, over 127 lives of up to a year on WebShop and $τ^2$-bench. We compare the true outcome, the user's stated verdict and the model's own judgment with a separate, frozen "feedback sense" that reads the user's reaction, built in three versions. Judging itself, the model improved and then decayed, over a year and whatever its adapter. A sense that took polite give-ups for success collapsed 3 of 5 lives; one built for discernment, which first asks whether the user gave a clear verdict, kept 4 of 5 near the oracle for two weeks, then faded on clean but scarce lessons, to the level of self-judging by day 60. When the user cannot see the outcome ($τ^2$-bench), the sense read reactions correctly, yet 80% of its lessons were failures the customer had thanked. Across 97 lives, the share of failed attempts among the lessons predicted the outcome (Spearman $ρ$ = -0.81). In our experiments, learning from users held up only where the judge kept failures out and the user could see the outcome; lopsided lessons may also narrow the model.

### Towards Reliable Scaling Laws for Tabular Foundation Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24214) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24214) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24214) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24214)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Scaling laws are widely used to predict foundation model performance and guide compute allocation. However, tabular foundation models (TFMs) differ from conventional foundation models in that they are pretrained on virtually unlimited synthetic data from configurable distributions, while ultimately being evaluated on real-world datasets. We investigate whether scaling laws for TabPFNv2-style models reliably extrapolate to larger compute budgets and generalize across evaluation settings. Our analysis reveals differences between synthetic and real-world scaling behavior and shows that accurately fitting a scaling law does not necessarily yield reliable prescriptions on unseen datasets. In particular, selecting configurations using too few evaluation datasets leads to systematically over-optimistic performance estimates. We further demonstrate that compute-optimal scaling requires expanding the range of synthetic pretraining dataset sizes alongside model size and training compute. Increasing the maximum numbers of rows and features sampled during pretraining improves performance and produces more predictable scaling trends. These insights guide the training of more efficient TabPFNv2-like models, using the open-source training pipeline of nanoTabPFN.

### OpenKAGE: Open-Ended Lifelong Learning in the Open World via Knowledge-Sharing Agent-Group Evolution
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24302) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24302) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24302) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24302)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Building autonomous agents that continually explore open worlds and acquire new capabilities through experience is a long-standing aspiration in artificial intelligence. Existing research often pursues lifelong learning within predefined tasks, curricula, or objectives, with self-improvement largely confined to individual agents. We introduce OpenKAGE, an agent-group evolution framework for open-ended lifelong learning. Within this framework, agents autonomously explore open worlds and continually acquire new capabilities by accumulating and reusing knowledge from their own interactions and the experience of others. OpenKAGE builds on two key mechanisms: (1) a two-layer knowledge representation, with a memory layer for situated memories and an experience layer for transferable experience and skills that retains the applicability conditions needed for reuse; and (2) a knowledge-sharing mechanism that semantically consolidates textual experience into a continuously evolving shared knowledge graph, while executable skills are shared verbatim. In Minecraft, OpenKAGE supports open-ended lifelong learning, enabling agent groups to sustain exploration and reach advanced technological milestones. Furthermore, the accumulated knowledge can be transferred to a newly initialized agent group on a different backbone model, improving its survival and exploration progress.

### How Complete Must an Environment Be to Train a Capable GUI Agent?
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23946) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23946) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23946) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23946)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Graphical user interface (GUI) agents are widely trained in executable GUI environments. To lower cost, build environments in batches, and reduce manual effort, each work trades away part of the environment through its construction choices. We describe these choices along three dimensions: reward coverage, the goal-reaching trajectories the verifier rewards; data coverage, the application data the agent is exposed to beyond what the task reads or writes; and feature coverage, the application features beyond what the task uses. Existing works set each dimension on engineering grounds, but they do not measure its effect on training. How complete must an environment be to train a capable agent, and along which dimensions? In MobileGym, we adjust each dimension separately, train Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct, and evaluate every policy in the original full environment. A verifier that rewards only one reference path lowers success by 10.53 points, and removing unneeded records also lowers success. Pruning unneeded features instead raises success from 51.61% to 56.13%, entirely on tasks whose environments it left unchanged. The gain reverses when each episode is weighted equally and vanishes when only episodes on the pruned tasks are balanced. Without pruning, length weighting there reinforces long detour successes that erode skills on other tasks. Following these findings, we build MobileGym-Lite, which keeps reward and data coverage complete and prunes unneeded features. It outperforms Full in the full environment (55.48% versus 51.61% success) with lower construction cost.

### Piccolo: Co-Evolving Decision Models and Data through Recursive Self-Improvement
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24651v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24651v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24651v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24651v1)

2026/11/18 15:53 GTM

We present Piccolo, a family of multimodal decision models (0.8B to 27B) that coevolves model capabilities and training data via Agentic Data Engineering and recursive self-improvement (RSI). The models predict probabilities over candidate answers for general-purpose text and visual judgments, agent harnesses, data workflows, and interactive games. Piccolo-27B tops the Decision Index Vision Full leaderboard with a score of 75.59, while Piccolo-9B and Piccolo-4B achieve 68.35 and 67.46, respectively. All three rank first on the Private benchmark in their respective model-size categories. Research agents analyze model behavior, construct training data, conduct experiments, and use the results to refine subsequent proposals. Semantic slices and a shared research memory connect these actions across the model family's development. With training recipes comprising hundreds of thousands of examples, data selection and feedback-driven refinement are central to the self-improvement process. Our large-scale RSI exploration yields three counter-intuitive insights for decision-model development: (1) Backbone-recipe co-adaptation outweighs raw model recency: earlier backbones can surpass newer generations on visual tasks under identical recipes; (2) Targeted data curation enables 4B-9B models to rival 27B baselines on specialized workflows; and (3) Continuous loss reduction can decouple from and even degrade discrete decision accuracy. Together with the released weights and tools from the Data-Juicer and AgentScope ecosystems, Piccolo provides an actionable blueprint for building frontier decision models and scaling data-driven RSI.

### GRAFT: Execution-Guided Genetic Recombination for Agent Design
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23509) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23509) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23509) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23509)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Automated agent design searches for executable architectures that improve LLM-based agent performance, with evolutionary methods using language-model crossover to recombine promising mechanisms for task decomposition, model coordination, and verification. This search, however, is typically guided by aggregate fitness alone, which hides the instance-level successes and failures behind each score. We introduce Execution Guided Genetic Recombination for Agent Design (GRAFT), which uses aggregate fitness for selection and survival and parent-specific execution evidence to guide semantic crossover with integrated mutation. Its feedback harness reuses bounded samples of successful and failed runs from fitness evaluation, withholding reference answers and requiring no additional task executions. We evaluate GRAFT across six reasoning and programming settings using open-weight optimizer and executor models. It achieves a mean test accuracy of 82.0%, exceeding the strongest baseline by 9.8% points. Controlled ablations show that adding execution evidence improves test accuracy with initialization, search rules, repair policies, and evaluation budgets held fixed. Our results support the value of parent-specific execution evidence for evolutionary agent design.

### Training Language Models for Introspective Oversight
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23365) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23365) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23365) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23365)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Language models increasingly exhibit behaviors that are difficult to detect in transcripts, such as reward hacking, deception, and sycophancy. Asking the assistant follow-up questions about its actions is unreliable, since models are generally not trained to report their own misbehavior. As a result, approaches to oversight typically involve querying external models. These face limitations: external transcript monitoring operates only on visible model outputs, and activation decoders lack full context and are prone to confabulation. We introduce introspective oversight, which trains a new role in the model to answer arbitrary questions about the assistant, rewarding answers that are verified against the assistant's actual behavior. We implement this by adding a separate, third-person observer role that answers these queries, which we can train solely for accuracy and separately from the assistant. On five held-out oversight evaluations, an observer trained on Qwen3.6-27B obtains a mean AUROC of 0.907, outperforming honesty training (0.864), transcript monitoring (0.828), and activation oracles (0.796). We further find that training an observer makes the assistant itself easier to monitor, with every baseline method improving on average when applied to the fine-tuned assistant. These results suggest that training models to answer verifiable questions about themselves is a tractable approach to overseeing unintended behavior.

### Semantic Context Handoff with On-Demand Evidence for Long-Horizon Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24357) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24357) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24357) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24357)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Traditional long-horizon LLM agents often hand off context through full-history prompting, retaining all previous action--observation interactions. As execution proceeds, this history keeps growing, increasing inference cost and potentially degrading accuracy. Existing methods control this growth by preserving compact conclusions while removing earlier observations, but doing so can discard evidence that later becomes relevant. We introduce Semantic Context Handoff (SCH), whose central abstraction is state--evidence separation: a compact semantic state carries the agent's current interpretation, while supporting action--observation evidence remains separately addressable and can re-enter context when later execution makes it relevant, without separate summarization or retrieval-model calls. Empirically, SCH improves long-horizon agent performance while substantially reducing context cost. In a controlled 150-task SWE-bench study, SCH's evidence recovery improves Pass@1 from $80.0\%$ to $86.9\%$ at essentially unchanged token use and execution time. In the complete-system comparison, SCH achieves 86.9% Pass@1 while using 43% fewer tokens and 41% less wall-clock time than the strongest baseline by task performance. Across models and benchmarks, SCH consistently reduces token use relative to full-history prompting while remaining competitive with or improving upon the evaluated baselines. Trajectory analysis confirms that SCH recovers evidence from substantially earlier parts of the interaction history rather than merely preserving a recent context window.

### Risk-Triggered Search: Behavior-Preserving Safe Control
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24409) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24409) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24409) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24409)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Pre-trained visual world models generate multi-step action plans from current and goal observations, yet terminal-proximity objectives fail to prevent forbidden-zone traversal or rigid-body collision sliding. Purely visual interfaces may lack analytical dynamics, structured states, or explicit collision geometry, requiring internal prediction of action-conditioned process risk. We propose Risk-Triggered Search (RTS) for frozen Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world models. Rather than relying on static state features that cannot distinguish candidate plans sharing an observation, RTS unrolls action-conditioned future latent prefixes in the frozen world model and trains a lightweight risk head with process-violation supervision. To intervene selectively while preserving safe nominal behavior, RTS uses the same predicted prefix-risk criterion as both the search trigger gate and hard feasibility gate: safe nominal plans execute directly, whereas alarms trigger local Cross-Entropy Method (CEM) search to minimize nominal deviation within the feasible set. To prevent isotropic Euclidean minimal modification from shrinking obstacle-directed actions into zero-amplitude stalling, we introduce an amplitude-preserving metric $d_{\mathrm{AP}}$ that retains tangential progression while maintaining a unique zero at the nominal plan. Under unified gating and this unique-zero condition, bypassing safe-state search yields closed-loop trajectories strictly identical to every-step same-kernel search. Evaluated under a process-safety benchmark and Safe Arrival Rate (SAR) metric across five tasks, RTS improves SAR over Direct by 12.67--41.67 percentage points on navigation and articulated control and +2.08/+4.17 pp on Cube/PushT boundary cohorts, maintaining 100% action identity with every-step search while cutting controller time by 17.71%--70.41% across 1,968 paired trajectories.

### MLE Agents: Autonomous ML Engineering in Large-scale Recommendation Systems
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23910) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23910) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23910) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23910)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

The typical workflow for improving large-scale ranking and recommendation models is slow and tedious: an engineer forms a hypothesis, implements it as a code change, trains a model, and evaluates it both offline and online, often relying on their own intuition and experience along the way. Autonomous LLM agents promise to speed this up by running the loop on their own, but it is still unclear whether they can do this in industry-scale systems rather than on benchmarks or small proxy tasks. We build human-on-the-loop machine learning engineer (MLE) agents that propose, implement, train, and evaluate model changes on large-scale search rankers. Engineers retain control over objective design, search direction, and final-model review. We identify three obstacles that make this industry setting challenging: feedback takes hours per experiment, offline metrics do not always predict online impact, and improvements saturate after the early wins. We address these with parallel training runs, an objective calibrated to match online impact, and careful context engineering. In one campaign, the system ran 1,097 completed candidate experiments and accepted 325, a 29.6\% acceptance rate compared with 15.6\% in the earlier campaign under a different objective and search constraint. Its best model increases search volume by 0.23\% in an A/B test of large scale. Several improvements were architectural changes the team had never tried, drawn from the broader industry and literature, suggesting that MLE agents can explore regions of the design space that human intuition overlooks. We find that the design of the harness around the model matters: intuition suggests that a stronger underlying model is enough, but in our experience, the design of the loop around it is just as critical. More broadly, our work shows that autonomous agents can do real machine-learning engineering in industry-scale systems.

### RefinementGuard: Counterexample-Directed Semantic Qualification for Hidden Agent-Tool Execution
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23611) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23611) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23611) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23611)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Tool runtimes can lower an agent-selected operation into a hidden parallel graph, reducing latency and context while risking semantic mistranslation. We introduce RefinementGuard: translation validation for hidden, effectful agent-tool lowerings, coupled to artifact-bound, fail-closed runtime admission. Qualification compares a candidate graph with an independent reference on isolated state using explicit parent-operation obligations. We evaluate two repository-level contracts, bug localization and patch validation, on controlled repositories. Among 100 activated, non-equivalent development/qualification faults adjudicated by one human, parent-semantic validation rejects 49/100 (operator-cluster bootstrap 95% CI [33.7%, 64.8%]). The objective is selective admission, not maximum mutation rejection: exact equality rejects 100/100 faults but also all 60 valid variants, whereas obligation-based validation accepts 60/60; its 51 residual misses identify concrete specification gaps. On a frozen disjoint input split, the held-out B2-B3 result is localization-only because 21 patch mutants fail structurally; B3 rejects 38/56 localization defects. In a controlled asynchronous-delay regime, the localization serial/guarded median-latency ratio is 4.50x, and hidden execution exposes 74.3% less parent-visible context than visible execution. Patch validation shows no demonstrated speedup; zero-delay runs expose overhead. End-to-end qualification thus detects lower-layer misses while preserving localization's parallelism.

### Counterfactual World Models: JEPAs That Reason About "What If"
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24778v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24778v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24778v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24778v1)

2026/11/18 17:24 GTM

World models operate in a world they cannot fully observe. When a hidden disturbance, such as a current pushing a boat, changes the outcome of an action, a counterfactual question follows: what would have happened under a different action, subject to the same disturbance? Its answer separates bad decisions from bad luck. We prove that world models trained on factual transitions cannot answer it on their own, whatever their data or capacity. For many physical systems, the observed outcome and a simple property of the dynamics suffice: natura non facit saltus, nature makes no leaps. When actions and disturbances act as separate forces, their interaction is second-order in time, so the counterfactual outcome is, to first order, the observed outcome plus the difference between the world model's predictions under the alternative and factual actions. This yields a counterfactual world model with no counterfactual data, extra parameters, or retraining. For transitions of duration $h$, we prove an $O(h^2)$ bound on the counterfactual error relative to the disturbance variance, establish a matching minimax lower bound from unpaired factual data, and characterize failures at discontinuities, coordinate folds, and disturbances that scale the command. We validate these predictions from pixels with JEPA world models on frozen DINOv2 and masked autoencoder (MAE) encoders. On four rendered simulators, our approach improves counterfactual $R^2$ from $38$--$46\%$ to $86$--$96\%$ with the same world models; on a fifth, an underwater vehicle, it reaches $90$--$94\%$ with sixteen times more factual data. Four further frozen encoders, DINOv3, SigLIP~2, CLIP and EVA-02, give $81$--$95\%$ on the same simulators. In hindsight planning, it finds the action that would have reached the goal in $35$--$69\%$ of events, against $14$--$23\%$ for an oracle planner that knows the simulator but not the outcome.

Enterprise LLM agents operate in dynamic environments where foundation models evolve rapidly, workflows update frequently, and deployments need to satisfy accuracy and cost requirements. We propose ARISE (Agent Refinement through Iterative Self-Evaluation), a practical memory architecture in which the agent's memory evolves from its own failures without updating model weights, tools, or workflow logic. ARISE uses the model itself or a teacher model to attribute the failures and propose corrections. Then, ARISE keeps the corrections as retrievable micro memory, consolidates them into macro rules, and accepts a rule update only if held-out accuracy rises. Across 14 enterprise-workflow domains from SOP-Bench and $τ^2$-bench using 5 models, ARISE-Teacher, which uses Claude Opus 4.6 for attribution and consolidation, gains 10.9 pp over the baseline on SOP-Bench and 5.8 pp on $τ^2$-bench. ARISE-Teacher also outperforms GEPA by 4.5 pp on $τ^2$-bench and 1.4 pp on SOP-Bench. With the deployment model as its own attributor, ARISE-Self still gains 10.0 pp on SOP-Bench. ARISE-Teacher uses 4.0% fewer model calls and 5.2% fewer output tokens at comparable per-task cost (-1.0%).

### automodel: A Skill for Agentic Model Discovery
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24347) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24347) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24347) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24347)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

The search for better models, whether expressed as analytical equations or neural architectures, has increasingly shifted from manual design toward data-driven automation. While a multitude of algorithms exist, ranging from exhaustive search to AlphaEvolve, recent advances in general-purpose coding agents such as Claude Code and OpenAI Codex raise the question: could providing such systems with a "model discovery recipe" be enough to discover or improve models? We present automodel, an agent skill for general model discovery. With case studies in astrophysics, cardiovascular dynamics, precision agriculture, traffic flow, and vehicle tire dynamics, we show how commercial agents equipped with automodel can successfully identify novel and scientifically-relevant models. Our findings suggest that encoding model-discovery methodology as a portable agent skill can provide a simple, interactive, powerful and broadly applicable alternative to domain-specific automated discovery pipelines. The skill is available at: https://github.com/unlayer-ai/automodel.

### Response-Relevant Information Is Not Sufficient for Neural Training-State Equivalence
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23245) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23245) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23245) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23245)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Response-relevant information is not sufficient evidence that two neural networks are in the same training state. We define training-state equivalence operationally: two checkpoints are equivalent, relative to an intervention family and horizon, if they respond alike when training is perturbed and continues. On fresh runs of a one-layer transformer learning modular addition, response to a weight-decay pulse is highly reproducible relative to between-checkpoint differences (rho_state = 0.934 at 500 steps), establishing the target as measurable. Fifteen descriptors of weight spectra, representation geometry and optimiser geometry carry within-run response-relevant information beyond weight norm and loss on a development dataset, predicting held-out response deviation across seed blocks. But their apparent 16.1% advantage at matching checkpoints by future response does not separate from a conditional placebo preserving each descriptor's fitted dependence on the conventional observables, and a calibrated positive control makes simple insensitivity an unlikely explanation. Information a decoder can extract need not be organised in the pairwise geometry that similarity judgments require.

### Late Entry in Looped Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24576v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24576v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24576v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24576v1)

2026/11/18 15:09 GTM

Looped language models deepen reasoning at a compact parameter count by iterating a shared layer stack T times per token, turning recurrence depth directly into sequential decoding latency. Conventional early-exit decoding halts after initial passes without cache completion, truncating cached key-value states of past tokens to shallow depth. We study the opposite strategy, late entry, where new tokens skip initial passes and enter directly into the final K < T passes over a deep context cache. Once lightweight fine-tuning resolves hidden-state norm mismatch at the entry boundary, deep cached context substitutes for skipped token-local passes, allowing late entry to outperform matched early exit across model families and pass budgets. To turn this asymmetry into wall-clock speedups without stalling to build deep caches, candidate blocks verify through only the final K passes on the critical path while previously committed tokens complete passes 1 -> T alongside subsequent steps at zero extra serial calls. Projecting these finished T-pass wake states inside the existing output GEMM (Wake verification, governed by logit-margin tolerance tau in [0, inf]) unifies greedy-exact T-pass decoding (tau = 0, matching K = T block decoding within bf16 reduction order), margin-gated verification (tau > 0), and non-rollback lossy late entry (tau = inf) inside a single static CUDA graph. On Ouro-2.6B (T = 4), K = 2 Wake reaches 5.2x greedy-exact speedup at tau = 0 (+26% over K = 4 block decoding on GSM8K) and 6.8x at tau = inf (+16.9 points over trained early exit at matched speed). Scaling test-time recurrence across T in {8, 16, 32, 64} on Huginn-3.5B cuts K = T/4 rollbacks 21.5x with depth, driving greedy-exact (tau = 0) speedups to 6.1x (T = 32) and 8.4x (T = 64, +153% over K = 64 block) and tau = inf speedups to 10.6 to 12.5x.

### Reducing Inference Cost of Coding Agents with Timeout Cascades
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24324) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24324) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24324) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24324)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

AI coding agents have become one of the most popular applications of large language models. Yet, agentic AI comes at the cost of a tremendous amount of GPU compute resources, which limits its profitability and its viability at scale. In this paper, we investigate whether smaller, cheaper models can cut this cost without sacrificing accuracy. We evaluate 36 popular large and small open-weight models released since 2024 on a software engineering benchmark, and find that recent small models are closing the accuracy gap compared to their large counterparts. We investigate the structure of this gap and find that the gap is highly concentrated around hard tasks that small language models fail to solve while achieving remarkably good accuracy for easy and medium complexity tasks. Following this observation, we show that by utilizing a hybrid inference strategy that tries a small model first and escalates to a large one only when needed, we can achieve up to 33% reduction in total GPU-hour costs and 16.4% on all tasks from SWE-bench Verified, without sacrificing accuracy. Strikingly, this simple cascade nearly matches an oracle-style task router, highlighting a practical path for deploying cost-aware coding agents in production without complex learned task routers and with minimal engineering complexity.

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

We investigate whether a weight edit can alter how a model trades off honoring a commitment against benefiting more people. In a concept-supervised text model, we alter fitted choice weights so that the benefit required to override a formal pledge is larger than for an ordinary promise. Across six independently trained text readers, the edit selects the larger group in both orders on 100% of targeted ordinary conflicts and 83.3% of targeted formal conflicts. It preserves the source model's success rates on smaller conflicts and its factual accuracy. The remaining formal-conflict failures coincide with errors reading a beneficiary number. A separate classifier demonstrates why this distinction matters: reusing a factual answer passes an unconditional choice test but fails to preserve small-gap promises. In a YOLO11n pilot on COCO128, a bias edit increased small-target matches from 3/43 to 9/43, and an equivalent score calibration reproduced the retained detections. These exploratory results show a way to test selective changes to learned priorities in a model with an exposed trade-off mechanism. Whether the method can discover such changes in less structured language models remains open.

### How Reward Gain Imbalance Shapes Ranking: Rethinking Rubric-Based Reward Aggregation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24005) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24005) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24005) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24005)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Rubric-based reinforcement learning constructs rewards for aligning large language models with human preferences, typically by equally aggregating continuous scores that a judge assigns to individual criteria. However, scoring heterogeneity across criteria can distort candidate rankings. Existing work has focused on improving judges, learning criterion weights, or refining aggregation, whereas the interaction between scoring heterogeneity and ranking remains insufficiently understood. We formalize this heterogeneity as \emph{reward gain imbalance}: each criterion enters the aggregated reward with its own reward gain, the gap between its expected scores on satisfied and unsatisfied responses. Counterintuitively, our experiments show that removing this imbalance, even with ground-truth labels, lowers ranking accuracy. To explain this, we derive an exact decomposition of the reward difference between any two candidates into a base satisfaction signal, a reward gain imbalance term, and a residual term, showing that reward gain imbalance can attenuate or amplify the base signal, and finding that misrankings usually concentrate on pairs where it attenuates. Guided by this analysis, we propose the adaptive \textbf{D}ual-\textbf{C}enter \textbf{C}lipping aggregation (DCC), which bounds each criterion's contribution to the reward difference instead of simply removing the imbalance. Using only the scores of the current candidate group, it determines a clipping interval that imposes a common upper bound on per-criterion score differences between candidates, limiting the excessive influence of extreme scores while preserving continuous judgment information....

### SCAFFOLD: Toward Warrant-Preserving Evidence Transport for Reusable LLM-Agent Skills
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23990) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23990) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23990) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23990)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

An agent skill that succeeded yesterday may be executable today without being justified for reuse today. Tests may cover only observable contract clauses, outcome oracles may be absent, evaluations may inherit the same erroneous specification, and permissions or dependencies may change between validation and invocation. We argue that the appropriate unit of prospective trust is a Warranted Reuse Claim (WRC): a defeasible, claim-specific authorization tied to a versioned skill, target context, evidence, assumptions, and identified defeaters. The paper sharpens this position through Warrant-Preserving Evidence Transport (WPET), a proposed discipline for deciding which historical evidence remains admissible after context change. It distinguishes evidence admissibility from truth of the supported safety or effectiveness claim; states a conditional preservation result and its limits; specifies three-valued applicability and asymmetric context-transfer judgments; and illustrates how provenance dependence and policy-invalidating changes alter authorization. We explicitly relate the proposal to assurance cases, contracts, and skill-library maintenance rather than claiming novelty for their ingredients. A falsifiable longitudinal evaluation would compare WRC-guided policies with strong version-aware baselines at matched coverage, utility, and review cost. This paper contributes a formalized problem statement, testable design obligations, and reporting protocol, not an implemented verifier, empirical superiority result, or proof of universal agent safety.

### Openhagen Worlds: Calibrated Environments for Measuring and Training Scientific Discovery Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24711v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24711v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24711v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24711v1)

2026/11/18 16:36 GTM

Agents that do science must choose informative experiments and reason from their results, but most discovery benchmarks cannot separate that skill from recall, unstated scoring rules or shortcut policies. We introduce Openhagen Worlds, a suite of 27 procedurally generated environments (some with finite truth spaces), from classic puzzles of experimental reasoning to simulator-grounded physics and chemistry, in which an agent runs experiments under a budget and commits an answer that a deterministic verifier checks against a hidden truth. Every observation states the complete scoring rule, and each of the 178 difficulty levels is calibrated: it is admitted only if, on 64 development instances, a scripted oracle succeeds on at least 0.75, the strongest plausible non-deductive policy on at most 0.25 and each gated shortcut on at most 0.35. The 92 core levels span all 27 families and are sized to train small models; the 86 frontier rungs extend the 15 discovery worlds with harder questions and with campaigns of three, five and eight or more committed stages on one hidden system. On the same 84 withheld frontier instances, one per family and rung, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash solves 32 at its default reasoning effort with 16,384 tokens per call and 57 at its maximum effort with up to 393,216 tokens per call (oracle: 79); the headroom that remains is largest in eight-stage campaigns. On-policy expert iteration gives a 9B model modest gains: its core-tier success on trained families rises from 0.090 to 0.148, partly through better action format, with no detectable loss on GPQA Diamond. A hosted service plays the core tier's withheld instances.

### Inherited Adapter Experts for Replay-Free Domain-Incremental Learning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24145) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24145) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24145) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24145)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Replay-free domain-incremental learning trains a model on the same classes in successive domains without keeping earlier images. Per-domain learners freeze a pre-trained ViT and train an additional expert for each new domain: the small set of parameters and the classification head learned on that domain. Test images carry no domain label, so a router selects one expert for each image. Recent work trains this router. However, a training-free Mahalanobis router costs the analyzed learners under 2.7 points of final accuracy relative to always selecting each image's own-domain expert. The experts, not this router, limit accuracy. Our learner, Lamarck, therefore uses this router and strengthens the experts. Its adapters, small trainable layers that correct every image token after each deep ViT block, add 4.4 points of final accuracy on the deepfake benchmark CDDB-Hard. In addition, each expert is initialized from its predecessor at no extra cost: the domains share their classes, so an image misrouted to a later expert loses less. Against twelve frozen-backbone baselines under one protocol, Lamarck is first on CDDB-Hard by 5.2 points, second on DomainNet and OfficeHome, and highest in mean accuracy.

### Harvesting Expert-Parallel Bubbles for Fine-Tuning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24496) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24496) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24496) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24496)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

GPUs are expensive, yet latency-critical large language model (LLM) inference leaves part of them idle. Colocation reclaims this capacity by running best-effort work, such as fine-tuning, alongside serving. Many leading LLMs now use a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture, which adds a new source of idle time. Under expert parallelism, MoE experts are spread across GPUs, and because the router sends uneven numbers of tokens to each GPU, lightly loaded GPUs wait for the most loaded one. Even after applying load balancing solutions, such bubbles still take 8--13% of prefill time. We present BubbleTea, the first colocation system that leverages expert-parallel bubbles for fine-tuning. Unlike existing colocation systems, which make one scheduling decision for all GPUs per forward pass, BubbleTea predicts each GPU's bubble at every layer from the router's token assignment, and fills it with short chunks of fine-tuning work that reuse the weights already loaded for serving. We also introduce compiler passes that automatically derive these chunks from the served LLM as-is, without porting each model architecture to a separate training engine. Our evaluation on three MoE models, served on four A100 GPUs, shows that fine-tuning throughput with BubbleTea is 1.9--6.2$\times$ faster than with LLMStation, a state-of-the-art colocation system. BubbleTea keeps p90 inference latency within 10% of serving alone in 50 of 63 workloads, compared to 44 for LLMStation. On bursty production traces, BubbleTea trains 3.3$\times$ faster than LLMStation and 14$\times$ faster than a stock trainer colocated under CUDA MPS, with p90 inference latency staying within 7% of serving alone.

### Beyond Corpus-Driven Imitation: Evolving Musical Mindsets in the Agentic Orchestra via Quantum Reinforcement Learning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24290) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24290) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24290) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24290)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

This paper presents a framework for computer-aided music composition and performance based on quantum reinforcement learning and multi-agent interaction. We introduce the agentic orchestra, where musical agents evolve distinct mindsets through trial-and-error engagement with a musical environment, rather than through training on large datasets. The agents are implemented as parameterised quantum circuits. They evolve distinct compositional behaviours, referred to as mindsets. This enables the exploration of musical decision spaces using quantum superposition and entanglement within a hybrid quantum-classical loop. We present two complementary agent architectures: atomistic agents, which process pitch and rhythm independently, and holistic agents, which handle pitch and duration as unified note events. Mindsets are shaped through musically grounded reward functions, which can be combined and weighted to develop different mindsets across agents. The resulting system positions quantum computing agents less as autonomous composers and more as adaptive musical partners, each developing its own musical personality through interaction rather than imitation. Demonstrations show how agents generalise their learned behaviour to previously unseen material, operating in interactive and offline batch modes using MIDI-encoded music. While quantum computers are still in development, experiments conducted on IBM quantum hardware confirmed that musically meaningful learning is achievable with existing quantum computers. For composers and performers, the agentic orchestra opens new possibilities for emergent, process-oriented musical creativity, offering a scalable foundation for quantum computer-aided music composition as quantum computing technology continues to advance.

### General progress substitutes for specific training data in language models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24844v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24844v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24844v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24844v1)

2026/11/18 18:07 GTM

Language models acquire their capabilities from training data, and developers withhold data during training to keep models from learning capabilities that could be misused. How long this delays a capability, and how much data is needed to acquire it later, is unknown. Here we show that the general progress a model makes on the rest of its training data substitutes for the data that was withheld. We withheld the training signal for individual skills in models of chess, Othello, molecular strings, bracket languages and web text. The delay of a withheld skill in larger models was predicted from smaller ones. A chess model never explicitly trained to respond to check ranked the legal responses almost as well as a model that was. When models were then trained on the withheld data, they acquired the skill with a small fraction of the data needed from initialization, and this fraction decreased as the models progressed. A control rule of equal difficulty was learned much more slowly. A programming language withheld from the training text of a 7-billion-parameter model was regained in the same way. Data filtering therefore delays a capability, and the data required to regain it decreases as models improve.
