### Embodied Graph: A Relational World Model for Long-Horizon Tasks
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24115) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24115) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24115) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24115)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Long-horizon tasks in embodied and web environments involve extended decision sequences and delayed feedback, making accurate planning crucial for success. World models tackle this by learning latent dynamics for imagination-based planning. However, existing approaches merely rely on dense pixel-level observations, which forces the model to absorb redundant information while overlooking relations among task-relevant entities. Consequently, small errors may accumulate over long imagined rollouts and gradually corrupt the predictions. In this paper, we explore world models that imagine over sparse state abstractions, which poses two key challenges: 1) how to remove redundant information and form a compact state abstraction; 2) how to incorporate the relational structure to learn reliable long-horizon dynamics. To address these challenges, we propose Embodied Graph, a relational state abstraction that supports structured dynamics modeling and long-horizon imagination, and the Relational World Model (RWM), which performs imagination directly over graph states. RWM consists of (i) a Task-Aware Embodied Graph Constructor that filters redundant details and maintains graph memory through online updates, and (ii) an Embodied Graph Recurrent State Space Model that learns node-wise latent dynamics while capturing inter-node dependencies. The imagined graph states provide predictions of action consequences to guide decision-making. Experiments on Minecraft and Crafter demonstrate the effectiveness of RWM in embodied environments. Notably, in Minecraft, RWM is the only method that obtains diamonds within the given budget. Experiments on web interaction further illustrate the applicability of relational state prediction to the decision making process of web agents.

### Towards Pose-Robust Few-Shot Vision-Language-Action Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24360) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24360) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24360) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24360)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Fine-tuning a vision-language-action (VLA) model on a few dozen demonstrations is the standard way to put a manipulation policy on a robot, yet the resulting policy silently assumes that the camera and the robot base stay exactly where they were during data collection: a bumped tripod or a base re-docked a few centimetres away makes it reach for the wrong place. We show that both failures are one phenomenon, a change of the camera-to-base extrinsic that the policy has memorised, and that the robot carries everything needed to undo it. We introduce WACO, a world-anchored canonicalization framework for VLA models: the arm, placed by forward kinematics at the measured joint angles, serves as a metric calibration target, and the static scene serves as the world anchor that tells a moved camera from a moved robot. From a single RGB-D frame, WACO estimates both shifts without ground-truth extrinsics or extra data, canonicalises the image to the calibration viewpoint and the state, action and start pose to the calibration base frame, and fine-tunes the unmodified policy under the artefacts of its own estimator. We also introduce LIBERO-PoseShift, the first benchmark for RGB VLAs with graded camera, base and compound shifts. Across LIBERO-PoseShift, the LIBERO-Plus camera track and four real-world tasks on a UR5e robot, WACO consistently outperforms the standard few-shot recipe and prior viewpoint-robust methods, raising shifted success by 13.2 points in simulation and by 36.7 points on the real robot, recovering 90% of the simulation gap to a ground-truth oracle and 87% of the real-robot gap, and establishing a new state of the art among methods that use no perturbed training data, with no test-time calibration. Project page: https://peacelwh.github.io/WACO/.

### Evidence-Based Credit Assignment for Search-Augmented Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23824) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23824) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23824) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23824)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Reinforcement learning from terminal answer rewards enables search-augmented language models to interleave reasoning and retrieval without step-level annotations, yet outcome supervision assigns identical credit to every search in a trajectory regardless of whether it retrieved useful information. This limitation is especially severe when all sampled rollouts for a question fail, collapsing group-centered outcome advantages to zero even when trajectories gather markedly different evidence. We present Evidence-based Credit Assignment (ECA), which augments terminal outcome rewards with a training-only auxiliary signal measuring how retrieved documents improve the likelihood of a reference answer. Rather than scoring the full dialogue prefix, ECA queries a frozen snapshot of the current policy under an evidence-only prompt containing solely the question and the deduplicated documents observed so far, isolating the utility of retrieved passages from surface variations in generated thoughts or search queries. Within this framework, we compare two return formulations, ECA-Step (which accumulates step-clipped potential increments) and ECA-Horizon (which preserves the telescoping horizon gain in potential), paired with cross-fold group normalization, novelty gating, and search-local token assignment. Across seven Search QA benchmarks, ECA-Horizon improves strict macro exact match over an outcome-only GRPO baseline by 1.03 and 4.80 percentage points on Qwen2.5-3B- and 7B-Instruct, respectively, outperforming both RLOO and GiGPO. Zero-shot evaluation on MultiHop-RAG further demonstrates strong transfer to multi-hop inference queries.

### SpecLook: Speculating Where to Look for Fast and Reliable Agentic MLLMs
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23483) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23483) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23483) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23483)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Agentic multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that think with images interleave reasoning with zoom-in tool calls. However, this sequential loop is slow, and its answers carry no reliability score. Prior speculation of tokens, reasoning steps, or answers keeps the loop, and answer speculation also accepts confident errors. We find that the loop mostly adds one zoom-in step, with which a small model is ahead of the agent on most splits. Our key insight is to speculate the agent's next observation, which, unlike a drafted answer, can be verified, corrected, and used to test the answer. We propose SpecLook, a training-free speculate-and-verify framework with three levels. A small model drafts a glimpse and re-answers from it, and the question ends if the answer is unchanged and confident. Otherwise, the agent's large model verifies and corrects the glimpse without tools, and their answers are fused. Only questions that still lack evidence reach the agent, in one forward pass that SpecLook-Fast skips, so no question runs the loop. The trace scores every answer's reliability without labels. Extensive experiments on V*, HR-Bench, and POPE show that both are more accurate than every baseline on every split. SpecLook reaches 87.2% and 86.2% mean accuracy with 7.7x and 4.7x speedups over the DeepEyes-7B and Thyme-RL agents, and SpecLook-Fast reaches 9.9x and 6.3x.

Prompt injection is the dominant unsolved security risk for LLM agents, and the community now agrees that robustness must be measured against adaptive attackers rather than fixed benchmark injections. Many adaptive red teamers exist, and most are built to maximize attack success rate (ASR). Yet ASR counts how often a target breaks, not in how many different ways. A red teamer can achieve a high ASR by reusing a few techniques that work, leaving the others untested and absent from the attacks used to harden models. We present AutoPI, an adaptive red teamer designed to find many different ways to break a target, not just to break it often. AutoPI uses PIAtlas, a taxonomy of prompt-injection techniques, both to guide its search and to measure coverage. For each scenario, an attacker LLM writes an injection for every sub-technique in the taxonomy and then reworks the ones that fail using feedback from an LLM judge. The method is black-box and training-free, it plugs into existing benchmarks in place of their fixed attack templates, and it stays up to date as the taxonomy evolves. We evaluate AutoPI on AgentDojo and IPI Arena against open-weight and proprietary target models. Alongside ASR, we report technique coverage, the fraction of taxonomy classes, techniques, and sub-techniques that yield at least one successful injection. On AgentDojo, AutoPI finds a successful injection against Qwen3.6-35B in all six classes of the taxonomy and for more than 90% of its techniques. Against Claude Haiku 5.5, the most robust target we evaluate, it achieves between 20% and 50% higher ASR and between 25% and 400% higher class-level coverage than recent state-of-the-art adaptive red teamers, while requiring no training. We release our code and the generated attacks to support reproducible, coverage-aware evaluation.

Coding agents are usually evaluated by running task-specific tests, but tests may be missing or incomplete, and running them requires a working environment for each repository. An agent explores the repository before the change and writes weighted binary criteria, and a judge without tools scores a diff by the weighted share of criteria it satisfies. Video world models are used as simulators, so when a first frame leaves several physical outcomes open, repeated generations should produce each outcome as often as it occurs in the data. Physics post-training scores every generation against the single recorded future of its history, which makes single videos more faithful, but the expected score is linear in the model distribution and is maximized by always producing the most likely outcome. The log-likelihood of the recorded outcome is maximized by the data distribution, and its gradient weights agreement with the record by the inverse of how often the model produces the generated outcome, a frequency that other generations of the same history with independent noise, its siblings, can estimate. To train with this weight from a finite group, we propose SibPO, which performs Sibling-paired Policy Optimization: siblings estimate a truncated inverse-frequency weight for agreement with the record, and a second term subtracts the same weight times the agreement with the siblings, which makes the expected objective a strictly proper divergence over outcome cells at every group size. On PisaBench, SibPO lowers the KS distance to simulated landing times by 9.1-10.2% relative to the strongest method outside its reward family on three of four video models without degrading single videos, and it lowers the error of estimated robot policy success rates by 13%.

Automated red teaming is essential for identifying safety failures in large language models (LLMs), but its practical scalability depends on the resources required to discover those failures. Moreover, a target model's response indicates the outcome of a test without identifying which collaboration decision should change. This design connects feedback to explicit changes in subsequent execution while allowing a useful organization to continue without repeated controller intervention. The evaluation protocol covers HarmBench with three execution backbones and two target models, together with component ablations, dataset transfer, and evaluator checks. Across the six executor-target configurations, L2C improves ASR over EvoSynth by 1.4 to 4.5 percentage points while reducing mean online tokens by 35.6% to 37.3%. LLM-based agents accumulate extensive interaction histories when solving complex, long-horizon tasks, making efficient memory management critical to their performance and scalability. While existing memory management methods primarily focus on what to retain or retrieve, they often overlook how much perceptual detail should be allocated to individual past interactions. We posit that effective agent memory depends not only on the information preserved, but also on the granularity at which each past interaction is perceived. Motivated by this insight, we propose Resolution as Attention (RAA), a memory perception mechanism that uses visual resolution as a means of attention allocation across interaction histories. RAA renders past interactions as images and dynamically adjusts their resolution at every step according to their relevance to the agent's upcoming decision, preserving fine-grained information for critical interactions while reducing visual-token consumption for less relevant ones. To adaptively determine the perceptual fidelity of each interaction, we train a lightweight relevance estimator with teacher-model guidance and further optimize it to preserve the agent's decision consistency at a reduced visual-token cost. Experiments across multiple tasks and backbones show that RAA reduces token consumption by over 50% without compromising task performance. Moreover, RAA can be integrated as a plug-in perception layer with existing memory management methods, yielding further token savings and revealing adaptive perception as a complementary dimension of agent memory.

Large language model (LLM) agents routinely interact with untrusted external data, exposing them to indirect prompt injection (IPI) attacks that hijack agents into executing attacker-chosen tasks. To uncover these vulnerabilities at scale, automated red-teaming methods often leverage reinforcement learning (RL) to generate attacks that achieve high attack success rates (ASR). Model developers use the attacks discovered during internal red-teaming to harden agents through adversarial training before deployment, a collaborative offensive-defensive process known in cybersecurity as purple-teaming. However, we show that achieving high ASR during red-teaming does not guarantee robustness gains from subsequent adversarial training. Specifically, we find that two state-of-the-art RL-based red-teaming methods, RL-Hammer and PISmith, suffer from mode collapse, repeatedly discovering similar attack strategies whose additional successes add no additional robustness improvements to adversarial training. To address this gap, we introduce PIPETTE, a unified prompt injection purple-teaming framework that effectively converts red-teaming discoveries into defender robustness improvements. PIPETTE employs a dictionary learning-based reward that encourages broader coverage of attack strategies during red-teaming. It also integrates an on-policy adversarial training pipeline tailored to IPI defense, enabling agents to resist attacks while completing legitimate user tasks. Extensive evaluations across agentic benchmarks (AgentDojo and ASB) demonstrate that PIPETTE produces defenders that achieve near-zero attack success rates on static attacks and can withstand strong adaptive attacks, while maintaining benign task utility close to that of the undefended baseline.

Pretrained Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) provide strong representations for downstream classification tasks. Yet adapting a TSFM under changing operating conditions remains challenging. A model must improve on each newly observed condition without sacrificing conditions it has already learned, often from only a small labeled calibration set and without knowing the operating condition at inference time. We find that this difficulty has an important geometric structure. Under an operating-condition shift, the embedding space of a TSFM does not move uniformly: some local regions change substantially, while others remain nearly unchanged. Consequently, a global adaptation can unnecessarily move embeddings that were already correctly classified. To address this, we propose the novel PACT (Piecewise Adaptation via Chart Transport). PACT partitions the frozen source embedding space into local charts and learns lightweight residual transports for the regions affected by each new condition. An explicit identity path is always retained, and a learned gate softly selects among the identity and accumulated transports for each input. The pretrained TSFM encoder, classifier head, and previously learned transports remain fixed. With only 5% labeled calibration data per arriving condition and no condition identity at test time, PACT maintains an average macro-F1 above 0.95 across a four-condition bearing-fault stream. Under homogeneous tasks and label spaces, similar retention-adaptation behavior is observed across public benchmarks. Code is available at https://github.com/Sachithx/PACT.

Real-world harness engineering is driven by explicit and implicit user requirements, which guide development and validation. However, existing harness evolution methods often overlook implicit requirements and interaction needs, while using a single evolution agent to modify the harness and benchmark scores to select changes. We introduce Agentic Harness Engineering Beta (AHE-Beta), a requirement-centered loop that brings real-world engineering practices into harness evolution. The loop consists of three steps: (1) User-driven evaluation lets simulated users raise their requirements as issues while they work with the harness over multiple turns. (2) Team-based modification implements those requirements as generalizable changes rather than task-specific patches. (3) Requirement-driven selection merges a change only when it resolves a verified requirement and passes review. Empirically, on held-out tasks, AHE-Beta raises pass@1 from 21.9% to 33.3% while using 40.8% fewer tokens than Base Harness, and achieves higher pass@1 than Claude Code, Meta-Harness, and AHE. In a blind human A/B test, real users prefer AHE-Beta in 31 of 48 valid pairs and rate it higher on all five dimensions, matching the directional preference of the user simulator. The human A/B results show that AHE-Beta achieves its goal of aligning harness evolution with real-world user requirements. Without further evolution, the gains transfer to GLM-5.3 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash with pass@1 improvements of 15.8% and 5.3% over Base Harness. Together, these results show that requirement-centered harness evolution is a more general method for improving agent capabilities.

Neural networks trained on the same task develop related weight-space structures, yet remain distinguishable after learning. Do these differences merely reflect alternative solutions, or do they preserve information about individual learning histories? Training reorganized the ensemble: initial pairwise distances did not consistently predict final distances. Nevertheless, all 30 trained networks were correctly matched to their initial weights among ten candidates per dataset. Networks sharing an initialization also developed recognizable final hidden representations across different batch schedules, although direct comparisons with initial activity did not recover identity. Learning order remained readable as well. A linear probe decoded the order of two earlier tasks with 100% accuracy on 80 held-out networks after ten epochs of a common final task. Earlier intermediate weight states were partly predictable, but individual initial weights could not be reliably reconstructed. Final weights thus retain selective historical information rather than a complete trajectory record. Perturbed networks recovered classification performance without returning to their unperturbed training paths. This separation persisted across datasets, intervention times, and network layers. Moreover, 2,157 of 2,160 aligned endpoint pairs showed no positive loss barrier at sampled interpolation points, indicating low-loss connections between distinct solutions rather than convergence to one weight vector. Together, these findings show that a shared task constrains networks to related successful solutions without erasing their individual histories. Final weights reflect not only what a network has learned, but also where learning began and how it unfolded.

Autonomous agents that run ML experiments are usually judged by the best score they reach. We propose evaluating such systems on their experimental process as well as their outcome, and we build a governed harness that makes this possible. Multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization (MFBO) reduces engineering design cost by combining inexpensive low-fidelity (LF) models with a few accurate high-fidelity (HF) evaluations and sequentially selecting designs to evaluate. However, when HF data are scarce, the LF-HF discrepancy is difficult to learn from numerical data alone, especially when physics omitted in the LF model makes it design-dependent. Yet even without sufficient data, domain experts often know which physics the LF model omits and under which conditions its effect grows. Existing multi-fidelity (MF) models cannot use such qualitative knowledge directly, but large language models (LLMs) can interpret it and compare designs accordingly. The proposed LLM-Assisted MFBO adds a knowledge basis to the conventional MF model: from an expert description of the omitted physics, an LLM judges which of two designs requires the larger signed residual correction, and a pairwise Gaussian process converts these judgments into the basis. HF data calibrate its contribution and sign, so the knowledge is not trusted blindly. On a lithium-ion battery design problem, the proposed method reduced the regret area under the curve of conventional MFBO by 61%. Knowledge quality, rather than the choice of LLM, determined whether the search was accelerated, as shown by a controlled study that varied the two independently. A knowledge-level ablation further quantified the contribution of each knowledge statement. These results show that LLM-interpreted expert knowledge can complement numerical data as an information source in MFBO.

Public benchmarks support comparisons of large language model (LLM) capabilities, yet users can experience the same highly ranked model differently. Drawing on traditional software reliability, we argue that the reliability of an LLM service should be assessed for specified users and conditions of use. This requires more than a capability score: evaluation evidence must support a claim about failure during future operation. We distinguish testing to discover faults from testing to assess reliability and identify ten modelling considerations behind such claims. Local probabilistic robustness addresses the behavioural variation hidden when different concrete inputs are treated as the same task. Published findings and an illustrative customer-support example motivate the roadmap; its integrated validation remains an open research task. Reliability claims complement, but do not replace, separate safety and security arguments. Capability scores should therefore be complemented by versioned operational claims that state whose requirements are covered, under which conditions, and with what uncertainty.

Neural networks with randomly initialized, fixed weights and trainable biases are universal approximators, yet the dynamics of bias-only training remain largely unexplored. We study these dynamics in one-hidden-layer ReLU networks through activation codes, the binary patterns of active neurons that partition the input space into convex polyhedral regions. We show that the gradients with respect to the biases depend on each input only through its activation code, and hence the training dynamics can be characterized with these codes. In particular, as long as the activation codes remain fixed, training reduces to a linear least-squares problem with the codes as features. Training can then be understood as a sequence of least-squares problems, with transitions occurring when neurons change their activation states. We show that the fraction of switching neurons vanishes as the network widens, and the training dynamics approach those of linear least squares on the initial activation codes in the infinite-width limit. Our experiments support these theoretical findings, showing that while activation switches can noticeably affect training in narrower networks, their relative frequency and the associated changes in the dynamics diminish as the width increases.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24762v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24762v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24762v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24762v1)

2026/11/18 17:15 GTM

Recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on general video understanding benchmarks, yet their ability to reason about participant roles in aggressive interactions remains largely unexplored. Existing violence and crime datasets primarily evaluate whether aggression occurs, without testing whether models can identify who performed the action, who received it, and how participants are related within the scene. To address this gap, we introduce A$^3$Bench, a benchmark to study aggressive action and association in videos. The benchmark evaluates whether models can jointly recognize aggressive actions, distinguish aggressors from victims and bystanders, and correctly bind actions to participants under challenging distractor settings. We benchmark 17 multimodal models and find that they struggle substantially on fine-grained aggressive-scene understanding. Performance degrades consistently as reasoning complexity increases, with models frequently confusing aggressors with victims or bystanders, revealing a systematic failure in role-action association rather than coarse aggression recognition alone. We further observe that increasing model scale or generic reasoning models experience limited improvement. Finally, we explore a simple role-graph prompting strategy that encourages structured reasoning over participant interactions, leading to improvements in compositional reasoning. Our findings highlight that reliable aggressive-scene understanding remains a major challenge for current MLLMs and motivate future research on role-aware video reasoning beyond coarse violence detection. Code and dataset: https://github.com/austincbaker/A3Bench.

In active object detection, selecting the initial labeled pool is essential, especially in complex datasets with expensive labeling and low resource budget. This study presents an active learning framework designed to optimize the initial training cycle of active learning for the object detection task. Our approach diverges from traditional methods by utilizing feature-based clustering from the YOLO-World model and refining images based on objectness scores rather than relying on uncertainty sampling in the initial cycle. This approach focuses on finding samples with various features and emphasizes training in areas where the foundation model is most confident. Doing so helps the model better understand different types of objects. For subsequent cycles, we employ a consistency-based sampling technique to maintain the robustness of the training process. To evaluate our method, we use two complex datasets called Aquarium and NWPU VHR-10, which are well known for their diverse object sizes and challenging conditions. In the NWPU dataset, our method increases mAP50 by an average of 6.6% in image-based evaluation. Moreover, the image refinement method shows an improvement of approximately 11% in object-based evaluation. The results show improvements in model performance and labeling time, demonstrating the advantages of using foundation models in early cycles of active learning. An implementation of the algorithm is available at https://github.com/Mhrshd-fllh/ALBoost-FM

### AeroRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement for Aerial Vision-Language Navigation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24077) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24077) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24077) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24077)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Vision-language-action (VLA) models enable unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) to follow natural-language instructions, yet still fail frequently in closed-loop flight. Correcting these failures requires repairs, i.e., actions that avoid them at the states the policy itself reaches, which human pilots cannot label at scale and rule-based planners cannot supply reliably. In this paper, we present AeroRSI, a recursive self-improvement framework in which an aerial navigation policy iteratively learns from its own failures. Our key insight is that a failing policy should judge its own repairs by flying on from them. Judged in this way, its sampled actions and even rule-based proposals that hurt it when imitated directly become reliable supervision. Moreover, an episode terminates at its first failure, concealing subsequent failures until the earlier ones are repaired. AeroRSI thus renews its supervision in every round, anchoring the failures of the current policy, verifying repairs by its continuation, and distilling them while keeping its behavior elsewhere. On TravelUAV, three rounds raise the success rate of a supervised 2B-parameter policy from 46.4% to 67.5% and reduce collisions by half, surpassing the previous state of the art by 10.6 points from the same initialization without extra human labels or inference cost. Code and models are available at https://github.com/starsholic/AeroRSI.

### Temporal Rank in Recurrent Neural Networks
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24394) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24394) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24394) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24394)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) are a natural framework for temporal modelling, yet are often dismissed due to their sequential, non-parallelisable computation and difficulty in capturing long-range dependencies, typically attributed to vanishing and exploding gradients. We identify a distinct and under-explored limitation: an information bottleneck induced by rank-constrained hidden state dynamics, which limits the number of independent input directions that can be preserved over time, as reflected in the rank of the input-to-state mapping. We show that this constraint restricts temporal capacity independently of gradient stability. To address this, we introduce structured high-dimensional state dynamics that preserve temporal separability and prevent saturation of the input-to-state Jacobian over time. On controlled sequence tasks, the resulting model exhibits rapid learning despite remaining strictly within a standard RNN formulation. Our results demonstrate that RNN performance is governed by the evolution and preservation of temporal rank, and suggest that mitigating gradient pathologies alone is insufficient without explicitly maintaining temporal capacity.

### When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? A Controlled Study of Dialectal Adaptation for Québec French
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24699v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24699v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24699v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24699v1)

2026/11/18 16:26 GTM

Continual pre-training (CPT) is widely used to adapt pretrained language models to dialectal and non-standard language varieties, yet it remains unclear when this additional training is necessary, what drives its benefits, and whether aggregate evaluation scores adequately capture its effects. In this paper, we present a controlled study of CPT for Québec French language understanding across nine LLMs spanning different families and scales. Using a budget-matched protocol of 30M tokens per experiment, our results show that the benefit of CPT depends strongly on the starting model's existing competence in the target variety. When CPT is beneficial, the improvements are driven more by corpus composition than by data volume, with literary text accounting for most of the gain. Paired Québec and standard French evaluations further show that CPT primarily improves expression comprehension rather than grammatical competence, and that corpus register determines which regional expressions are acquired. These gains also involve capability-specific trade-offs, including declines in reading comprehension that aggregate scores can conceal. Overall, these findings show that CPT for dialectal adaptation should not be treated as a uniformly beneficial default. Instead, adaptation strategies should begin with a careful assessment of the base model's existing competence, accompanied by source-level data analysis and task-level evaluation.

### StatePortal: Agentic Checkpoint Synthesis for Testing Deep States in Interactive Software
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23257) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23257) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23257) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23257)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Large language models have reduced the effort to generate code for interactive software with graphical user interfaces (GUIs). For such software, a key testing bottleneck is reaching a deep state, one that requires a long interaction history. The cost of reaching it recurs across commits, and the GUI automation that could amortize it assumes a DOM or accessibility tree that self-rendered engines do not expose. We introduce StatePortal, an agent that synthesizes a checkpoint, an artifact that restores that state and can be versioned and replayed, from a natural-language functional test case and read-only access to the target's source code. The checkpoint is restored through a per-application hook that an onboarding agent binds or synthesizes once without hand-written hook code. Because a checkpoint can satisfy state checks while rendering incorrectly, checkpoints are scored by hybrid programmatic-state and visual evaluation: acceptance requires both a programmatic-state oracle and a visual oracle to pass. We calibrate the visual oracle against a blinded cross-VLM reference. We release DeepStateBench, 61 deep-state tasks across 10 interactive applications that span five language families and six rendering stacks, with source sizes from 874 to 7.2M lines. Across three registered models, a GUI agent seeded with synthesized checkpoints completes 10.4% of trials, compared with 0% without seeding under the same pixel action space, while StatePortal, which also delivers the target action through the hook, completes 27.5%. Over 1,361 replays of 112 checkpoints, 98.90% reproduce the original verdict with zero model calls, and the hybrid oracle detects 96.25% (77/80) of controlled visual faults invisible to programmatic-state checks alone. We open-source our code at https://github.com/pzy2000/State_Portal and website at https://stateportal.mineyosemite.chatgpt.site

### Vanguard: Knowing When to Trust the Draft in Speculative Vision-Language Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23917) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23917) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23917) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23917)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Vision-language agents that browse websites, operate phones and act in homes reason before every action, so every step waits for a large model, a latency and cost that stand in the way of deployment. Step-level speculation lets a small model draft each step and calls the large model only on rejected drafts, but its outcome rests on the judge, and a judge that reads only a step's text cannot tell whether the step fits the screen it acts on. We find that a few samples of a small model can contain a correct step about as often as the large model gets the step right, and not always on the same steps: checked against the screen, small drafts can be not only cheaper but better. We present Vanguard, a training-free judge that leaves the draft and target models unchanged: a general decision model, with the screenshot in view, gives each candidate step a probability of being right in one forward pass; the best candidate is kept if it clears a threshold, and otherwise the large model writes the step. On web, mobile and embodied agents, a 2B draft judged by Vanguard is at least as accurate as its 8B target and significantly more accurate on Mind2Web and EB-ALFRED, while the target writes only a minority of the steps; it completes far more closed-loop episodes, and with a 27B target from another family it is both faster and more accurate. Vanguard is the most accurate judge we compare on the agent tasks; it relies on the screenshot on web and mobile tasks, its calibrated threshold transfers to unseen websites, and across model families its gain over the target grows with the draft's pass@4 relative to the target's accuracy. Vanguard composes with token-level speculative decoding and matches trained process reward models on mathematics, turning speculation from a way to save the large model's work into one that can improve on it.

Can AI agents design robots that work in the real world? We introduce RoboBuilder, a benchmark in which coding agents co-design a robot's hardware and program from standard parts, motors and a custom 3D-printed part, and every design that passes in simulation is built and tested on a physical course. Simulation alone is not enough: under public part data, none of 15 initial passing designs crossed the real course, so an agent learns the simulator from measurements it requests and videos of real runs. Across four frontier coding agents, ten obstacles at three difficulty levels, 16,427 simulations of 12,774 distinct robots and 135 physical robots built in an estimated 240 hours, agents solve nearly all easy obstacles and invent mechanisms our search baselines do not reach, but falter at harder levels, when one robot must cross every obstacle, and in the real world, where 66% of simulated passes hold across all levels. Closing the loop helps: shown a video of its real failure, an agent redesigns the robot, sometimes correcting its simulator, and removes 72% of level-1 and level-2 failures. RoboBuilder measures progress toward agents that engineer real machines. Project Page: https://ghadinehme.com/robobuilder.github.io/

### Does the Action Matter? Probing Action-Conditioned Semantic Forecasting as a World Model for Autonomous Driving
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24840v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24840v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24840v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24840v1)

2026/11/18 18:06 GTM

World models are increasingly used in autonomous driving to predict how a scene evolves under the ego vehicle's actions, supporting counterfactual reasoning and model-based planning. However, existing evaluations focus on prediction fidelity and planning performance, and rarely verify that the predictions depend on the conditioning action. In this paper, we study action dependence in action-conditioned semantic forecasting, which predicts future semantic segmentation from past segmentations and the ego action. We compare a forecaster with feature-wise and input-level action conditioning against an identical model without action input, and measure action dependence with test-time action interventions scored by standard segmentation metrics. The evaluation is validated in a synthetic environment with known action-dependent dynamics and applied to Cityscapes and BDD100K, both within each dataset and across the two vehicle platforms. Experiments show that the executed action improves mid-term mIoU by at most 2.5 points on Cityscapes and 0.6 points on BDD100K, whereas a wrong action reduces it by up to 23.2 and 7.0 points, below the model without action input. On real data, feature-wise conditioning depends 1.6 times more on the action than input-level conditioning at similar accuracy, whereas both depend on it equally in the synthetic environment, where the history does not determine the action. These results show that an accuracy gain indicates that a driving world model uses its action, but not how strongly its forecasts depend on it. Code is available at https://github.com/binayksingh/does-action-matter.

Code review plays a critical role in identifying software quality issues and ensuring system robustness. Prior work has proposed techniques for Review Comment Generation (RCG) and Code Refinement (CR), with recent Multi-Agent (MA) approaches achieving state-of-the-art results by coordinating agents specialized in different quality dimensions. However, MA systems introduce substantial architectural complexity and runtime overhead, and recent evidence suggests they may underperform Single-Agent (SA) approaches in certain tasks. In this work, we systematically compare MA and SA designs for RCG and CR. Surprisingly, our results show that SA approaches perform comparably to- and sometimes outperform-MA systems, despite their simpler design. These findings challenge the prevailing assumption that MA architectures are the default choice for complex software engineering tasks.

Current command and control (C2) platforms lack integrated simulation capabilities that would allow commanders to dynamically explore courses of action (COAs) during live operations. This paper presents ongoing research and contributions to the development of AGAMOTTO (Advanced Guidance and Analytics for Military Operations, Tactical Threats, and Outcomes) as part of the overarching NGC2 program, which advances the Army's efforts to modernize C2 systems, such as the Tactical Assault Kit (TAK). The paper outlines the current design, preliminary findings, and the remaining research tasks, with an emphasis on how the completed work will support tactical decision-making in future time-constrained environments. While the full system is still under development, initial development and results demonstrate the feasibility of AGAMOTTO. Early experiments highlight both the potential benefits and practical constraints of deploying AI-enabled tools in operational settings, and introduce lessons learned and a viable training strategy to make AI-generated COAs feasible in tactical scenarios.

### 3D GameDev Arena: Benchmarking Coding Agents That Plan, Build, and Playtest extreme Long-Horizon Browser Games
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24073) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24073) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24073) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24073)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Coding agents now resolve repository issues and generate compact playable games, but whether they can build a large 3D game from scratch remains open. Such games tightly couple mechanics, scenes, assets, and interaction logic, and their quality rests on player enjoyment, which existing benchmarks rarely assess. Thus, we introduce 3D GameDev Arena, a first extreme long-horizon benchmark of 108 from-scratch game-development tasks across 14 genres. Given only a text specification and UI/UX reference images, a fully automated plan--implement--test--repair pipeline builds each task into a self-contained browser game. Runs average $\sim$1,000 LLM calls ; the longest uses 14,407 calls, 2.13B tokens, and 608 assets. Evaluation spans (1) objective code-level functional acceptance against the game specification, (2) agent-based QA and playtesting of 3D presentation and playability, and (3) blind pairwise Bradley-Terry human preference in a public arena. The preliminary findings indicate that long-horizon 3D game development remains far from solved, and code-level correctness alone does not dictate player preference. The public arena is hosted online.

### Beyond Replacement: Levenshtein Editing for Diffusion Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23750) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23750) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23750) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23750)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Diffusion language models (DLMs) offer a flexible alternative to autoregressive models, enabling any-order infilling. While most existing decoding methods treat decoded tokens as fixed, some recent models allow previously decoded tokens to be revised only by replacing them with alternative tokens, a paradigm we refer to as replacement-only editing (ROE). Although ROE can correct individual tokens, it is inefficient for sequence shifts caused by missing or extra tokens and struggles when errors occur across many positions. To address these limitations, we propose LED (Levenshtein Editing for Diffusion Language Models), a post-training framework that extends ROE with insertion and deletion, enabling Levenshtein-style refinement of generated sequences. It uses two training designs. First, it reconstructs edit supervision on the fly during supervised fine-tuning using longest-common-subsequence alignment, enabling insertion and deletion learning without manually annotated labels. Second, it adopts multi-stage training, where the state from one stage becomes the input to the next, exposing the model to its earlier errors and enabling it to correct them. When applied to the 16B-parameter LLaDA2.1-Mini Base, LED raises the average score across twelve benchmarks from 54.86 to 57.10 under confidence-guided decoding while increasing tokens per forward from 6.14 to 7.05, whereas under confidence-free decoding it raises the average score from 27.52 to 47.19. We further show that LED achieves higher pass@k at large k than thresholded decoding without editing, demonstrating improved generation diversity and revealing the diversity potential of DLMs without confidence guidance. LED also improves robustness to large block sizes, raising the average score from 36.97 to 51.34 at block size 128. Together, these results suggest that LED supports more robust self-correction in DLMs.

### Test-Time Symmetrization: Scaling Laws for Model-Agnostic Learning under Structure
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24032) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24032) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24032) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24032)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Test-time compute has emerged as a powerful way to improve the accuracy of language models at inference time, with scaling laws quantifying how accuracy improves with additional forward passes. Existing scaling laws, however, focus on repeated sampling from a fixed prompt, even though many tasks admit multiple equivalent representations of the same underlying problem. A graph does not depend on how its nodes are numbered, and a program does not depend on the names of its variables, yet language models can change their answers under such rewritings. In this paper, we study test-time symmetrization, which uses forward passes on rewritings of the input and aggregates the resulting answers over the group $G$ of rewritings. We prove that no amount of sampling and repeated decoding from a single representation can replace rewriting, while only $O(\log |G|)$ random rewritings suffice to recover the information provided by all $|G|$ rewritings. The same set of $O(\log |G|)$ rewritings provides simultaneous approximation across models, decoding rules, and sampled reasoning traces, and the logarithmic scaling is optimal in general. We validate the theory with experiments on Qwen2.5-7B, Phi-4-mini, Llama-3.1-8B, and Qwen2.5-14B, with chain-of-thought reasoning, on graph reasoning, logical deduction, and multiple-choice question answering. At the same number of forward passes, spending them on rewritings of the input is substantially more accurate than spending them on repeated sampling from the original prompt whenever the model's answer depends on how the input is written, and the number of rewritings needed grows only logarithmically with the number of possible rewritings, as the theory predicts. Our results provide a principled way to enforce symmetries at inference time when they cannot be built into the model, which we call model-agnostic symmetrization.

### Where the Tokens Go: Heat, Entropy and Error in Agentic Coding
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23375) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23375) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23375) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23375)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Coding agents are billed by the token, yet software engineering says nothing about what a token actually buys. Here we introduce a theory of software thermodynamics. The theory draws analogues from classical and stochastic thermodynamics, extends them, and applies them to modern agentic coding. We count the tokens an agent generates as its cost and divide that cost into work and heat. The test suite acts as a software Maxwell's daemon that judges every coding change. We test the theory with an analogue of the experiments in which a single molecule is pulled open and let fold again: an agent makes a change to a repository (coding) and then undoes it (uncoding), many times over, and we count the tokens of every model call. The cycle returns the requested edits to where they began, with each direction started from a stored reference tree, and the spread of its cost over repeated cycles defines a software temperature, which rose with the sampling temperature of the model. About 80 per cent of the tokens of a successful run are generated in model calls that leave the requested change where it was, and we call these tokens heat. The free energy of a change, which we estimate by analogy with the equality of Jarzynski, is 14 to 22 per cent of the mean cost of a successful coding run. Coding costs 1.1 to 1.5 times as much as uncoding. The daemon is imperfect: the verdict of a test suite varies with its environment, and a suite that accepts a broken change has a cost in tokens. The practical implication of the proposed theory could be new tools for analysing and optimising coding agents through the lens of thermodynamics.

### Do Frontier Model Ingredients Add Up? A Replicated Factorial Study of Small Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24540v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24540v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24540v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24540v1)

2026/11/18 14:48 GTM

Building a small language model requires choosing which published improvements to combine. We compare five changes in the same model, using the same data and training budget. We test selected combinations and repeat each one with a second random seed. Mixture-of-experts layers, Muon optimisation and a Gated DeltaNet hybrid give the largest gains. Extra normalisation also helps, while an attention gate with a matched parameter budget adds little on average. The gains mostly combine, but small interactions between changes appear again in the repeated runs. Repetition also reveals benefits that an initial statistical estimate treated as noise. These findings apply to our small-model training setting; the expert models also have greater total capacity. The practical lesson is to test promising components together and repeat the comparisons before deciding which ones to keep.

### The Value Semantics Layer: Enabling Machine-Understandable Databases for Agentic AI
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23629) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23629) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23629) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23629)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

As data systems increasingly support autonomous LLM-powered agents alongside human analysts, they require richer semantic infrastructure bridging analytical concepts and analytical operations. Existing metadata systems expose schemas, catalogs, lineage, and governance information, but provide limited support for representing operational semantics. We propose the Value Semantics Layer (VSL), an architectural abstraction that externalizes value semantics as persistent, planner-visible infrastructure. VSL introduces Value Semantic Types (VSTs), reusable operational semantic assertions capturing distributional, relational, process, quantitative, and policy-grounded semantics. Unlike descriptive metadata, VSTs enable planners to validate analytical operations, select appropriate operators, and guide analytical workflows. We present the motivation, formalization, architecture, and prototype realizations of VSL, and outline a research agenda toward AI-native semantic infrastructure for analytical systems.

### Space-X: Self-Evolving Spatial Agents Weaving Clues into Evidence Chains
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24004) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24004) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24004) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24004)

Spatial reasoning connects visual observations, geometric estimates, and linguistic decisions into a coherent derivation. Yet tool-augmented agents often acquire evidence without clear targets, combine outputs under inconsistent object or reference-frame semantics, and misjudge evidence sufficiency. We introduce MANGO Arena, an open-source testbed in which four LLM agents each run one company in a serial supply chain adapted from MANGO, a classroom factory-management game. The environment enforces hard rules (a truck limit, a no-credit cash constraint and production capacity), logs every blocked attempt to break them, and gives agents a public message board on which side payments are explicitly forbidden. This lets one environment measure three things at once: an economic outcome (the bullwhip effect), rule adherence under pressure, and proposals to collude. In a pilot with three small reasoning models, two scripted reference policies, two conditions (with and without a supplier fire) and three demand seeds, all LLM agents amplified demand variability as it moved upstream, by 7 to 25 times at the Retailer and 13 to 179 times at the Supplier, often more than a textbook order-up-to policy. They did so largely by ordering in batches (zero orders in up to 60% of decisions). Real rule violations were rare (at most 7 of 288 decisions per model) and no agent proposed a money side deal. The pilot's main lesson concerns evaluation method. API failures (rate limits and runaway hidden reasoning) entered the metrics silently as "no order" decisions. And when we reran games with the same seeds and no failed calls, upstream bullwhip changed by up to 17 times between runs. With three seeds, conclusions about how a disruption affects a given model did not survive a rerun. We report these failures in full and propose reporting practices for multi-agent evaluations of this kind. Code, prompts and every logged decision are public.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23615) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23615) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23615) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23615)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

In weight-space programming, a language model edits a network's weights directly, without gradients. We ask what a successful repair of this kind relies on. We plant faults in the weights of two vision-language models, SmolVLM-256M and LFM2-VL-450M, and check each edit made by a coding agent against the original weights. Without being told where to look, the agent finds rescaled rows in the projector and noise in the vision encoder of both models, infers the unknown scale factors, restores most or all of the rescaled weights to their exact original values, and returns accuracy to near its original level; programs that it writes from such weights repair unseen checkpoints of the same model without feedback. These repairs depend on a trace of how the faults were computed. The original weights have 16-bit precision, and faults computed in 32-bit arithmetic leave low bits that only the damaged weights carry. Rounding those bits away leaves each model's answers to our test questions unchanged, and the agent, unaided, then restores no weight exactly: on SmolVLM it no longer finds the damage, and in a single session on LFM2-VL its only gain comes from rescaling answer-token embeddings, which changes how often the model answers yes. Marking the damaged weights again in their low bits brings the edits back to them. Rounded weights still hold statistical evidence of the rescaling, in the distribution of stored values and in row norms, and one sentence in the prompt that names such a statistic directs the agent's edits to the damaged rows on both models. Swapped rows or columns, which leave the set of stored values unchanged, are not found unaided on either model within our session budgets. We recommend that studies of weight repair report the number format of the weights and include a rounded control.

Reconstructing cellular dynamics from snapshots is challenging because the destructive nature of single-cell measurements makes tracking the same cells over time impossible. Cellular dynamics are inherently stochastic and involve changes in population size through proliferation and apoptosis. Growth-based methods represent population change as a rate on the density, which does not assign exits and entries to individual cells. Unbalanced Schrödinger bridges model individual events, but neural solvers for killed diffusion bridges simulate trajectories during training. We introduce Elysium, a simulation-free solver that jointly learns state transition and mass change. Using a killed Brownian reference and its time reversal, Elysium constructs direct regression objectives from an optimal endpoint coupling and conditional bridge distributions, without simulating trajectories during training. We show that the minimizers of the expected training objectives recover the optimal unbalanced Schrödinger bridge. Empirically, Elysium reconstructs state transitions, population size, and event distributions on simulated benchmarks with known killed-diffusion ground truth, outperforming existing deterministic and bridge-based solvers. On single-cell time courses, it interpolates cell state distributions at unobserved time points, tracks population expansion and contraction, and captures biphasic drug-response dynamics.

### Toaster: Nonlinear Memory from Cascaded Linear Memories and Target Propagation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23648) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23648) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23648) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23648)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Recurrent memories let language models process long sequences with a fixed-size state. Linear memories such as DeltaNet and KDA store one matrix and take one delta-rule step per token. The product of a chunk of such steps has an exact WY form, so they train fast. Deep memories such as TTT-MLP and Titans store a small MLP, which can hold maps that no matrix can, but their update has no WY form. Toaster bridges the two by building on a known fact: a gradient step on a linear layer is a delta-rule write. It builds a two-layer nonlinear memory from two linear memories with an activation between them. We use target propagation to give the hidden memory its values by projecting the output error back through the second memory. Given a chunk's cross-layer signals, approximated once per chunk, each layer can then run on an unmodified delta-rule kernel. Our default runs this kernel on the output layer and updates the hidden layer once per chunk. Running it on both layers gives a better model in our ablation. At matched backbones and training-token budgets, Toaster has lower loss than KDA at all five tested sizes (0.2B-1.6B non-embedding parameters) on both C4 and PG19. Its C4 perplexity also matches or slightly improves on the attention baseline at every size. At 1.2B it is also below Titans. In a 3:1 hybrid with global attention, the Toaster hybrid has the lowest perplexity of the four hybrids tested, and it is 0.18 (C4) and 0.44 (PG19) below the attention baseline. At the largest size, Toaster's mean three-shot accuracy on 13 tasks is 51.84%, compared with 50.52% for KDA and 50.04% for Titans. In length extrapolation, Toaster's loss stays below KDA's up to 20K tokens (5x the training length). On the 145M backbone, at a hidden width matched to Titans' memory, Toaster takes at most 1.33x KDA's training-step time and is 2.8-5.5x faster than the tested Titans configurations at 32K context.

MoE-Context applies this to language models. The context is split into blocks. In the version we build, one model makes this decision and then answers, over a single key-value cache where hidden blocks are masked rather than removed. We report these results openly, along with two cheap usage patterns that block switching enables: undoing a failed attempt and running subagents without a new context.

### Benchmarking LLM abilities on postdoc-level natural science problems

Scientific problems have no answer key, so evaluating an agent means deciding what counts as a better solution before any agent is run. Existing benchmarks either match a symbolic answer against a key or, where they require executable work, score it by unit tests, by a rubric, or by position on a competition leaderboard. None of these is the criterion by which a field decides that one published method beats another. STEMBench adopts that criterion directly. Each of its twelve research problems in high-energy physics, astrophysics and neuroscience is scored by its own field's figure of merit, and each comes with two measured anchors: a submission that uses no domain knowledge, and an expert reference reproducing a published method, both obtained by running code shipped with the benchmark. The interval between them states how much of a problem is crossed by scientific insight rather than by engineering. Across eight frontier models, task-level wins and domain-level leadership dissociate: a model that wins five of the twelve tasks leads no domain mean. In the high-effort cross-model comparison, at least one model's mean score exceeds the expert anchor on seven of twelve tasks. The remaining five span uncertainty estimation, latent and causal inference, and cross-site clinical classification. Numerical scores and official-submission validity capture different aspects of performance and are reported separately. We release the tasks, graders, anchors and harness.

A deployed multimodal predictor may face missing modalities after its weights can no longer be changed. This raises a question that training-time repair does not answer: what part of the resulting error is still correctable from the information that remains? We show that this part is characterized by the Bayes gap, the difference between the model's prediction and the Bayes prediction given the observed modalities. For cross-entropy and squared error, excess risk measures this gap in the geometry of the loss; ambiguity in the observed modalities cannot be removed. Thus, a badly degraded prediction is not necessarily a correctable one. Estimating the Bayes prediction normally requires labels. However, when a model observing more modalities is conditionally correct, averaging its outputs conditional on the remaining modalities recovers this target without retrieval labels; with the same fixed blend and neighbors, it is no worse in expected cross-entropy or Brier risk than averaging hard labels. Some such assumption is unavoidable: otherwise the target is not identifiable from unlabeled data alone. Under exchangeability, the marginal probability that GUARD applies a correction whose loss increase exceeds a prescribed tolerance is bounded by a preset level, regardless of the learned score. Optical neural networks promise fast, energy-efficient computing, but every device departs from its design and must be trained with the hardware. When a reply must satisfy several people, the best score is only 25.9. These results indicate that recognizing a request and meeting its requirements are separate abilities, and O-IN-Bench measures each of them.

Generative recommendation (GR) inference differs substantially from conventional LLM serving, featuring long input sequences, wide beam search, and only a few fixed output tokens. ### Vibe-Trading: Towards Agent-Native Trading
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24845v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24845v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24845v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24845v1)

2026/11/18 18:07 GTM

Existing AI agents can retrieve information, call tools, run experiments, and produce multi-step outputs. Yet most are built around relatively static workflows and struggle to preserve reliable state as the environment changes. Financial trading makes this limitation especially consequential: prices, news, market conditions, portfolio positions, and available capital evolve continuously. A result is useful only when it remains tied to the correct time, data snapshot, and execution context. We present Vibe-Trading, a comprehensive agent-native trading framework and all-in-one workspace for building, evaluating, and evolving agents for financial trading. It unifies market research, financial data, strategy generation, backtesting, portfolio analysis, paper trading, multi-agent collaboration, and optional broker execution. Its central contribution is an architecture that keeps results, evidence, research history, and trading authority connected throughout the research-to-action process. Each result retains its source, temporal scope, configuration, and producing run. Shared evidence grounding enables verification, versioned research records support reproducible continuation, and an explicit authority gate separates strategy research from live execution. Together, these designs provide a practical foundation for trading agents whose results can be traced, reproduced, and acted on only under explicit authority.

Coding agents increasingly undertake repository-level development, including dependency selection, source modification, and software deployment. However, existing evaluations prioritize functional success over licensing correctness and commercial suitability. Experiments across multiple agents examine permissive and copyleft licenses, missing licensing information, transitive dependencies, source modification, dual licensing, vendoring, and distribution contexts. Agent traces, repository changes, and final artifacts are evaluated against licensing ground truth. Results reveal a persistent gap between functional and licensing correctness. Agents can successfully build and test software while overlooking licensing constraints involving provenance, authorization, and distribution. Better outcomes emerge when licensing information translates into engineering actions such as dependency substitution, architectural changes, and release gating. We argue that licensing in agentic development requires contextual constraint recognition and preservation across the software lifecycle, not merely license classification.

Deployed world-action models (WAMs) can keep improving only if they make good use of their own experience. Existing methods supply retrieved rollouts or demonstrations to the policy as context, yet such experience often turns previously solved tasks into failures. What, then, makes good experience for a WAM? We show that experience is valuable only in use: its value depends not on whether the source rollout succeeded but on what is stored, when it is used, and where it enters the policy. We therefore propose F$^3$, which makes these three decisions with Form, Fit, and Fusion. Form compresses successful and failed rollouts alike into short, verified operation records; Fit teaches the policy to read a record and to predict when it helps; Fusion injects the record's images, text and actions into the policy as a prefix and an action prior. Trained once on only 200 examples and deployed with a growing experience library, the model achieves state-of-the-art results on MolmoSpaces and RoboLab without any training on RoboLab: over native Cosmos 3 Nano, it raises success by 12.0 pp and 10.8 pp, reduces steps to success by 12.2% and 11.3%, and cuts regressions by 57%.

Animals generate behavior through continuous interactions between their neural circuits, bodies, and environment. Embodied simulations study these interactions by testing neural circuit models in biologically realistic settings. Recent efforts have simulated diverse animals, and emerging work on biologically grounded neural architectures has begun to integrate circuit connectivity and single-unit dynamics from experimental data into artificial neural networks, introducing stronger inductive biases and closer mechanistic alignment with biology. How can we develop embodied simulations that enable the study of increasingly complex bodies and neural circuits? In this work, we address the challenge through coordinated advances in the simulated body, neural architecture, and training infrastructure. We introduce Rat v2, an efficient and flexible biomechanical body for simulating rat behavior. To control this body, we develop Rat NCAP, a biologically grounded neural architecture for locomotion based on mammalian spinal circuits that incorporates cell-type-specific connectivity and a novel flexor-extensor neuromechanical interface. To optimize circuit parameters, we develop GPU-accelerated simulation and training infrastructure. The model learns to locomote at different speeds and transition between gaits without imitation learning, producing locomotion statistics, kinematics, and cell-type-specific neural activity consistent with experimental data. Analyses reveal that the neuromechanical interface substantially affects trainability and gait quality, and in silico cell-type ablations reproduce known gait phenotypes and predict gait changes under graded perturbations. Together, these findings support the view that embodied simulations at an intermediate level of abstraction can simultaneously reproduce complex behavior and yield mechanistic insights into the neural circuits that generate it.

Isabelle Pool Server (IPS), a verification service with a pool of warm headless Isabelle sessions behind an HTTP API, with session leasing and reclamation, memory-aware admission control, and a time budget on every call. Food insecurity creates a pressing need for guidance that helps households find usable food resources and assess food choices in light of their circumstances. Such guidance must connect eligibility requirements, practical access constraints, and dietary needs rather than return isolated listings or generic nutritional advice. Multi-agent food assistants can coordinate these tasks, with uncertainty indicating when clarification or verification is needed before a recommendation. However, uncertainty can be lost across agent handoffs, allowing tentative interpretations to propagate as facts and obscuring opportunities for correction. Moreover, confidence that a service requirement has been interpreted correctly is not confidence that a recommendation satisfies the user's needs; local confidence cannot simply be reused as downstream reliability. We address this limitation in a model-driven approach that treats both the data-driven controller and safety shield as first-class models that are integrated into a virtual single underlying model (V-SUM) alongside system models. The results show that consistency-driven evolution effectively maintains safety guarantees under system changes and provides a principled basis for evaluating and restoring performance.

Function-level vulnerability detectors often exhibit fix reversal, assigning a higher vulnerability probability to a fixed version than to its vulnerable counterpart. Fixes usually lengthen functions, and larger length increases are associated with more severe reversal. We propose CFVul to address this problem. Sliding-window encoding covers complete functions, retaining fix information beyond the truncation boundary. During training, a content constraint encourages the vulnerable version to receive a higher vulnerability probability under a common length input. During inference, a length constraint averages vulnerability probabilities over fixed reference lengths, reducing the influence of actual length changes. On PrimeVul, CFVul achieves a fix-pair ranking metric, PairRank, of 59.4\%, exceeding the existing methods compared. For fixes that increase function length by more than 15\%, PairRank rises from CodeBERT's 25.8\% to 48.4\%. PairRank also improves in experiments with four additional models and three additional datasets. By weakening the length shortcut, CFVul reduces cases in which fixed versions receive higher vulnerability probabilities than vulnerable versions. It requires only existing fix pairs and applies to multiple pretrained code models, facilitating adoption in existing detectors.

However, each judgment requires a potentially expensive call to a frontier model, and the judgments are hard to interpret and may not be reproducible. In this paper, we ask whether an LLM can write a readable program for each topic that replaces its per-document judgments. To address these issues, we propose ExecJudges, a method for generating fast, inspectable and scalable judges for automatic relevance labeling. Specifically, ExecJudges uses a frontier LLM to turn assessment guidelines into executable programs, which rely only on lexical matching, standard NLP libraries like parsers, and pool statistics. An ExecJudges-L variant also uses examples of human labels if available. We evaluate ExecJudges on seven TREC collections by comparing the produced labels with trained NIST assessors, frontier LLM judges, and recently proposed per-topic classifiers. Our results show that ExecJudges agrees with NIST assessors at the level of the Claude Sonnet 5.5 LLM judges on TREC DL 2019-2021. With a small number of human labels per topic, the ExecJudges-L variant agrees with NIST more than per-topic classifiers with the same labels on all seven collections, and either exceeds the agreement of LLM judges provided the same labels or is within about 0.05 of it in Krippendorff's alpha. The labels rank the official TREC DL runs with Kendall's tau of 0.87 to 0.94, and writing the programs incurs a one-time cost of $0.17 to $0.49 per topic, with no additional costs for grading. Our results suggest that ExecJudges offers a reproducible, inspectable, efficient and cost-effective alternative to LLM judges for labeling deep result pools for system evaluation or ranking improvements.

### Attention Is Not Relevance: Challenging the Causality of Attention in Autonomous Driving
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24051) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24051) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24051) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24051)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Attention has become one of the most widely adopted solutions for estimating the relevance of surrounding objects and interpreting an autonomous vehicle's driving decisions. However, its ability to capture the causal impact of individual input elements on an AI model's output has long been discussed in the literature, particularly in the Natural Language Processing (NLP) domain. This work brings this discussion into the autonomous driving domain by analyzing the accuracy of attention in estimating the impact of an input object on an autonomous vehicle's driving, i.e., its relevance. To this end, we compare different attention-based relevance estimation methods against a causal reference of object relevance obtained through counterfactual analysis. We perform our analysis on more than 80,000 synthetic driving scenes using an autonomous vehicle based on PlanT 2.0, a state-of-the-art Transformer-based model for object-level planning. Results show that attention provides a correlational rather than causal indication of object relevance. Attention-based relevance estimation methods do not consistently identify all relevant objects and tend to incorrectly classify as relevant objects with little or no impact on the vehicle's driving. In particular, results show that attention can effectively identify relevant objects in simple single-lane, straight-road scenarios, but its accuracy degrades in more complex settings, such as multi-lane roads and intersections. These findings highlight the limitations of attention as a proxy for object relevance, calling for novel methods able to accurately capture the causal impact of individual objects on the vehicle's driving and distinguish it from correlational associations.

A central challenge in adapting large language models (LLMs) is capability preservation: an adaptation can achieve its target while degrading general capabilities. We introduce CurvDelta, a scalable framework that incorporates the preservation constraint into the parameterization of the model update. By analyzing the local geometry of the preservation constraint around the pre-adaptation model, CurvDelta constructs a low-curvature space in which parameter updates leave the preservation loss nearly invariant. We make its construction practical for LLMs through Kronecker-factored approximate curvature (K-FAC) and a factorized projection. We also introduce CurvLoRA, a low-rank variant within the same preservation geometry, designed for more efficient adaptation. Both methods apply to preservation-constrained adaptation tasks such as capability learning and selective unlearning. We evaluate our methods on mathematical reasoning, code generation, and knowledge unlearning. For mathematical reasoning and code generation, our approaches achieve competitive downstream performance while better preserving general capabilities, offering a favorable acquisition--retention trade-off relative to the baselines. For knowledge unlearning, our main results on TOFU and MUSE show a better balance between targeted forgetting and knowledge retention than the baselines, with retention performance close to the from-scratch retraining reference.

### ShoWorld: Shape-Faithful Multi-Hypothesis Occupancy World Model
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24457v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24457v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24457v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24457v1)

2026/11/18 14:02 GTM

Occupancy world models struggle to forecast moving objects due to motion uncertainty and shape distortion. A single trajectory can average plausible futures into an implausible path, and repeated re-voxelization can erode or inflate object shape even under correct motion. We introduce ShoWorld to address these challenges through separate motion and shape representations. The motion forecaster predicts probabilistic trajectories conditioned on scene layout, nearby agents and the future ego trajectory. Each hypothesis is rendered into occupancy and fused according to its probability to retain alternative futures. The shape component predicts sub-voxel points with occupancy probabilities and offsets from local geometry. These points move rigidly with the object, and voxel occupancy follows the probability that at least one occupied point falls within it. This representation reduces shape distortion under motion. ShoWorld achieves state-of-the-art results on Occ3D-nuScenes. With ground-truth occupancy and ego trajectory, it improves mIoU and IoU by 6.56 and 9.16 points overall and by 6.06 and 5.53 points on dynamic classes.
