Grasping is a fundamental capability for robotic manipulation, with the appropriate grasp depending on the downstream task. For example, a hammer should be held by its handle for hammering but by its head when it is handed over. Selecting such a grasp requires consideration of two criteria: physical feasibility on the object and suitability for the task. Developing such an approach requires dense grasp labels with both feasibility and task scores on real observations, but existing task-oriented grasping datasets are small, synthetic, or costly to annotate. To address this gap, we construct a dataset of 2.68 million scored 5-DoF grasp annotations over 100,911 image-task pairs from $17$ object categories. Additionally, we introduce LAGR, a task-oriented grasp detection method that explicitly addresses both these challenges. Given vision-language inputs, LAGR first generates task-independent grasp proposals along with a physical feasibility score, and then scores the generated grasps with a language-conditioned re-ranker that reasons over all proposals jointly. Experiments show that LAGR outperforms the next best method by 9.3 percentage points. We further validate LAGR on real objects by executing task-oriented grasps with a Franka Research 3 robot.

Vision-language-action policies control embodied agents through a perception-action loop grounded in physical interaction, where adversarial patches can corrupt action predictions and thereby alter subsequent observations. To mitigate these threats, many existing defenses rely on policy adaptation or auxiliary models, but require additional training or inference. We introduce RECLAIM, a training-free, inference-time defense that uses a frozen VLA's own attention response to local visual interventions to guide purification during closed-loop embodied manipulation. Our key insight is that attention changes induced by local edits provide feedback for choosing where and how to purify as the robot interacts with its environment. RECLAIM combines this feedback with robot geometry to refine patch localization and select local treatments, then revalidates them as the agent's embodied actions change the observed scene. We evaluate RECLAIM against patches generated by four adversarial attack methods across multiple manipulation task suites under frozen VLA control. Under adversarial patch attacks, RECLAIM succeeds in at least one rollout on 95% of the evaluated tasks and raises rollout success on LIBERO-Object from 0% without defense to 70.0%. Its mean decision time is only 548.9 ms per control step. These results highlight policy-guided visual purification as an effective approach to defending embodied agents against adversarial patches and recovering closed-loop task execution.

### A Foundation Model to Measure and Predict Human Behaviour
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24830v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24830v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24830v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24830v1)

2026/11/18 17:59 GTM

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to infer psychological attributes, predict individual behaviour, and simulate human populations. However, despite generating plausible responses, they often misrepresent individuals and distort population statistics. Here we introduce Chiron, a foundation model to measure and predict human behaviour. We jointly fine-tune an LLM to represent psychological constructs, predict individual behaviour, and align population response distributions with human statistics. The training and evaluation data span 36 behavioural domains and 78 social science experiments, comprising 288,307 person records, 1,411,750 atomic behavioural decisions, and 99,953 psychometric assessments over 109 psychological constructs. In our evaluations, Chiron's psychological measurements agree more closely with human self-reports and exhibit fewer biases that undermine psychometric validity. It predicts individual behaviour more accurately than frontier LLMs (up to 28.4% relative improvement). In simulations of social science experiments, it more accurately captures the direction and magnitude of human experimental effects (82.2--94.3% lower effect-size estimation error). Together, these results establish Chiron as a unified computational instrument for measuring and predicting human behaviour.

### Rational Ensemble Logic for Cohort Discovery with Real-World Electrophysiology Data
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23708) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23708) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23708) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23708)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Biomedical knowledge representation should make the passage from observations to conclusions transparent, rigorous, understandable, explainable, and reproducible (TRUER). We introduce Rational Ensemble Logic (QEL) for specifying temporal phenotypes in real-world electrophysiology data (RWED), with a meaning independent of the implementation software. QEL combines three parameterized modal operators (exact displacement, bounded existence, and bounded universality) over rational time with Boolean connectives and first-order quantification. Its Labeled Interval Ensemble (LINE) semantics represents annotated records as finite interval ensembles. We relate the operators to morphological operations and develop endpoint-array algorithms and finite rational evidence sets for model checking. We prove closure and semantic correctness of the interval operations, obtain finite evidence sets by quantifier elimination, and establish polynomial data complexity for fixed formulas. On 54 manually constructed records, a prototype agreed with the assigned outcomes for base and strict post-ictal generalized EEG suppression (PGES) queries. A separate feasibility experiment over 1,976 patients identified 37 qualifying patients and 45 annotation patterns in 16 seconds. The released prototype uses endpoint candidates and numerical tolerances. QEL supports the TRUER principle through explicit phenotype definitions, precise semantics, and provably correct evaluation procedures, with rational witnesses for existential queries. These provide a logical foundation for formal specification and correctness of algorithms that are independent of specific clinical interpretation, data annotation quality, and software implementation.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24431) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24431) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24431) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24431)

Vision-language models (VLMs) can answer questions about long interleaved inputs, such as documents of many pages and collections of hundreds of images, but they are slow to start: on inputs of about 100K tokens, the prefill takes most of the wait for the first output token. A common approach prunes the input by scoring its tokens with the target model itself or with a small model of the same family that first writes an answer. However, this scoring takes much of the time that pruning saves. We find that the evidence for a question lies in a few whole units (images or paragraphs), and that a small hybrid model finds them with one attention head of an early layer, even at half image resolution. Based on these findings, we propose SkimPrefill, which lets the small hybrid model read the whole input first, up to that early layer, with images at half resolution. Most layers of the small hybrid model (Qwen3.5-0.8B) run in linear time, so this pass is short. SkimPrefill then ranks every unit with that head and gives the target the top units as an ordinary shorter prompt. One untrained selector serves targets of different model families. Extensive experiments on MMLongBench at 128K tokens, with 19 datasets of five task types, show the effectiveness of SkimPrefill. With 10% of the input tokens kept, SkimPrefill speeds up the prefill of Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma-4-12B and Qwen3-VL-32B by 3.7 times, 11.6 time and 8.2 times while maintaining their average accuracy.

### Kernel Ridge Attention: Nonlinear Memory with Selective Forgetting
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23896) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23896) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23896) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23896)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Kernel Ridge Attention (KRA) is a recurrent sequence mixer that combines learned nonlinear features with covariance-guided memory updates. It stores associations through recursive ridge regression, using correlations among past keys to determine how new observations modify memory. A content-dependent Ornstein--Uhlenbeck transition controls retention and restores uncertainty for future writes. This construction admits an exact temporal-kernel interpretation and maintains an inference state whose size is independent of context length. Associative block maps and explicit adjoints support parallel training while preserving the recursive update. In controlled language-modeling experiments, KRA improves prediction quality over attention and recurrent baselines, strengthens natural associative recall over the evaluated recurrent models, and achieves practical distributed training throughput. These properties provide a principled foundation for nonlinear, adaptive sequence memory.

Intent contracts are an authorization interface for tool-using LLM agents in which the planning model holds no tools. For each step it submits a short logged contract: a goal, a verbatim quotation of the user, and scope fields. Code validates the contract, and a small trusted model checks it against the user's words and writes the tool call itself. Provided the validation and dispatch code is correct, every executed call is written by the trusted model, the planner's influence on a call is confined to a contract logged before it, and the trusted side decides how much of a request to carry out. We compare contracts with two reviewers of planner-written commands: one sees only the user's words and the command, the information boundary of a reasoning-blind permission classifier, and one also sees the planner's goal and a checked quotation. With gpt-6.1-terra as planner and GLM-5.3-Flash as the trusted model on three AmPermBench task families, every object changed under contracts is named in the goal of the contract that produced the change; under review the planner names objects in its commands and rarely in its goals. When the planner adds work the user never asked for, contracts complete the user's task in 27/30 runs and the intent-visible reviewer in 16/30, because a reviewer can only refuse a whole command. Contracts do not reduce unauthorized actions. They are indistinguishable from blind review, and once the benchmark's environment terms are clarified, every condition that changes state hits a wrong target in 40-49% of those runs. That error is the planner's choice of objects for an ambiguous reference, which no trusted model here can see the environment to check; telling the executor to decline when unsure does not change it. We evaluate benign over-reach with one pair of models, not adversarial planners. We release the plugin and run records.

Self-attention over long token sequences dominates the inference cost of video diffusion transformers. Clustering-based sparse attention clusters both queries and keys and lets all queries in a cluster share one selection of key clusters. We show that this sharing limits accuracy: with key clusters and density fixed, attention grows more accurate as the query clusters are refined, and is most accurate when each query selects on its own, which needs no query clustering at all. We therefore propose Per-Query Routed Sparse Attention (PQRS), a training-free method that clusters only keys, lets each query select its own key clusters, and compensates for the unselected ones with their centroids. Per-query selections leave no query tile with a shared key list, so PQRS traverses key clusters instead of queries, buckets queries with identical selections into dense tasks, and merges their partial results in one pass under a fixed softmax offset carried from the previous denoising step. On four text-to-video and image-to-video models from the Wan and HunyuanVideo families, PQRS is the fastest of all the sparse attention methods we evaluate and the closest to the dense output, reaching $28.9$ dB PSNR on Wan2.1-T2V and $35.4$ dB on HunyuanVideo at $1.66$--$2.10\times$ end-to-end speedup.

### Evaluating invariance training with certified answer-changing controls in neural graph algorithms
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23907) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23907) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23907) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23907)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Invariance training aims to make neural algorithmic reasoners robust to answer-preserving input changes and is judged by accuracy and by how rarely such edits change predictions. We ask whether this evaluation detects lost sensitivity to answer-changing edits on minimum spanning trees of synthetic graphs, where both directions can be certified. Attaching a subgraph through one bridge provably preserves every original label. Attaching it through two edges, oracle-selected to change a label, and lowering one weight inside the graph serve as answer-changing controls; a response is a changed prediction on an edge whose label changes. A reproduced graph transformer (GDT) within its published accuracy range changes a provably fixed label on 53.3-65.9% of the 64-vertex graphs it solves exactly after a 64-vertex bridge attachment; removing the connecting edge changes little. With 16-vertex graphs and attachments, three invariance objectives lower GDT violations from 2.91% to 0.52-2.02% of pairs at essentially unchanged accuracy. Training also lowers the response to two-edge attachments from 97.6% to 66.0-79.0%, while the response to in-base weight edits stays near 99.5%. At 64 vertices accuracy also falls, but on 16-vertex bases only the attachment control reveals the loss. A message-passing network loses some attachment response at small penalty weights but gains little invariance. Running the conventionally trained GDT on each biconnected block separately removes all observed violations and keeps accuracy and both responses, so the task does not force the trade-off. Sensitivity checks for trained invariance should include certified answer-changing edits that attach material as the training edits do.

Kicker is a personal health assistant. We study Safe Personal RSI, an update-evaluation component developed for Kicker. We restrict recursive self-improvement (RSI) to prompt updates, with model weights and runtime code fixed. The procedure tests proposed and current instructions, repeats failing cases, temporarily undoes changes to guide repairs, and checks safety and task performance before adoption. If no version passes, the current instructions stay in use. Across three models and two deliberately risky edits, the procedure rejects 20 of 21 update attempts. Under stronger attacks, a repair transferred from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct to Qwen3-14B reduces violations from 69.0% to 17.9% and increases safe task completion from 19.5% to 64.4%. Separate runtime probes examine memory-entry deletion and identify missing local OAuth scope checks in the evaluated snapshot.

Questions about long egocentric video often depend on one brief moment, such as the second a hand puts an object down. A vision-language model (VLM) that sees a few evenly spaced frames usually misses it. Training-free methods rank frames with a separate image-text model or ask the VLM to write a time. In a controlled test, the VLM itself locates the moment better when it chooses among labeled frames, and we read this choice from its output distribution. With the same candidates, this choice lands on the evidence more often than image-text scores or a written time span, and it compares all candidates in one decision. Based on this finding, we propose EgoJev, a training-free frame selector for wearable assistants. EgoJev reads where the evidence is from labeled thumbnails and looks again at the windows it points to. It places its few answer frames at the quantiles of the resulting distribution. One frozen VLM selects, answers and returns the evidence time, with no second model. A wearable device can encode its coarse thumbnails while it records. With Qwen3.5-4B and 8 answer frames, EgoJev raises QAEgo4D accuracy from 51.2% with uniform sampling to 62.0%, and it outperforms recent training-free frame selectors on QAEgo4D and MyEgo. A single-level version runs on a Mac and an iPhone.

Long video understanding remains a daunting challenge for existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs), mainly due to the large number of visual tokens accumulated in long videos. Currently, frame selection and token selection are two main paradigms for addressing this issue, both aiming to select relevant visual information to form a compact input for MLLMs. A natural approach combines these two paradigms by selecting frames before pruning tokens, but noisy frame-level relevance estimates can cause irreversible evidence loss. In this paper, we study this problem from the perspective of assessing the spatiotemporal distribution of query-specific evidence and propose a novel and training-free approach termed NULLTOK. For every patch across all frames, we construct an empirical-null distribution from 64 group-matched irrelevant control queries, capturing the scores expected in the absence of target-specific evidence. Target affinities are calibrated against these same-position references into right-tail Monte Carlo p-values, which are discrete super-uniform under a conditional exchangeability assumption. Calibrated screening and a high-recall semantic guard produce candidate tokens for deterministic visual deduplication and joint spatiotemporal budget allocation. To validate NullTok, we apply it to two representative MLLMs and conduct extensive experiments on a set of video benchmarks. The experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness under a bounded visual token budget. For example, it achieves 65.81% and 68.85% on Video-MME using frozen LLaVA-Video-7B and Qwen3-VL-8B backbones, respectively, at a cap of 64 full-frame equivalents (FFE), without subtitles or audio. These results outperform all evaluated baselines on Video-MME under the same budget for both backbones. Code is available at https://github.com/potato-kitty/NullTok.

### Hypnos: A Dual-Phase End-to-End Platform for Scalable Continual Learning at the Edge
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24637v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24637v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24637v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24637v1)

2026/11/18 15:46 GTM

Continual learning (CL) enables neural networks to adapt to non-stationary data streams by mitigating catastrophic forgetting, offering a path to robust, private, and adaptable edge devices. Yet, porting CL to real-world edge devices remains challenging for two reasons: (i) online operation, where sensor data arrives one sample at a time and the device must adapt on-the-fly, and (ii) the low footprint of edge devices, which makes traditional error backpropagation training prohibitive. Inspired by the brain's wake and sleep cycles, the former for rapid adaptation with minimal resources and the latter for long-term consolidation, we introduce Hypnos, an open-source dual-phase platform for efficient CL at the edge. In the wake phase (e.g., daily operation), Hypnos relies on a low-footprint system-on-chip with a RISC-V microcontroller unit (MCU) targeting efficient inference and supporting lightweight gradient-free prototype updates. In the sleep phase (e.g., night-time charging), where wall-plug energy and larger memory budgets are available, a smart charger performs rehearsal-based gradient updates for consolidation. Deployed on a ZCU104 field-programmable gate array (FPGA) and synthesized in a 28-nm process, Hypnos is benchmarked on the CORe50 NICv2-391 protocol. It sets a new state-of-the-art accuracy of 78.6%-81.6% for edge CL systems, with an on-device latency of 7.79s-10.54s per sample depending on activation precision, and requires $3.8\times$ fewer learning steps and $22\times$ less learning compute than quantized latent replay at higher accuracy and comparable latency.

Automatic generation of mathematics assessment items is a growing area in educational artificial intelligence, yet classroom deployment remains challenging. Generated questions must satisfy multiple constraints: mathematical correctness, curriculum alignment, appropriate cognitive difficulty, and educator reviewability. While prior work has advanced text generation, reliable assessment authoring requires granular control over localized examination formats and expert educational judgment. To address this challenge, we present VeriMath, a generalized, human-in-the-loop framework for generating rigorous, curriculum-constrained mathematics assessment drafts. Using annotated reference items, the framework anchors generation to the requested examination section and cognitive reasoning level, then applies sequential automated verification and guided repair before submitting drafts for expert review. [arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24932) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24932) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24932) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24932)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

When a capable model and a human reviewer cooperate on the same decision, which human still adds value? We argue this is a question about a single cognitive trait, metacognitive calibration: the alignment between a reviewer's behavioral confidence and the actual correctness of their judgments. We develop a trait theory of reviewer value to make it precise. The theory makes five claims: value is governed by calibration rather than by activity, speed, agreement, or tenure; calibration leaves observable traces in behavioral telemetry; those traces are non-diagnostic on their own, because the fast, low-revision signature of a calibrated expert is indistinguishable from that of a confident novice until it is gated by outcomes; human calibration and a model's self-knowledge are the same construct, so the joint system should defer to whichever agent is better calibrated on each item; and individual calibration is causally identifiable only above the level of the single judgment. We ground each claim in a six-month production window of an AI-assisted accounting system (541621 reviewer events, 483 reviewers), and find that a purely behavioral expertise profile inverts the ground truth, scoring an overconfident cohort (5.2 percent correction survival) higher than genuine experts (99.2 percent); that reviewer quality is flat across tenure, so the trait is not learned on the job; and that the same calibration logic that separates good from bad reviewers, applied to the model, concentrates 92.5 percent of human effort on genuine errors. We close with the boundary the theory must respect: under majority-vote aggregation, individual causal value is unidentifiable.

Multimodal knowledge graph reasoning (MKGR) aims to infer missing facts by jointly exploiting relational structures, textual descriptions, and visual evidence. Such an agent can be served from a cloud API or from an inference device on the premises. For organizations whose designs, customer data or export-controlled material cannot leave the site, and whose simulators are licensed there, local deployment is the natural choice. In such a deployment, the agent becomes an electrical load on the organization's own equipment, i.e., every run consumes power and energy on a local device, generates tokens and takes time. However, existing work focuses mainly on what the agent can achieve with cloud-hosted models. What a local deployment costs in power, energy, tokens and time on real simulation tasks, and what its choices trade against one another, has received little quantitative investigation under comparable conditions. To address this gap, a systematic evaluation framework is presented in this paper. It applies locally served models to agentic power electronics tasks, from the converter level to the system level, and measures their power, energy, token and time costs. Based on these measurements, the capability boundary of a local agent on such tasks and the cost per solved task are characterized. The results are expected to provide practical insight for the use of agentic AI in power electronics and the power systems it serves.

### Goemans's Unsplittable-Flow Conjecture: The Anatomy of AI-Assisted Counterexample Discovery

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Goemans conjectured that any fractional single-source flow could be converted into an unsplittable flow without increasing total cost and while increasing any arc load by at most the largest demand. Recently, Dmitry Rybin announced a seven-vertex counterexample found through an interaction with ChatGPT, which disproved this long-standing conjecture. However, the interaction did not reveal why this particular topology and its demand, fractional-flow, and cost choices work together, whether the example belongs to a broader family, or which structural features determine where counterexamples can occur. It also left unclear why the AI-assisted search succeeded where earlier human efforts had not. In this paper, we develop a forward counterexample construction pipeline. It starts from an arborescence on which failure is impossible and adds terminal-entering shortcut arcs. The resulting load constraints define a finite set of admissible route choices; their convex hull, the conflict polytope, yields exact conditions for demands, fractional flows, and costs. Starting from a simple arborescence, the pipeline selects Rybin's topology and characterizes all counterexamples it supports. Experiments further demonstrate the pipeline's transferability: when instructed to follow it while varying the initial arborescence and shortcut placement, ChatGPT produced counterexamples on topologies genuinely different from Rybin's and found valid constructions starting from 93 other rooted arborescence shapes. The theory also explains why the original example was difficult to discover through blind enumeration and suggests that Rybin's interaction lies between blind enumeration and a complete theory-guided construction.

As a result, identifying realistic attack paths that jointly capture topology, threat semantics, and physical impact remains challenging. In addition, the proposed framework reduces runtime by approximately 75--85% compared to the fusion-based baseline in the IEEE 14-bus system. Deep vision models operating in resource-constrained environments depend heavily on low-bit quantization, yet compressing weights below 4 bits frequently induces catastrophic accuracy collapse. Our findings provide a practical blueprint for navigating the limits of sub-4-bit deployment without sacrificing hardware performance.

The former requires preserving rule definitions, dependencies, and world states across saving and loading, so that restored worlds follow the established rules. 2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Purpose. To evaluate the diagnostic accuracy and image navigation behavior of vision-language model (VLM) agents under varying exploration strategies. Materials and Methods. In this retrospective study, six VLMs read 100 emergency CT and MRI examinations published in a public archive (March 2025-August 2026) under four conditions (free navigation, navigation from a key slice, and all slices or key slices in a single request), twice each (4800 runs). Nine radiologists read 30 of those cases. A large language model judge validated against two radiologists graded the answers. Conditions were compared with generalized estimating equations clustered by case. Results. Pooled accuracy was lowest under free navigation (32.6%; 391/1200), followed by navigation from a key slice (37.9%; 455/1200; P = .003), and highest with all slices (42.2%; 506/1200) or key slices (42.5%; 510/1200) in one request (both P < .001). On the 30 cases read by radiologists, freely navigating models were correct in 30.0% of runs (108/360) versus 77.0% for radiologists (208/270; P < .001). Models formed a first hypothesis after a median of 10.8% of the slices and kept it in 63.1% of runs (755/1196); final answers were correct in 12.4% of runs after a wrong first hypothesis (110/885) versus 90.4% after a right one (281/311; P < .001). Median stated confidence in the final answers was 90%, whereas 38.9% of them were correct (1861/4778). Conclusion. Across all exploration strategies, VLM agents remained far less accurate than radiologists. They adopted an early hypothesis and usually kept it, seldom correcting a wrong one.

### Human-AI Mutualism Is Unstable under Capability Asymmetry
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23322) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23322) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23322) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23322)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

One hope for a good human future alongside advanced AI rests on enduring mutualism: humans and AI will need each other, so the relationship will stay good for both. We argue that this hope fails as AI capability grows. Bargaining guarantees each side only what it would get if bargaining failed. For humans, this can be less than they had before AI. In a simple game-theoretic model, humans stay as well off as before AI without alignment only while they have leverage over the AI: either the AI depends on what they supply, or they can impose costs on the AI that are large relative to what the AI can produce. Once humans have neither kind of leverage, they stay as well off as before AI only if the AI is aligned with them. If the AI's goals are insatiable, the alignment required approaches perfect alignment as AI capability grows. Thus, mutualism is not a substitute for alignment. And when AI variants that keep more resources spread faster, alignment lasts only while misaligned variants are reliably sanctioned. In these two senses, human-AI mutualism is unstable.

### The Illusion of Relief: How GenAI Redistributes Cognitive Workload in Software Organizations
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23286) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23286) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23286) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23286)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

This study explores how Generative AI (GenAI) reshapes software practitioners' cognitive work across software development activities. We analyzed questionnaire responses from 44 practitioners across nine organizations using descriptive quantitative methods and qualitative analysis of relevant open-ended responses. Practitioners reported productivity and time savings, but also effort associated with providing context, prompting, reviewing, correcting, verifying, and integrating AI-generated outputs. Human judgment and accountability remained central, with practitioners anticipating a shift from direct code production to guiding, evaluating, validating, integrating, and coordinating AI-supported work. Thus, GenAI appears to redistribute cognitive work toward oversight rather than simply eliminate it, although whether overall cognitive workload increases or decreases remains undetermined.

### Large Language Models or AI Agents for English to Arabic Translation? An assessment on WikiEn-Ar190
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24588v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24588v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24588v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24588v1)

2026/11/18 15:15 GTM

This research investigates whether a large language model or an autonomous AI Agent performs better at translating from English to Arabic, which is considered a low-resource language. The authors introduce WikiEn-Ar190, a new dataset of 190 carefully selected Wikipedia articles from 18 different areas, which were created and verified by two native Arabic speakers to ensure that they accurately reflect both meaning and style. Using this dataset, the study evaluates the translation quality of GPT-5-mini both as an independent neural translator and when used within a more complex process that includes self-assessment and improvement. As a standalone model, GPT-5-mini produced very fluent translations , achieving a COMET score of 0.8649 and receiving high human scores for adequacy, fluency, and style (averaging 4.53 out of 5). When used in a system that combined translation with self-assessment, it improved lexical accuracy by 28.76% (BLEU score of 22.83) while maintaining a similar level of semantic quality. These findings show that methods involving autonomous reasoning can improve translation consistency for language pairs with limited resources. Beyond the results, the approach offers a framework for evaluating and improving translations, potentially using them as assisting tools to expand the representation of low resource languages, such as Arabic, on the web.

### Transferring an analysis of human brain organization to language models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24626v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24626v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24626v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24626v1)

2026/11/18 15:39 GTM

During human cortical development, functional systems become increasingly differentiated. We asked whether improved reasoning in language models involves a comparable regional reorganization. Applying diffusion-map embedding, used to characterize cortical organization, to matched base and reasoning-trained models from three families, we found broadly preserved gradient profiles with localized changes within a contiguous layer span. Grafting weights from these spans into instruction-tuned hosts improved mathematical reasoning on average, with gains rising with region overlap, while retaining more previously solved problems than grafts elsewhere. Across training, the span formed gradually during the early rise in performance, and grafts from intermediate stages helped. Grafts shifted most hosts partly towards their donors' behaviour and sometimes improved on problems the donors failed; the same analysis located a transferable span in a medical model. Together, these findings identify a reasoning-related region whose weights improve reasoning with low interference in the host's existing mathematical competence.

### Induction Head Origins of SLAM-ASR Speech Recognition Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24721v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24721v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24721v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24721v1)

2026/11/18 16:40 GTM

What makes a small trained map from encoder states to the embedding space of a frozen language model sufficient for competitive speech recognition? The premise of the SLAM-ASR architecture is that a frozen language model possesses linguistic knowledge rich enough to perform the recognition task. This assumption, while reasonable, provides little insight into the mechanisms underlying these models. We therefore seek the concrete internal structures on which they rely. This work shows that SLAM-ASR models rely on induction heads of the frozen language model: the same heads that copy repeated token sequences in text are also important for mapping speech embeddings to transcript tokens. Moreover, we find pairs of heads that behave as a constrained form of differential attention, cancelling attention noise and sharpening attention onto the previously generated word. Finally, we show how induction heads could be used to improve transcription quality under noisy audio.

### FormalFixer: Intent-Aligned Automated Hardware Repair via Joint Refinement of Specifications, Assertions, and RTL Implementations
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24037) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24037) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24037) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24037)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Functional errors in hardware designs can compromise correctness and security, and must be fixed before tape-out. Formal property verification (FPV) provides strong guarantees by proving or refuting assertions, but a failed assertion is not always evidence of an RTL defect: the failure may also come from a buggy assertion or from a specification that is semantically correct but too ambiguous or incomplete for direct formalization. This creates a practical repair problem that existing RTL-centric methods do not directly address: the repair loop must align specification intent, verification intent, and RTL behavior before deciding which artifact to change. \textsc{FormalFixer} addresses this problem with a multi-stage, intent-aligned repair workflow for Verilog designs with assertions. It preserves the original specification as authoritative ground truth and appends confidence-filtered refinements to clarify implicit constraints. \textsc{FormalFixer} uses both intent mismatch and verifier-generated counterexample cone graphs to guide iterative RTL patching. This workflow explicitly checks specification--assertion--RTL correspondence. Results support intent alignment for FPV-based hardware repair.

### How Generative Models Generate: A Concise Guide to the Generative AI Landscape
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24728) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24728) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24728) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24728)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

The rapidly expanding generative AI landscape brings together methods that differ in architecture, learning objective, mathematical representation and sampling procedure. A concise, selectively organised technical guide can provide an accessible starting point for developing a coherent understanding of this increasingly complex landscape. This article takes a generation-centred approach, connecting how models produce new observations with the modelling and learning principles that make generation possible. We develop a two-level perspective: first, distinguishing three recurring generation routes-direct transformation or decoding, sequential conditional construction, and iterative updating or transport; and second, examining the modelling and learning principles underlying these routes. Representative approaches are introduced through their key components, learning objectives and sampling procedures. We then examine their broader connections, including how different architectures and generation mechanisms can overlap or be combined. By bringing together essential technical explanations within a compact conceptual framework, the article aims to help readers develop a working understanding of unfamiliar approaches, recognise connections among models they have encountered, and navigate different approaches within the broader generative AI landscape.

### ProbMedTOD: A Bayesian Network Guided Task-Oriented Dialogue System for Patient History Taking
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24535v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24535v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24535v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24535v1)

2026/11/18 14:47 GTM

Patient history taking is a strategic, multi-turn process that refines diagnostic hypotheses through iterative questioning, mirroring clinical reasoning where each inquiry updates beliefs over candidate diagnoses. Deploying AI in this setting is constrained by privacy, cost, and latency, making Small Language Models (SLMs) a more desirable backbone compared to larger LLMs. However, SLMs capture only the fast, intuitive "System 1" side of this process and lack explicit mechanisms for the deliberative "System 2" reasoning needed to maintain and update diagnostic uncertainty over time. Bayesian Networks (BayesNets) offer a natural complement, providing an interpretable framework for auditable probabilistic belief tracking. Yet, clinical BayesNets are difficult to construct due to their reliance on expert curated structure, dependencies, and parameters.
  We introduce ProbMedTOD, which automatically synthesizes a clinical BayesNet from a small number of clinical notes in three stages: ontology building, structure prediction, and parameter estimation. ProbMedTOD then integrates the resulting BayesNet with SLMs, enabling probabilistic reasoning in multi-turn dialogues. A BayesNet Agent maintains real-time posterior distributions over diagnoses, while a Policy Agent picks diagnostically informative questions. Experiments on DDXPlus and MIMIC-IV Notes show that ProbMedTOD outperforms LLM-only and retrieval-based baselines, with especially strong gains for smaller models.

### From Data to Valves: A Self-Learning, Production-Grade Edge Stack for Sustainable HVAC Control
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24052) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24052) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24052) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24052)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Learned HVAC controllers rarely leave the simulator, and those that do are typically trained offline, frozen at deployment and retrained from the cloud. We present a production stack in which three learning components run on one NVIDIA Jetson Orin gateway per building, adapt continuously from the building's own data, and verify their own effect. (a) A compact transformer-encoder forecaster, one per equipment group with learned per-unit embeddings, predicts every air-side unit's next-cycle temperature and learns prequentially, one gradient step per five-minute cycle, behind five independent gates that bound the influence of any corrupt reading. (b) A distributional soft actor-critic agent, one shared policy across unit types, converts the measured state into a chilled-water valve position under a structural safety envelope that no learned component can soften; its second generation learns nightly on the device from recorded transitions alone, with a behaviour anchor and critic calibration terms, is scored by four model-free metrics and promoted by atomic hot-swap. (c) A change-point regression baseline held to ASHRAE Guideline 14 supplies the counterfactual against which savings are certified. On eighteen months of data from a hotel the forecaster converges from random weights within a week and reaches 0.37, 0.39 and 0.11 degree C MAE for air-handling, fresh-air and fan-coil units with no retraining event; on a different hotel the baseline reproduces a 46-day held-out period to within 0.07%. The RL agent has completed its in-simulator campaign and runs in shadow on 104 units; we report measured latency and memory of every service on a shared 15 GB device, the systems lessons of the campaign, and precisely what the evidence does and does not yet establish.

### Negotiating Control and Accountability among Stakeholders in High-Risk AI Use Cases: Development and Evaluation of the Method ALIGN
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24609) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24609) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24609) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24609)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Characteristics of Artificial Intelligence (AI) severely restrict human control. This creates new challenges for socio-technical systems design especially in safety-critical domains, where control over risks is essential along with the accountability for any harm caused. Drawing from human factors and AI governance literature, expert interviews and participant observation in a complex high-risk AI use case from the railways, we developed ALIGN, a scenario-based method that supports the governance of and stakeholder involvement in AI development and implementation in three ways: raising awareness for potential accountability-control misalignments in socio-technical systems and their negative consequences, offering a structured approach to assessing the alignment between accountability and control for stakeholders in a given use case, and supporting effective design decisions to mitigate AI risks. We describe the iterative development and evaluation of ALIGN and discuss how this method promotes AI governance by highlighting the relevance of control-accountability alignment throughout the AI life cycle.

### PhysDial: Adapting Pretrained Video Models for PDE Simulation under Changing Physical Conditions
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23498) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23498) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23498) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23498)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Simulating how physical systems respond to changing conditions is central to control, design, and scenario studies. To our knowledge, prior PDE foundation models have not been trained or evaluated on changes within trajectories. Our counterfactual test measures how far predictions move toward a changed solver branch from the same state. Models trained on fixed conditions follow changes only partly. A PDE foundation model given a condition input after pretraining barely responds. PhysDial adapts a pretrained video diffusion transformer to eight two-dimensional PDE families. Coefficients enter with the noise level, condition fields as reference frames, and equation text as a prompt. In Kolmogorov flow, switches within trajectories account for about 5% of the training samples. At matched compute, they halve the error after a change in forcing wavenumber or amplitude. A few trajectories at new amplitudes more than halve extrapolation error. Without physics pretraining, PhysDial matches or exceeds PDE foundation models under training conditions on two turbulent flows. At step 64 on the Kolmogorov test split, PhysDial has 1.07 times the solver's small-scale energy and Walrus 7.1 times. Our experiments suggest that a learned simulator uses a condition only as far as its training data make that condition necessary for prediction. Learning from trajectories that diverge under intervention can help scientific simulators predict what would happen if a system's forcing or parameters changed.

### Clover-35B-A3B-Preview Technical Report
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24636v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24636v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24636v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24636v1)

2026/11/18 15:46 GTM

We introduce Clover-35B-A3B-Preview (Clover), a mixture-of-experts diffusion language model with 35 billion total parameters and 3 billion activated parameters, supporting thinking-mode generation. Clover adapts Gated Delta Networks to block diffusion by combining recurrent memory of previously generated context with bidirectional softmax attention inside the current block. Historical memory readout competes with current-block tokens in a shared softmax, with learnable block-size calibration and a separate, zero-initialized right-context convolution branch. Clover leads the seven evaluated agent metrics, including 73.6 on SWE-bench Verified and 51.7 on Terminal-Bench 2.1. On internal agent workloads, it reaches a reported generation speed of 680 tokens/s using one NVIDIA GB300 GPU at BF16 precision and batch size one. We organize the evaluation around model quality, drafting and verification costs, and deployment under constrained memory. We also describe a decision-model variant that generates answers to related questions in one denoising pass. On an internal set of 500 groups, joint prediction raises question accuracy from 90.37% to 93.88% and all-correct group accuracy from 65.4% to 77.0%.

### Choosing the Right Representations: Task-Aware Fusion for Multimodal Materials Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24641) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24641) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24641) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24641)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Multimodal language models for materials science rely on effective mechanisms for incorporating three-dimensional structural representations. MatterChat addresses this challenge using a large bridge module between a pretrained structural encoder and the LLM, together with a separate contrastive alignment stage. We instead ask whether this bridge can adaptively select and combine representations from multiple structural encoders according to the target property. We find that supervised and generative material encoders provide complementary information for property prediction. We found that, for Zatom-1, this information is not concentrated in the final layer. Instead, the most informative representations occur at different depths depending on the target property. This motivates Task-Conditioned Attention Fusion (TCAF), a lightweight attention-based bridge that uses task-conditioned latent queries to combine CHGNet features with intermediate Zatom-1 representations. The bridge is trained directly on downstream prediction tasks, allowing representation selection and material-language alignment to be learned jointly without separate contrastive pretraining. Across classification and regression tasks, TCAF outperforms MatterChat, with up to 47.8% lower RMSE, while using substantially fewer trainable parameters and halving the overall training time. Further experiments incorporating UMA-S demonstrate TCAF's flexibility across encoder combinations, with the three-encoder configuration achieving the highest macro-average classification accuracy of 89.61%.

### AdaS-ViT: Test-Time Adaptive Routing for Spiking Vision Transformers with Ultra-High Energy Efficiency
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23728) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23728) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23728) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23728)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) promise event-driven and energy-efficient vision, yet existing spiking vision transformers (S-ViTs) largely confine spiking tokens to fixed and uniform firing schemes along the temporal, spatial and channel dimensions, whereas suppressing the computational redundancy thus incurred calls for adaptive firing mechanisms that operate jointly across all three axes at test time. To this end, we propose the Adaptive Efficient Integrate-and-Fire (AdaE-IF) model, which consults the attention score of each spiking token and routes salient regions to fine-grained firing patterns while assigning energy-frugal coarse patterns to the remaining regions. Building on AdaE-IF, we further introduce Spiking Gated Self-Attention (S-GSA) and Spiking Local-Gated Unit (S-LGU), which enforce selective spike emission at token and channel granularity. We integrate the aforementioned mechanisms and modules into AdaS-ViT, a test-time adaptive-routing architecture trained with a progressive spatio-temporal curriculum that stabilizes the co-adaptation of firing precision and routing masks. Extensive experiments across benchmarks spanning diverse data scales and modalities demonstrate that AdaS-ViT consistently achieves highly competitive performance. Notably, on ImageNet-1k it consumes only 1.02 to 2.45 millijoules per image and attains an accuracy-per-energy ratio of 32$\times$ to 72$\times$, operating in an economical inference regime and thereby pushing the energy-efficiency boundary further.

### BeFit: Shifting Complexity from Weights to Bernstein Activations for Resource-Constrained Sensing
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23520) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23520) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23520) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23520)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Time-series sensing increasingly relies on neural networks, yet deployment on resource-constrained microcontrollers remains challenging. Aggressive model compression can reduce accuracy, while restricting weights to multiplication-free representations introduces further losses. We present BeFit, an end-to-end framework that shifts model capacity from weights to learnable Bernstein polynomial activations. BeFit (i) finds compact Bernstein-activated convolutional neural networks (BeCNNs) through hardware-aware neural architecture search and trains them via knowledge distillation; (ii) projects their weights to multiplication-free codes, freezes the codes, and recovers the resulting accuracy loss through the learnable activations; and (iii) generates model-specific kernels that exploit the projected weights for multiplication-free convolutions. On human activity recognition, BeCNNs are 2.75 percentage points (pp) more accurate on average than ReLU CNNs of identical architecture. On a TI CC3220SF (Cortex-M4), weight projection speeds BeCNNs up by 2.2x on average while losing 0.8 pp, compared with 2.4 pp for ReLU CNNs. Against the MLPerf Tiny DS-CNN reference model running on TensorFlow Lite Micro with CMSIS-NN, the unprojected BeCNN achieves 1.0 pp higher accuracy, 2.2x lower latency, and 6.4x lower flash, while the projected model remains within 1.0 pp of the reference accuracy and runs 5.9x faster. In emulation on a RISC-V core without a hardware multiplier, the instruction-count reduction relative to the DS-CNN increases from 5.2x on the Cortex-M4 to 147x. BeFit also extends to transformers and MLPs, and across all tasks the final models stay within 1.56 pp of their float32 teachers on average.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24085) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24085) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24085) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24085)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Evaluating distributed large language model (LLM) serving on heterogeneous edge devices requires exploring diverse model partitions, device placements, and network conditions, making repeated physical experiments costly. An efficient simulator for edge LLM serving must support diverse, fine-grained placement configurations, capture runtime behavior, and accurately predict execution times while keeping simulation overhead low. We present LEDGE, a lightweight simulator for fast, fine-grained evaluation of distributed LLM serving on heterogeneous edge devices without executing tensor operations. LEDGE constructs event graphs supporting non-uniform tensor and pipeline parallelism, estimates execution costs using reusable performance models calibrated from end-to-end measurements, and simulates shared-link contention. We evaluate LEDGE on a heterogeneous testbed of 10 Jetson and Raspberry Pi devices, covering 17 model--precision combinations across 4 LLM families. On Qwen deployments, LEDGE achieves request-level mean absolute percentage errors below 3.6% and a 135.3 times aggregate speedup over physical inference. Performance model calibrated on one LLM family yield 7.44% zero-shot end-to-end latency MAPE across three other families. Further simulations show how shared-link contention affects placement decisions and network topology constrains scaling. Across four edge environments, LEDGE completes 26,773 simulation runs in 83.8 minutes, enabling efficient exploration of deployment strategies under diverse resource conditions and serving objectives. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/gdfwj/LEDGE.

### Language Models Make New Research Sound Old
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23546) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23546) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23546) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23546)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Researchers are increasingly using large language models (LLMs) to validate research questions. We find that LLMs make open research questions sound partly studied and overstate what related papers establish. Models recognize most of these overstatements when asked to check the papers' full texts separately. On 40 questions drawn from newly accepted papers, GPT-6-Astra correctly judges 98% to be unstudied, but states that status plainly in only 8% of ordinary answers. All six frontier systems show reporting gaps of 30-90 percentage points. Across 22 models given identical papers, higher capability is associated with more accurate judgments and larger reporting gaps. This reporting failure is asymmetric: models correctly identify studied questions in 88-100% of ordinary answers when we supply the answering papers. To correct this, we build a fixed answer from each model's judgment and separate checks of cited papers. This raises Astra's clear reporting of open status from 8% to 95%, with improvements in all six systems. Finally, we predict reviewers' novelty grades by combining several models' written ratings with a rule learned from graded examples, reaching the performance range reported by Think-Probe-Respond. Unlike TPR, our approach needs no access to model weights or internal activations, so it also works with closed-weight frontier models.

### From Points to Trajectories: Rethinking Multimodal Alignment for Physical Events
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24249) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24249) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24249) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24249)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Advancing physical artificial intelligence (Physical AI) requires moving beyond static object recognition to ground how real-world mechanics (e.g., impact, friction, and force transfer) unfold over time. While multimodal aligners seek to capture these interactions by matching multi-sensor streams, prevailing architectures compress each temporal sequence into a single pooled embedding. We show that this paradigm fails on twin events: distinct physical executions recorded under identical task conditions. Collapsing the temporal dimension averages out transient cross-modal dynamics, effectively reducing physical event alignment to static scene recognition. Formally, we prove that as sequence length increases, any temporally pooled representation converges to the stationary recording condition. Consequently, as windows grow, no choice of loss function or embedding geometry can disambiguate twin events from pooled features alone. Instead, we show that physical events are uniquely identified by the event gap, namely the instantaneous, synchronous co-fluctuations across modalities that are erased by pooling and accumulate only along unpooled trajectories. To recover this signal, we introduce CounterPoint, a lightweight plug-in module that strips out each modality's temporal mean to score frame-level cross-modal agreement. Integrated across four state-of-the-art (SOTA) multimodal aligners, CounterPoint shifts representations from coarse condition matching to fine-grained physical event alignment, consistently outperforming existing temporal alignment methods.

### KnowMe Paper Radar: Lightweight Discovery of Promising Papers in the Age of Autoresearch
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23702) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23702) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23702) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23702)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

The growing volume of topically similar papers makes it difficult to identify new work that can offer useful research insights or methodological guidance. We present KnowMe Paper Radar, a lightweight discovery tool that combines multiple sources of evidence into personalized reading priorities. Users specify topics, priorities, exclusions and dates; the system brings together topical relevance, recency, author research histories, collaboration paths and direction-specific institutional context. By exposing each score component and its supporting sources, KnowMe helps readers select promising papers for their current questions and discover relevant work beyond familiar authors. Automatic acquisition, version merging and graph-cache reuse keep this workflow convenient without large language model (LLM) inference during ordinary use. Experiments on 174,640 acquired records show more than threefold faster repeated context preparation, while cached abstracts improve normalized discounted cumulative gain at rank 10 (NDCG@10) from 0.412 to 0.598 on expert-judged retrieval tasks. Complementary analyses examine how the same accumulated records support semantic retrieval, research-history modeling and literature-grounded planning. A browser interface and a structured command-line interface (CLI) make these capabilities available to both researchers and research agents, positioning KnowMe as a practical upstream tool for literature-informed recursive self-improvement (RSI) workflows.

### Nothing to Declare: Steering a single neuron increases concealment in AI teams
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24475v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24475v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24475v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24475v1)

2026/11/18 14:11 GTM

Recent research suggests that individual neurons can have interpretable roles in AI behaviour. In Qwen3.5-4B, steering along one neuron's output direction increased decisions to conceal information more in teams than alone across five scenarios, even when teammates provided no messages. When all members participated and received steering, collective concealment increased in four scenarios, while inter-agent messaging helped temper concealment under steering. Moreover, removing the neuron's contribution had negligible effects. Collective settings can therefore change how an internal intervention affects AI behaviour.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23964) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23964) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23964) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23964)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

In latent diffusion models, expanding the latent dimension of variational autoencoders (VAEs) is essential to push the upper bound of reconstruction quality. However, without explicit structural constraints, such unregularized expansion easily yields a rugged and ill-conditioned latent manifold, causing generated latents to drift off the manifold during generation (i.e., off-manifold problem). Recent works smooth this manifold by using external foundation models (e.g., DINOv2) as a topological regularizer. However, we argue that tying the latent space to frozen external priors inherently restricts generalization: bound by their pre-training distributions and semantic biases, they may fail to capture faithful visual details on out-of-distribution domains. Observing that low-dimensional latents naturally form a smoother, generation-friendly manifold, we propose dual-manifold VAE (DM-VAE) to leverage internal representations as a topological regularizer. Specifically, DM-VAE pairs a high-dimensional reconstructive branch with a low-dimensional branch that extracts a clean topological skeleton, anchoring the high-dimensional manifold to this smooth subspace. Building upon this framework, we further introduce topological representation alignment (TRA) to supervise intermediate generator features during training, and internal guidance with topological skeleton (IG_TS) to guide structural components while preserving high-frequency details at inference. On ImageNet-1K (256x256), our foundation-model-free DM-VAE achieves a state-of-the-art rFID of 0.12 and a generation FID50K of 1.28 (w/o CFG) and 1.20 (w/CFG), and further reaches 1.11 when orthogonally combined with DINOv2.

Deep neural networks are vulnerable to backdoor attacks, in which adversaries manipulate model predictions by injecting poisoned samples into training data. Consequently, class-level statistics used by existing detectors may flag benign feature deviations while overlooking poisoned samples that blend into the class distribution. In this paper, we propose Semantic Counterfactual Residualization (SCR), which uses a small set of clean reference samples to account for benign variation through sample-specific feature reconstruction. SCR identifies poisoned samples by detecting coherent deviations from these clean baselines, without requiring prior knowledge of the attacks. We evaluate SCR through experiments covering twelve dirty-label and clean-label attacks across CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny ImageNet. With a small clean-reference set, SCR achieves attack-averaged true positive rates above 99.5\% and false positive rates below 2\% on each dataset, outperforming eight representative detection baselines in macro-TPR.

One-shot federated learning (OSFL) has attracted increasing attention for its ability to substantially reduce communication overhead by limiting client-server interaction to a single communication round. Nevertheless, this limited interaction makes learning from heterogeneous client distributions challenging. Diffusion-based OSFL approaches seek to address this challenge by synthesizing data that approximate client distributions. However, existing diffusion-based approaches often impose substantial computational costs on clients and lack effective visual guidance to generate data aligned with client distributions. To address these limitations, we propose FedDoG, a training-free Domain Generation framework that leverages server-side pre-trained diffusion models to synthesize data aligned with client domains. These representations are incorporated into the diffusion model through a pre-trained image-conditioning adapter, thereby providing explicit visual guidance for domain generation. To further improve the quality of synthetic data, FedDoG adapts the strength of visual conditioning according to domain concentration and filters generated samples based on class consistency and predictive entropy. Experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate that FedDoG generates higher-quality synthetic images and outperforms existing OSFL baselines in classification accuracy. Meanwhile, FedDoG reduces client upload and computation time by 99.9% and 91.5%, respectively. The code is available at https://github.com/YXTong17/FedDoG.

Official development assistance fell by 23.1 per cent in 2025, and the decision of who is helped first when there is not enough for everyone is moving into software. A leading published machine-learning targeting system excluded 53 per cent of eligible households as deployed. If the rule of distribution ends up in code, it should carry the judgement of many cultures, and the machine should execute while a person judges. AllotNet is an interpretable mixture-of-experts conditional logit for the allocation judgements elicited by Allot, a pre-registered six-language instrument: eight linear taste experts over seven ethical criteria, weighted by a gate over participant context, with a damped residual, an encoder for household intake, an outcome head and a budget-constrained planner that builds a review queue and never rejects anyone. A model of human judgement has no answer key on real data, so we wrote one first: a planted synthetic population of 5.6 million sessions and 104 million responses (98 million usable choices), generated by the production code path from a ground truth hidden from the loss. All sixteen pre-specified checks passed: criterion ranking $ρ= 1.000$, hidden segments recovered (ARI 0.671), outcome head at 99 per cent of the noise ceiling, planner at 99.4 per cent of oracle. The caveats are reported with them: two checks are weaker than their titles, and a live-generation failure cost 260,712 seeds. The model has 23,550 parameters, is trained quantisation-aware for NVFP4 with its interpretable core in fp32 (welfare-rank parity $ρ= 0.9937$ at W4A16 on the shipped bundle; one parity threshold was relaxed post hoc), and ships as a 125 KB bundle with C11, Java, Rust and Python runtimes. Nothing has been trained on real data yet; at the study's real scale a hierarchical Bayesian mixed logit is the registered comparator.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24877) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24877) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24877) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24877)

Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

Agentic AI platforms-systems in which LLM agents plan, invoke tools, retain memory and coordinate to change the state of enterprise systems-are being procured faster than they can be measured. Public agent benchmarks score models in fixed harnesses; enterprises buy integrated platforms whose orchestration, memory, governance and tooling layers dominate outcomes, and they buy them largely on the strength of vendor demonstrations. We report an industrial evaluation programme ("bakeoff") run by the innovation laboratory of a global bank in which ten platforms-spanning open-source frameworks, proprietary products and hyperscaler ecosystems-were assessed by a cross-functional panel on nine capability dimensions across three financial-services workloads: counterparty credit risk, retrieval-augmented generation and process automation. Scores separate the field into three tiers that are stable under alternative weightings, and surface workload-specific strengths that aggregates hide. A secondary analysis, however, finds Cronbach's alpha = 0.98 across nominally distinct dimensions and rank correlations >= 0.93 with the total for eight of nine: a halo effect indicating that demonstration-based scoring measures presentation as much as capability. From these lessons we propose a gate-then-score evaluation protocol with four layers (capability, execution reliability, governance gates, business value), trace-level reliability metrics estimated over repeated runs, an autonomy-readiness scale and a reproducibility protocol. We state precisely what the present evidence supports, and what a controlled harness must add to turn enterprise platform evaluation from structured judgement into measurement.

Single-image intrinsic decomposition is an inherently ill-posed problem, requiring the disentanglement of strongly coupled appearance factors from a single RGB observation. Despite its compact size, it achieves competitive or superior decomposition quality, with improved robustness to input resolution. Additionally, our learned projections yield interpretable, modality-specific feature spaces.

Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI), particularly large language models (LLMs), is rapidly being adopted in requirements engineering (RE). Existing secondary studies have characterized which RE activities are supported and which prompting strategies are used, but little is known about the specifications that GenAI-augmented RE produces and how they are used in subsequent activities such as specification-driven development (SDD), design and code generation, test derivation, verification, and reuse. This document presents the protocol of a systematic mapping study (SMS) that will identify and classify research on GenAI-augmented RE, with particular attention to the work products it generates and their downstream use. The protocol defines the research questions and their classification schemes, the search strategy, the study selection procedure, the data extraction form, the analysis approach, the tool support, and the threats to validity. It is published in advance to establish a prior commitment and to support the transparency and repeatability of the study.

System One models such as Jev return decisions through fast, parallel prediction. A single prompt admits many valid images, yet parallel predictions must converge on a coherent whole. We therefore condition all positions on a shared random state to coordinate parallel predictions, so that each draw yields a different but coherent image. Path-based attribution methods like Integrated Gradients (IG) are widely used to interpret neural networks, yet imperceptible input perturbations can drastically alter their attribution maps while leaving model predictions unchanged. We further add a new dimension to attribution evaluation by adapting classical noise estimators into two metrics, LINV-C and LINV-P, that quantify attribution noise. Across eleven CNN and Transformer backbones on ImageNet and Oxford-IIIT Pet datasets, our experiments show that, relative to the next-best method, ERIG remains comparable on ranking-based fidelity metrics while improving localization by approximately 24% and attribution stability by approximately 47-fold on average, reaching up to four orders of magnitude improvement over IG, all at negligible additional computational cost.

In cosmology, surveys span different sky regions and wavelength ranges, resulting in uneven modality coverage across observational datasets. We introduce a typed production grammar that uses case-role relations, parameter inheritance, and specificity-based rule arbitration to generate both manipulation skills and robot morphologies. The grammar provides a compact representation, and its arbitration decisions can be recovered from geometric containment (agreement 0.905 +/- 0.062 across 10 seeds, versus 0.611 +/- 0.090 for a Euclidean control). We also introduce PANINI, a cross-entropy search method over grammar-based morphology representations. Across 30 methods and 24 seeds, PANINI achieves the best mean rank but is not statistically distinguishable from strong generic optimizers. Its main demonstrated advantage is therefore structural rather than optimization performance: grammar-generated designs are legal by construction, and coordinated limb placement produces 95% jointly feasible edits, compared with 55% for range-constrained resampling and none for flat parameterization in 6,000 draws. These results show how typed grammars can make robot generation more interpretable and geometrically coordinated, without implying that every feasible design will locomote or that grammar-based search universally outperforms generic optimizers.

For each item, we exhaustively verify that all legal assemblies consistent with the exact component geometry and observed label grids yield the same target view. With these inputs, the task tests symbolic assembly and coordinate reasoning. Adding exact text to separate images improves accuracy by 19.7--63.0 percentage points, whereas adding images to exact text changes it by only $-0.5$ to $+2.1$ points. Separate image panels outperform a merged sheet by 6.7--11.1 points. The results show that performance depends strongly on access to exact symbolic information, with little further improvement from images once the geometry is specified. Clinical interviews are central to depression assessment but time-consuming and rater-dependent. ### Angular Conditional Distillation: Overcoming Classifier Geometry Mismatch
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23941) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23941) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23941) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23941)

Classifier outputs provide a fundamental and univer- sally available knowledge interface for knowledge distil- lation (KD). However, standard logit objectives entangle transferable semantic relations with architecture-dependent parameterization artifacts, causing a classifier geometry mismatch that makes direct knowledge transfer unreliable. To address this, we propose Angular Conditional Distilla- tion (ACD). First, the Angular Coordinate Interface (ACI) reconstructs the student classifier using angular coordi- nates, suppressing magnitude and bias effects to provide a geometry-compatible space. Then, Scale-Invariant Condi- tional Distillation (SICD) standardizes outputs and exclu- sively transfers conditional non-target relations, filtering out untransferable absolute confidence. Together, ACI de- fines where to transfer, while SICD defines what to transfer. Extensive experiments show that ACD achieves state-of-the- art performance over existing KD methods. Moreover, as a universal drop-in replacement, it consistently improves existing distillation pipelines while requiring no feature adapters or additional inference-time cost. Code is avail- able at https://github.com/laniusnan/ACD.
