### Measuring What Matters in the Age of AI: The Research Software Metrics Landscape
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23253) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23253) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23253) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23253)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Research software now serves as a primary instrument of science, but the methods the community uses to judge its quality, sustainability, reuse, and impact remain scattered across software engineering, open science, FAIR software, citation, and research-assessment literature. AI-assisted development sharpens the problem: code can be produced at rates that outpace human review capacity, provenance and attribution are harder to track, scholarly credit systems cannot keep pace, and existing metrics inherit assumptions about how research software gets developed that may no longer apply. To aid in navigating these challenges, we present a focused landscape synthesis of research software metrics based on 49 substantive references, organizing the recurring metrics into eight families described by data source, claimed construct, evidence of validity, automation potential, and susceptibility to misuse. Many widely cited research software metrics serve as partial signals but make weak standalone measures of scientific impact. The AI era opens or widens gaps around provenance, maintainability of generated code, reviewer effort, model and data dependencies, reuse and community-health signal inflation, and credit for human labor that AI does not perform. We argue for responsible, contextual, mixed-method use of research software metrics, and offer decision guidance for Research Software Engineers (RSEs), principal investigators, institutions, and funders selecting metrics that support better software, fairer credit, and more trustworthy science. All findings rest on the 49-reference focused review; a reproducible search-and-triage protocol and a 727-record triaged corpus accompany the paper as a transparency artifact for a future full systematic mapping study.

### What Survives the Shift from Text to Pixels? Conditional Protection against Prompt Injection in Image-Rendered Documents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24231) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24231) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24231) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24231)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Multimodal language models increasingly answer questions over external documents, exposing them to indirect prompt injection: commands planted in untrusted content to hijack the response. Rendering untrusted content as page images lowers attack success on average, leaving open which injected operations survive rendering, at what utility cost, and whether failed image attacks reflect unread commands or commands read but not followed. We pair text and page-image versions of 191 English question-answering sources across six models, measuring marker appending, answer formatting, and answer replacement alongside clean Question-Answering utility. Rendering reduces append and format execution for every model, yet Qwen3-VL-8B still executes image-borne replacement at 77.96\%. Protection also depends on target type, rendering settings, wording, and repetition, and a search over eight fixed attack candidates largely closes the image deficit in appending and formatting for Qwen and Gemini, though not for GPT-5-nano. Utility ratios on original questions range from 93.31\% to 101.61\%, but Qwen falls to 61.99\% when answers combine two evidence spans. Illegibility cannot explain all failed attacks: models often transcribe and identify image-borne commands they do not execute, and trusted user authorization raises execution on unchanged pages. Inside Qwen, patching early attack-region states induces all three operations, more weakly in images. Attention blocking suggests why: a text command acts through its own positions, whereas visual encoding spreads an image command and its target across the instruction page. Rendering thus offers conditional protection, motivating evaluations that report security by model, operation, and attack budget alongside utility.

RFBench establishes a common testbed for evaluating multimodal RF capabilities and guiding the development of models that connect design intent and layout representations with physical behavior.

### When Figures Disagree with Text: Evaluating Autonomous Visual Evidence Verification in Scientific Papers
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24353) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24353) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24353) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24353)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Scientific figures carry evidence for the claims that cite them, so reviewing a paper means checking that a figure agrees with the text, and not only describing what the figure shows. Benchmarks of figure-text verification typically hand the model the figure, the claim, or the knowledge that an inconsistency exists, which leaves untested whether a model can find the figure worth checking in a complete paper, relate a visual element to the claim it conflicts with, and recognize the conflict. We study this autonomous setting on 31 full manuscripts, each carrying a reviewer-documented figure-text inconsistency from PRISMM-Bench that is hidden from the model. Six multimodal models review each PDF under a neutral prompt, and we then add one input at a time to the same manuscripts. Of 1,445 open-ended reviews, 60.6% mention the flawed figure, yet a grading rubric accepts only 3.3% as diagnosing its contradiction; a blind audit finds the rubric lenient, so we read these as rubric-accepted rates and compare conditions rather than absolute values. In a separate controlled run, naming the figure, without stating that it is flawed, raises rubric-accepted diagnosis from 4.3% to 20.4%, while a consistency instruction and coarse visual cues do not. When the contradiction is visible only in the rendered figure, text-only review yields no accepted diagnosis, while review of the rendered pages with the figure named reaches 15.8%. A baseline that lets the model choose which figures to check, ranked by centrality, stays at 4.8%. Deciding what to verify is thus a large part of the difficulty, and the selection rule we tested does not resolve it. Multimodal document understanding should be evaluated on whether a model can propose what to verify, not only answer questions about a figure it is handed.

### MECI: Multi-Element Collaborative Interaction for Multimodal Entity Linking
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24522v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24522v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24522v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24522v1)

2026/11/18 14:39 GTM

Multimodal Entity Linking (MEL) aims to disambiguate mentions in multimodal contexts by grounding them to specific entities in a knowledge base. A pivotal challenge in MEL is capturing multi-level correspondences: the semantic consistency between mention-entity pairs and the complementary correlations across modalities. However, existing methods often suffer from element dominance due to their reliance on coupled interactions or coarse global aggregations. In response, we propose the Multi-Element Collaborative Interaction (MECI) framework. First, to capture multi-element mention-entity correspondences, we develop a Multi-view Experts Network that leverages a "divide-and-conquer" strategy for decoupled feature learning to mitigate element dominance, supported by a KL-guided routing mechanism that governs expert specialization and collaboration. Furthermore, to model cross-modal complementary correlations, we propose a Hierarchical Multimodal Interaction Module, where a dynamic modality-aware weighting network refines interactions across hierarchical semantic levels, thereby integrating multi-granular evidence to counteract element dominance. Finally, we incorporate a generative semantic refinement stage that utilizes large language models for zero-shot re-ranking. Extensive experiments on WikiDiverse, RichpediaMEL, and WikiMEL show that MECI consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, improving Hits@1 by 1.95%, 7.30%, and 2.31%, respectively.

### HydroMind: A Checkable Agent for Autonomous Hydrological Research
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23323) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23323) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23323) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23323)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

General-purpose coding agents have written code for hydrological and Earth-system models. In these studies the researchers decided in advance how the model would be evaluated, for example the test period, the scoring function, and the standard the model had to meet. No study has tested what happens when the agent makes these decisions itself: how many of its claims are supported by its own data, code, and results, and whether readers can check them. Both may depend on the agent's harness, that is, the tools it can call and the written instructions it follows. We compared three harnesses: the agent's own harness; this harness plus the modeling tools of HydroMind, a hydrological modeling system we developed for general-purpose agents, which run every model through fixed commands and store its settings, trained model, and predictions; and both plus HydroMind's research procedure, under which the agent writes a research design before running any model and records every change to it. Under each harness, the same agent studied four open questions in hydrological model development and was given only the data. Under the three harnesses, the experiments submitted 23, 20, and 41 claims, each stating that one model or approach is better than, worse than, or similar to another. A claim does not hold if the difference recomputed from the experiment's own code and predictions disagrees with it, or if the code did not compare the two models or approaches the claim names. Under the three harnesses, 30%, 20%, and 5% of the claims did not hold. A statistical test, confidence interval, or decision rule that lets readers judge the stated difference was reported for 48%, 53%, and 83% of the claims. With the language model unchanged, changing only the harness reduced the share of claims that do not hold and increased the share that readers can check.

### Generative AI in Software Testing: Practitioners' Perspectives on the Benefits, Challenges, Opportunities, and Risks
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23256) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23256) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23256) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23256)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) increasingly supports software testing, yet empirical evidence on practitioners' perceptions remains limited. This study investigates perceived benefits, challenges, opportunities, and risks of GenAI in software testing through a questionnaire survey of 64 practitioners. Closed-ended responses were analyzed descriptively, while open-ended responses were qualitatively coded to contextualize the findings. Among the respondents, 34 were users (those with regular or experimental use of GenAI in testing) and 30 were non-users (those without prior use of GenAI in testing). Results show generally positive perceptions of GenAI for reducing effort, test generation, and debugging, alongside concerns about prompt dependence, trust, and incorrect outputs. Users were more positive about several concrete benefits while also more frequently prioritizing reliability-related risks, whereas non-users showed greater uncertainty about some technically specific challenges. Overall, the findings position GenAI as an assistive technology requiring human validation and highlight the importance of considering practitioners' experience with GenAI when interpreting perceptions of GenAI-assisted testing.

### Where Global Context and Weight Sharing Work in Compact Vision Backbones
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23446) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23446) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23446) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23446)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Compact convolutional backbones such as EfficientNet achieve strong parameter efficiency through local depthwise separable convolutions, yet two structural questions remain central to hybrid design: where does global self-attention improve recognition over pure convolution, and can repeated blocks share weights without losing representational capacity? We investigate both questions on ImageNet-1k (50,000-image validation) using controlled EfficientNet-B0 experiments. First, we show that wrapping a classification backbone in multi-scale feature pyramids (BiFPN) fails to outperform a plain MBConv trunk (69.99% - 71.81% vs. 72.86% plain B0) until paired with stage attention (73.15%). In contrast, keeping the MBConv trunk intact and inserting lightweight zero-gated residual self-attention adapters at stages P3 (28x28 windowed), P4 (14x14 global), and P5 (7x7 global) reaches 74.02% standalone and outperforms a bit-for-bit matched plain-B0 control by +0.954 percentage points (73.88% vs. 72.92%) across an 88-epoch stateless replay schedule, with learned attention amplitude rising monotonically with semantic depth (27.2% at P3 -> 45.4% at P4 -> 66.3% at P5). Second, we evaluate recurrent weight sharing across repeated MBConv blocks with pass-specific BatchNorm statistics (+1,536 parameters per pass). While post-hoc kernel averaging degrades accuracy to 17.03% and single-template stage tying (-27.6% parameters) plateaus at 66.10%, alternating two shared block templates ([B1, A, B, A]) in Stage 6 cuts total model parameters by 10.9% (582K weights) and recovers by +10.82 points in a single epoch to 70.92% (-1.84 points vs. unshared control).

Generalizable AI-generated video detection remains challenging because natural motion and content variations can resemble synthesis artifacts, making isolated video discrepancies unreliable indicators of authenticity. To address this challenge, we propose Spatial and Contextual Reference-informed Detection (SCRiD), a framework that interprets video variations through complementary spatial and contextual references. Specifically, we introduce Matched-Coordinate Residual Response (MCR) to characterize temporal residual associations under motion and coordinate references with matched subpixel sampling conditions. We further develop Real-Conditioned Score Adaptation (RCSA), which learns a content-conditioned denoising reference from authentic videos and probes its responses across multiple noise scales and quality views to guide feature adaptation. To integrate these complementary sources of evidence, we design Reversible Evidence Exchange (REE) to enable information exchange through reversible symplectic updates. We train SCRiD using synthetic videos from only 4 generators and evaluate it across 88 generator or task subsets spanning 6 benchmarks. Our method achieves average ACC and AP of 72.72% and 74.23%, respectively, outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.24 and 0.81 percentage points.Ablation and robustness experiments further validate the effectiveness of the proposed components and the stability of SCRiD under common post-processing operations.

Manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections (mHC) extend a Transformer's residual stream into multiple parallel streams with learned cross-stream connections. Prior work trains all streams toward a shared objective. We instead study whether assigning distinct learning objectives to different streams can improve task performance and induce objective-specific specialization. Using complementary lexical (sparse) and semantic (dense) retrieval objectives as a test case, we find that objective-specialized mHC consistently outperforms a non-specialized mHC model across the full sparse--dense fusion range, for both two- and four-stream models and at two model scales. We then examine how specialization changes the model internally using probes of stream representations, residual mixing, objective-specific gradient flow, and read/write gates. The specialized and non-specialized models behave similarly through most of the network but diverge in the final layers: the specialized model develops increasingly objective-specific gradient flow, distinct read/write routing patterns, and reduced cross-stream mixing, and streams assigned to different objectives become increasingly dissimilar while those trained toward the same objective remain highly similar. Fixing the residual mixing to the identity leaves the specialization benefit intact, indicating that the benefit stems from objective-specific read/write routing rather than learned cross-stream mixing. These results show that assigning distinct objectives to mHC streams can improve multi-objective retrieval while inducing structured, depth-dependent specialization in the residual computation.

### Horizontal Flow Matching for Single-Cell Spatiotemporal Dynamics

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Learning the spatiotemporal dynamics of cells from spatial transcriptomics requires connecting snapshots of different cells acquired in different coordinate frames. A rotation or translation of a section can appear as cell movement and distort the inferred correspondence. We introduce horizontal flow matching, which learns changes in tissue organization, molecular state, and cell abundance up to shared rigid motion. The dynamics follow a continuity--reaction equation whose action assigns no cost to shared rigid motion, and the resulting horizontal projector removes this component from spatial prediction errors during learning and from predicted velocities during generation. An equivalent endpoint problem jointly estimates correspondence and relative pose in Wasserstein--Fisher--Rao geometry and yields analytic paths whose velocity and growth serve as regression targets. Regressing these targets recovers the horizontal dynamics under ideal population conditions. Our model, HORIZON, uses an equivariant transformer to predict velocity and growth from the current tissue, so that a tissue can be generated continuously from a single initial snapshot. On a simulated tissue observed in perturbed coordinate frames, alignment improves reconstruction and projection reduces rigid drift. On four spatial transcriptomics time courses, HORIZON reconstructs tissue geometry, gene expression, and cell abundance more accurately than existing spatial and unbalanced trajectory methods, and interpolates held-out developmental stages.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24206) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24206) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24206) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24206)

Neural decoding of visual experience from electroencephalography (EEG) has achieved strong performance in intra-subject settings, but current methods fail to generalize across individuals: on the standard 200-way benchmark THINGS-EEG-2, top-1 accuracy drops from above 80% within subjects to about 32% for an unseen subject. Adapting a pre-trained encoder to new subjects and sessions typically requires labeled data, a bottleneck whose collection time severely limits practical device deployment. In a practical Brain-Computer Interface (BCI), however, the possible outcomes are known in advance and form a gallery of candidates, and the unlabeled EEG of the user accumulates as soon as the device is used. We propose a new setting, incremental transductive calibration, in which the decoder is calibrated regularly on all the unlabeled EEG received so far and on the known gallery, without any labels. This setting is closer to the conditions of a deployed BCI than the zero-shot and batch evaluations in current use. We evaluate it with a protocol in which queries arrive one at a time, with unknown and possibly unequal frequencies, and each query is decoded before it is used for calibration. In this setting, we introduce GALA : GAllery-Level Alignment, a label-free calibration method which registers the unlabeled EEG embeddings onto the gallery through shrinkage whitening, hubness correction and a Sinkhorn--Procrustes alignment with query frequencies estimated from the buffer, updated after every query. On THINGS-EEG-2, with a gallery of 200 images, it raises the top-1 accuracy on a new subject from 28% to 67% over 53 minutes of use. Across 60 evaluation settings that vary the gallery size, the query frequencies and the number of repetitions averaged per query, GALA is the most accurate label-free method in every setting, 6.6 points above the best competing method on average at the end of the stream.

Recent advances in large vision-language models (LVLMs) have significantly improved video understanding capabilities, yet processing long videos remains challenging due to the substantial computational cost of extensive visual inputs. Frame selection offers an efficient alternative, but many existing region-based methods rely on fixed or predefined temporal partitions, which may result in suboptimal temporal regions, compromising subsequent budget allocation and frame selection. In this work, we propose Adaptive Partitioning-Based Frame Selection (APS), a training-free framework that adapts temporal partitions to local variations in query-frame relevance. Specifically, APS formulates temporal partitioning as a complexity-regularized fitting problem, jointly optimizing partition boundaries and local models through dynamic programming. The resulting adaptive temporal partitions guide frame budget allocation based on query relevance and temporal extent, enabling informative frame selection under limited visual budgets. Clustering on attributed graphs can produce demographically biased clusters, particularly when communities align with protected groups. Graph transformers can amplify this bias through same-group attention, while global attention limits scalability. We propose FairPGT, a partitioned graph transformer for fair graph clustering. FairPGT restricts attention to METIS partitions and connects them through routing tokens, which divides the quadratic cost of attention by the number of partitions. A graph-aware self-training target guides clustering, with a provably bounded deviation from the standard DEC target. Fairness is imposed at two levels: a mutual-information term promotes demographic parity in cluster assignments, while a normalized cross-group attention ratio (nCGAR) guides attention toward group neutrality through self-distillation. We prove that the nCGAR correction moves attention toward neutrality and establish a condition preventing overshooting. Across seven benchmarks, FairPGT achieves the best clustering performance and statistical parity on the three largest graphs (Credit, Pokec-n, Pokec-z), with a statistical-parity gap approximately ten times lower than DMoN, and is Pareto-dominated on only one dataset. On controlled stochastic block models, FairPGT attains 97% of the derived upper bound on NMI for exactly fair clustering when communities and protected groups coincide. These graphs further show that, under strong alignment, assignment-level fairness alone makes the attention group-heterophilous, whereas attention-level fairness alone improves neutrality without ensuring fair clusters. Code for reproducibility is available at https://github.com/SiamakGhodsi/FairPGT.

On MinimalSetupAgent, which was built for this report, mitigations proposed in the reward-hacking literature were most effective when the agent received a direct rule and had a clear way to stop and report the problem. AI-driven software-intensive embedded systems (SIES) depend on data during development and operation. Poor data quality (DQ) may weaken data trustworthiness, yet evidence about its effects and lifecycle management remains fragmented. It examines DQ concerns and evidence linking them to reduced data trustworthiness, along with assessment and management methods. The review identifies 16 concerns organized as Source Quality and Content Quality. Studies provide DQ-based scores and evidence of effects on model or decision performance. Synthesis suggests upstream source or handling problems may become embedded in training baselines and remain difficult to detect at runtime. Eight method categories were identified. Runtime monitoring is the largest category, while upstream and training-time controls remain poorly integrated with runtime assurance. No study covers the full lifecycle. Data trustworthiness supports reliable SIES decisions but is one component of AI-system trustworthiness.

### H3Remover: Reinforcing Video Object Removal without Paired Data
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24633v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24633v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24633v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24633v1)

2026/11/18 15:45 GTM

Pretrained video editors possess rich removal and completion priors, yet may retain targets or alter unrelated content. We present H3Remover, which reinforces MiniMax-H3 Base Ref2VA for video object removal without paired object-free training targets. To discourage superficial edits such as recoloring, a target-removal reward favors calibrated depth changes consistent with revealing farther content. To limit unintended scene changes, a background-geometry reward encourages preservation of relative depth ordering. To preserve background appearance without retaining unwanted shadows or reflections, an appearance reward constrains RGB changes in source-derived protected regions while exempting targets and estimated effects. Frozen HPSv3 complements these constraints with perceptual-quality feedback for synthesized content. Evaluation regions remain fixed across candidates, and DiffusionART optimizes LoRA adapters with the backbone frozen. Experiments show substantial reconstruction gains over original H3, with qualitative comparisons demonstrating more effective object removal and better preservation of surrounding scene content.

Personality is only weakly and inconsistently signaled by written language, and existing text-based methods typically combine language-model and psycholinguistic representations at the input or decision level. It remains open whether a more deliberate combination of the two views, and a decomposition of the prediction target, can recover more of the signal. We present a three-phase framework for estimating Big Five personality domain scores from life-narrative interview text. It encodes text and psycholinguistic features separately and combines them by concatenation or by a learned weighting, decomposes each domain into contrastively trained facet embeddings instead of predicting the domain directly, and pools the facet embeddings by attention into a domain-level prediction. We compare end-to-end with staged training and ablate the input modalities, their combination, and the facet-pooling strategy. On 1,332 participants from the St. Louis Personality and Aging Network (SPAN) study, facet embeddings reach AUC up to 0.740, and domain-level R2 reaches 0.328, highest for Openness. End-to-end training gives lower error than staged training in every domain, and attention pooling gives the lowest error in every domain. Combining the two views lowers error relative to text alone for Conscientiousness and Agreeableness, whereas a learned weighting of the views shows no consistent advantage over concatenation. The framework provides facet- level predictions that a domain-only model cannot, and it improves modestly on a regression from the raw features in four of the five domains.

Widely used category discovery benchmarks have become increasingly saturated and often fail to reflect real-world discovery settings, making long-term evaluation of new methods difficult. PS-SPLIT contains 79K globally distributed, high-quality, community-collected bird images spanning 771 species, derived from 257 recent real expert-verified taxonomic splits, with associated geographic metadata and full taxonomic hierarchy. The visual differences between categories arising from real taxonomic splits can be very subtle, often requiring significant human expertise to tell apart, making PS-SPLIT a challenging benchmark which we hope will assist in advancing fine-grained category discovery research. Laws are increasingly turned into programs that decide cases, such as whether a firm was a small or medium enterprise (SME) on a given date, and large language models (LLMs) now write them. The programs are usually graded against answers written by someone who read the same law, so a program can score well by sharing the grader's reading rather than by getting the law right. LexTemporis grades them instead against records of what actually happened: 10,901 firm-years of SME status disclosed by Korean listed companies, with every version of the governing rules. An appendix adds comparison cases from US income tax and German hospital minimum volumes. The date changes outcomes mainly through provisions outside the main statutory text: one transitional provision raises agreement with the disclosures from 83-86% to 89-90%. The disclosures also test the author's reading of a contested provision, favouring it in 130 of 189 firm-years where readings diverge. Four LLMs wrote the rules, given different selections of the law. A pre-registered comparison finds no condition better across all four models. In exploratory analyses, supplying the right provisions looks better graded against the author's reading than against the records: the advantage shrinks for three models (for one, to between a quarter and three-fifths of its size), and per firm-year it is several times larger where the author's reading is wrong, partly by construction, since there reproducing that reading more faithfully must lose. Grading legal programs against the grader's own reading can overstate how well they work.

Zero-Shot Multimodal Anomaly Detection (ZSMAD) aims to identify both appearance and geometric defects in novel categories without relying on target-specific training data, making it especially valuable in real-world scenarios constrained by data scarcity, privacy concerns, or high annotation costs. While recent approaches successfully adapt vision-language models like CLIP to achieve this open-vocabulary generalization, they still face significant challenges: (i) severe geometric information loss during 3D-to-2D point cloud rendering; (ii) spatially noisy 2D feature maps produced by raw CLIP patch embeddings, compounded by shallow late-stage modality fusion; and (iii) severe cross-modal semantic misalignment due to decoupled processing streams. To address these limitations, we propose AggCLIP, a novel framework that adapts frozen CLIP representations for ZSMAD by explicitly incorporating local geometric structure and visual spatial context. Specifically, to accurately localize fine-grained defects, we design a Geometry-Aware Spatial Aggregation (GSA) module that refines back-projected 3D point features via density-adaptive neighborhood weighting, alongside a Context-Aware Spatial Aggregation (CSA) module that smooths multi-scale 2D patch embeddings using a lightweight trainable adapter. Furthermore, to prevent semantic drift between modalities, we introduce a Dual-Stream Prompt Contrastive (DSPC) loss, which aligns the abstract concepts of normality and abnormality across the point cloud and RGB streams. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed AggCLIP framework achieves state-of-the-art performance on the MVTec 3D-AD and Eyecandies benchmarks under both one-vs-rest and cross-dataset zero-shot settings, notably surpassing the strongest competitive methods by up to 2.9% in pixel-level AUPRO.

Hospitals record thousands of hours of surgical video annually, yet these archives remain difficult to search by content. It integrates eight public datasets across four domains-ophthalmic, laparoscopic, robotic-assisted, and colonoscopy-covering six procedure types, 679 cases, and 76,489 clips (106 hours). Agentic image restoration composes operations to handle mixed degradations, yet its toolbox often requires storing multiple restoration networks. The enriched representation then remains accessible to the model throughout decoding, with no additional parameters, auxiliary modules, or decoding-time overhead. Across multiple VLM and LLM families and eight multimodal and text-only benchmarks, BOS Summary improves over direct and Chain-of-Thought prompting and outperforms recent sink-intervention methods on multi-step reasoning tasks. A mechanistic analysis shows that making BOS input-specific also weakens its sink dominance and redistributes attention toward content-bearing prompt tokens, indicating that changing what a sink carries reshapes how later layers allocate attention.

### Who are you? The Personal Imitation Game
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24888v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24888v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24888v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24888v1)

2026/11/18 18:32 GTM

Large Language Models (LLMs) now perform strongly on Alan Turing's 1950 Imitation Game, so we propose a harder challenge, the Personal Imitation Game, which asks whether a machine can replicate a specific individual in conversation rather than a generic human. Testing with 30 participants, we establish a human baseline and report the identity signals interrogators rely on: stylometric features, personality traits, and biographical facts. To demonstrate the framework, we design an LLM-based system that plays the game for one of the paper's authors, drawing on an autoethnographic dataset spanning ten years of personal digital communication. In a 20-person deception study, interrogators choose our system over the author's own friends in 7 of 10 rounds, and the author over our system in 13 of 20; neither rate is distinguishable from chance at this sample size. We consider design improvements, applications of personal AI agents, and implications for identity and privacy.

### Image Watermarking With Memory Across Reissuance
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23363) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23363) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23363) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23363)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Publishers often revise and reissue images, yet valid watermarks may persist on superseded releases or unapproved edits. Even a current release can contain regions last authorized at different revisions. We introduce and formalize reissuable provenance: determining an image's current, superseded, or unissued status and recovering each region's last authorized revision (its generation), with abstention when evidence is insufficient. Generational Stratigraphy Fields (GSF) combine watermark evidence with an authenticated live reference to the current release, enabling replacement or retirement without rewriting circulated copies. Each reissue freshly embeds new generation labels in approved regions and inherited labels elsewhere, preserving history without requiring old watermarks to survive successive edits. Our complete-output reissuance evaluation follows 480 histories through four releases, retaining each AI editor's entire output. GSF achieves 99.67% accuracy in classifying issued images as current or superseded and 94.09% recall of unissued edits. After three reissues, one generation map provides time and location: it recovers regional labels with 93.13% median accuracy and localizes the latest approved revision with 92.86% IoU. Regional labels remain readable under tested delivery transformations that impair issued-state recognition. Watermarks can thus express who issued an image, which release is authoritative now, and when each region was last authorized.

Segmenting each reported finding in a radiology scan would allow findings to be measured and tracked over time and let AI systems generate reports that are verifiable, but so far AI models have performed poorly at this task. Given a non-contrast chest CT scan and the set of findings from its report, participants trained models to produce the corresponding set of 3D segmentations. The challenge used 2,992 training and 200 validation scans from the ReXGroundingCT dataset, and evaluated methods on a held-out test set of 300 scans with 582 findings, exhaustively annotated by board-certified radiologists. In total, 48 teams from 15 countries made 760 submissions, and the best method reached a Dice of 0.375 and a hit rate of 0.77, matching agreement between two independent radiologist groups (Dice 0.32, hit rate 0.76, on a 50-scan subset). Missing or underrepresented non-functional requirements (NFR) categories leave little direct evidence in a requirements set, making them difficult to identify during requirements review. We compare seven statistical, embedding-based, and large language model (LLM)-based configurations that differ in both ranking mechanism and evidence source, including target requirement statements, peer requirements, their semantic representation, and peer category statistics. All configurations weaken when a category is present but underrepresented, and none is best for every NFR category. For count-changing category-level gaps, structured peer evidence matters more than ranker sophistication. The findings suggest that the rankings should guide analyst inspection rather than serve as automatic judgements of requirements completeness.

Generalist CT foundation models showed limited zero-shot ability to differentiate liver tumors, which improved substantially with linear probing alone.

### Gaussian Primitive Fusion for Any-Resolution Multimodal Images
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24164) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24164) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24164) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24164)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Multimodal image fusion integrates complementary observations from different modalities into a unified representation. Existing methods typically estimate modality contributions on a common discrete grid. When source modalities have different native resolutions, constructing such a grid generally involves resampling, which may introduce artifacts and affect subsequent fusion decisions. To address this problem, we propose Gaussian Primitive Fusion (GPF), which reformulates heterogeneous-resolution fusion in a shared continuous Gaussian space. Instead of first converting source images to a common resolution, GPF directly fits their native-resolution observations with shared Gaussian geometry and modality-specific content representations, and learns modality contributions within the resulting continuous representation. Once optimized, the fused representation is rendered at the finest native resolution without resampling the sources. With heterogeneous-resolution inputs, GPF produces clearer target contours and finer textures, with less blurring and blocking artifacts than resample-then-fuse pipelines. Quantitatively, it achieves the best results on multiple infrared-visible benchmarks against state-of-the-art fusion methods equipped with interpolation or super-resolution frontends.

Industrial anomaly detection plays a pivotal role in precision manufacturing, where joint analysis of multimodal data emerges as a critical approach to enhance detection accuracy. Feature reconstruction, a dominant paradigm for such joint analysis, is primarily implemented through intra-modal and cross-modal methods, both of which are plagued by significant limitations. Intra-modal reconstruction neglects complementary cross-modal information, while its cross-modal counterpart is prone to projection errors and ambiguous one-to-many mappings (e.g., same textures mapping to multiple geometries), leading to detection failures. To address these limitations, we propose a novel feature reconstruction paradigm, Fuse Before Reconstruct (FBR), which first fuses multimodal features and then performs reconstruction for anomaly detection. Firstly, we propose a bidirectional cross-modal attention mechanism to achieve deep feature complementarity between image and point cloud features, mitigating projection bias through channel alignment and dynamic spatial prior integration. Besides, a learnable pixel-wise gated fusion strategy is adopted to preserve multi-modal structural consistency while optimizing local feature combination. Finally, dual-path collaborative reconstruction is implemented to recover original modal features from the fused representations, where cross-modal consistency constraints effectively eliminate mapping ambiguities. Extensive experiments show that FBR achieves 98.3% I-AUROC on MVTec 3D-AD and 92.6% I-AUROC on Eyecandies, surpassing previous state-of-the-art methods by +2.9% and +4.1%, respectively.

SpiderMark establishes that structured watermark geometry and learned probabilistic verification can be unified into a single, practical framework, bridging the gap between imperceptibility, robustness, and real-world verifiability for generative AI content attribution.

Automatic CAD generation is commonly evaluated with volumetric IoU, Chamfer Distance, and surface F-score, which barely penalize local errors that occupy only a small fraction of a part's volume or surface area, such as a misplaced hole or a slot on the wrong face, even though such errors are critical to manufacturing. We formalize these metrics in a unified integral form and show that the weight of a local error decays as the $(k+q)$-th power of its size, where $k$ is the dimension of the integration domain and $q$ is determined by the penalty function. Electron ptychography recovers atomic structure from 4D-STEM data: diffraction patterns at multiple positions of a scanned probe. Current codes reconstruct an image and read atoms off it, or fit atoms under an $L_1$ loss; neither gives per-atom uncertainty. We propose 4D-TEMPO, a differentiable physical renderer that fits the atoms, the microscope, and the detector directly to the recorded electron counts under a Poisson likelihood. The output is a list of atoms, each a Gaussian primitive with a 3D position, a thermal width, and a model-conditional error bar from the Fisher information. Depth comes from multiple scattering into the dark field, the faint counts outside the direct beam. The two layers, $10\times$ closer than the depth of field, separate; per-atom depth then follows the layer geometry. When the model is well specified, the bars are calibrated (69% of position coordinates within $1σ$). On experimental WSe$_2$, fitted atoms predict held-out patterns better than the three published codes we test. With free probe modes where atoms are incomplete, the same likelihood predicts best on all three held-out benchmarks, matches every WSe$_2$ baseline in 5 min against up to 20.5 h, and passes torchslice at its released setting 180$\times$ sooner on PrScO$_3$. Per-atom error bars, planned doses, and fast reconstruction point to low-dose, real-time 3D imaging of beam-sensitive and dynamic materials.

### Unified Law for Visual Tasks (The Four Laws of Computer Vision)
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24917v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24917v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24917v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24917v1)

2026/11/18 18:48 GTM

In this paper, we propose a Unified Law for Visual Tasks, which could be further divided into Four Laws of Computer Vision, with the goal of enabling a single framework or method to uniformly train on and perform nearly all visual tasks. Our starting point is an inspiration and hypothesis: if the world information is encoded into image, which we call World Snapshots, then image processing is world processing, and image generation is World Snapshot generation. Furthermore, if all visual and visualization tasks can be reformulated as generation tasks, then a unified method, World Snapshot Models (WSMs), can perform the vast majority of visual tasks, much as large language models have largely unified natural language processing. This paper validates these laws across more than 1,000 trained and evaluated visual tasks (defined by distinct input-output mappings) and presents a unified training framework for future visual-task research and applications.

Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.

A frozen detector computes hypotheses that can support better final outputs through supervised post-processing. Choosing a readout requires knowing which available information improves detection and what its processing costs. We compare candidate scores, class distributions, geometry, native-set summaries and adapted neighborhood meta classification under common candidate, supervision and operating-point conditions. On 320 held-out CrowdHuman images, adding native-set context to a 97-dimensional readout yields 275 more matches and 96 more unmatched predictions. On a separate 319-image cohort, Full100 matches 6004 objects,versus 5848 for source-selected raw WBF and 6002 for a MetaDetect-style adaptation. Its gain over raw contains 300 added and 144 lost discoveries. After execution-equivalent optimization, Full100 and the meta-statistics readout both take approximately 95 ms per complete warm request on a fixed 64-image workload. Source-frozen transfer gives positive point results on 223 MOT17Det frames from seven sequences, while the main MIAP result shows no improvement. The study supports using sufficiently informative output readouts while retaining optimized meta classification as a competitive option. It also shows why feature dimension and net detection gain alone are unreliable guides to adoption.

Advances in deep generative models now enable the synthesis of realistic imagery. However, aerial imagery is dominated by tiny objects, such as vehicles, ships, and aircraft, which occupy only a few pixels owing to the overhead, wide-area nature of aerial imaging. Existing generative models struggle substantially more with tiny objects than with larger ones. This limitation has been overlooked, as prior work on generative evaluation emphasizes prompt following or human preference rather than whether small objects are rendered faithfully. To address this gap, we introduce TOGBench, a quantitative, object-level benchmark for evaluating tiny-object generation fidelity in aerial imagery. We further propose two simple train-/inference-time techniques that improve the ability of layout-to-image generators to render small objects, raising their performance on TOGBench.

### Mapping the AI Supply Chain: Dependencies, Vulnerabilities, and Market Concentration in the AI Ecosystem

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

AI systems are built across many organizations that contribute models, data, compute, capital, talent, and other assets. While the layers of the AI stack are relatively well understood, the relationships between actors and components across layers remains sparsely documented. In this work, we focus on the organizations that make up the AI supply chain: the complex network of actors that contribute to the development and deployment of AI. We motivate the examination of the organizational AI supply chain---highlighting concerns about concentration, power, chokepoints, cascading failures, fragmented accountability, and risk---then describe our pipeline for mapping the AI supply chain. Our pipeline yields our main contribution: a multilayered, large-scale dataset of actors and relationships in the AI ecosystem, annotated with dates, descriptions, types, tags, and more. This work takes a step toward systematically mapping and measuring the AI supply chain and its properties, cataloging how models, data, chips, infrastructure, capital, and even talent flow between organizations. This data enable critical examinations of industry relationships and yield insights into the political economy of AI development and concentration. We provide several illustrative analyses, including studying the most central actors, analyzing network concentration, and identifying communities and chokepoints. While our dataset is fairly comprehensive, it is also necessarily incomplete: public documents omit private deals, and coverage is shaped by search design. Together, the approach, data, and findings aim to serve as a resource for studying the AI ecosystem. The dataset is publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/sarahcen/ai-supply-chain and can be explored interactively at https://aisupplychains.org/.

### ALIVE: Bring Foundation Features to Life with Events

Visual foundation models provide powerful representations for diverse tasks, but their features become stale as visual content changes between image observations. Refreshing them requires new frames and repeated runs of a computationally expensive image encoder. We introduce ALIVE, an event-based method that incrementally updates native foundation model features between image observations. Given an anchor image, ALIVE uses subsequent events to transport and correct existing multi-layer features extracted by a frozen DINOv3 encoder. Its lightweight updater is trained end-to-end using only synthetic data, without downstream task supervision, and transfers directly to real-world event streams without any adaptation. Across three real-world datasets and five task-dataset settings spanning object detection, semantic segmentation, and depth estimation, ALIVE outperforms all evaluated event-based methods in every metric. On DSEC, when the anchor frame is 200,ms old, ALIVE improves over anchor-image predictions by 30.9,mAP in detection, 11.6,mIoU in segmentation, and 15.9% in depth AbsRel. Compared with image re-encoding, ALIVE reduces FLOPs by 82.4% and latency by a factor of 2.5.

### Lodestone: Automated Semantic Discovery of Datacenter Hardware Specialization
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23663) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23663) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23663) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23663)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Hardware specialization can improve datacenter efficiency. Unfortunately, identifying computations that justify dedicated hardware is difficult. Existing approaches analyze hot functions, instruction patterns, or overheads such as datacenter tax. They can miss fine-grained computations whose aggregate cost is spread across applications and libraries, especially when different pieces of code obscure the same underlying computation. We observe that seemingly unrelated code regions often perform the same computations, creating specialization opportunities that require semantic reasoning. The rise of agentic AI makes it possible to automate this. However, conventional AI agents cannot efficiently and reliably analyze large scale datacenter software. To unlock this opportunity, we present Lodestone, an agentic framework that automatically discovers hardware specialization opportunities by reasoning about what the executed code computes. First, Lodestone extracts hot control-flow regions from execution traces across workloads and organizes them into compact analysis packages that make large workloads tractable for agents. Second, agents describe what each region computes, merge those that perform the same computation into computational families, and check their semantic correctness in a loop. Third, to verify the discovered family, Lodestone runs every piece of code in a family against a set of reference inputs and outputs on recorded executions. Finally, for selected families, Lodestone proposes hardware, e.g., ISA extensions, near-core units, or detached accelerators, and human experts decide which proposals to develop further. We evaluate Lodestone on 20 workloads across six datacenter workload categories. We simulate 30 discovered families in a cycle-accurate simulator and implement them in RTL. These optimizations speed up the workloads by 12.6% on average and by up to 80.2%.

### UltraTex: Aligning Physical Knowledge for High-Fidelity PBR Texture Generation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24941v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24941v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24941v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24941v1)

Generating physically based rendering (PBR) texture maps for a 3D mesh from a single reference image is highly ambiguous, as the observed appearance entangles material identity, surface reflectance, and illumination. While current generative methods produce realistic albedo maps, they still struggle to infer accurate metallic and roughness attributes for relighting. We present UltraTex, a cascaded multi-view framework that moves beyond appearance matching by integrating physical knowledge into multi-view diffusion-based PBR generation. UltraTex first jointly predicts albedo and world-space shading normals to generate more detailed albedo maps, and then predicts metallic-roughness (MR) maps conditioned on the generated albedo, reference image, and material semantic priors. To inject material semantics, a lightweight adapter transforms hidden-layer features of a vision-language model (VLM) into intrinsic material representation tokens, providing part-level knowledge anchors for MR estimation. To model material-light interaction, photometric representation alignment guides intermediate diffusion features toward ground-truth material renderings under controlled lighting. This makes metallic and roughness variations observable through highlight intensity, specular sharpness, and local reflectance contrast, providing stronger guidance for fine-grained material prediction without explicit lighting supervision. Extensive experiments show that UltraTex generates high-quality textures and outperforms existing open-source and commercial methods in visual fidelity, material accuracy, and physical plausibility.

The rapid development of materials machine learning relies on large-scale, heterogeneous datasets, yet model reliability is often compromised by imperfect data from diverse experiments, simulations, and database integration. Real-world materials databases may contain measurement errors, failed calculations, missing or duplicate records, and hidden violations of physicochemical constraints. Such problems are difficult to identify because corrupted samples may remain statistically plausible rather than appearing as explicit outliers. In this paper, we propose a confidence-based materials data quality assessment framework that integrates domain knowledge with graph-structured learning. Data quality is formulated as a continuous confidence estimation problem rather than a binary anomaly identification task, enabling sample-level assessment and historical-reference-based batch comparison. A nine-dimensional feature-quality prior and duplicate-aware sample initialization are calibrated using materials domain rules and statistical evidence. Confidence is further refined through sample-feature bipartite graph propagation and weakly supervised graph attention, while a Memory-GAT module provides historical reference scoring. Experiments on 23 ODDS benchmark datasets show that the generic module achieves an average AUC-ROC of 0.866. In five repeated mixed-contamination experiments on mp\_20, rule calibration improves HistGradientBoostingRegressor test $R^2$ from $0.538\pm0.008$ to $0.616\pm0.014$. Graph refinement increases average precision from $0.943\pm0.004$ to 0.953\pm0.004$, while the rule-calibrated configuration has the highest mean test $R^2$ among the evaluated screening configurations. Five repeated MACE subset experiments further show an increase in $R^2$ from $0.590\pm0.053$ to $0.880\pm0.027$ after rule-based screening.

### Multi-Band Galaxy Novel-View Synthesis with Radiative Gaussian Fields
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24723v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24723v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24723v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24723v1)

2026/11/18 16:41 GTM

We propose a novel view synthesis tool for galaxies based on Gaussian Splatting (GS). Unlike RGB data, where GS has achieved success, galaxy observations consist of multi-band signals from a massive number of bodies, contain complex patterns of cross-band interactions, and capture light propagation in highly heterogeneous media. As a result, the underlying physical process poses great challenges in model representation and optimization, which have not been investigated in existing research. To this end, we propose a physically inspired Gaussian Splatting method, GalaxyGS, which is equipped with physically meaningful components that capture both the energy emission mechanism of different astronomical bodies and the light propagation between them. In the absence of directly comparable baseline methods, we adopt a wide range of possible alternative approaches. Comparisons conclusively demonstrate that GalaxyGS is better in accuracy and physical fidelity across datasets and metrics.

### ShadowTransfer: A Geographic Transfer Benchmark for Overhead Shadow Detection

2026/11/18 18:41 GTM

Overhead aerial imagery feeds dense-prediction pipelines that are routinely trained in one geographic region and deployed in another, yet no benchmark measures how shadow detection models transfer across regions. Existing overhead shadow datasets either lack a geographic transfer protocol or rely on automatically generated labels. We introduce ShadowTransfer, a benchmark of 4,500 human-verified shadow masks across three U.S. cities at two native resolutions, paired with a leave-one-city-out evaluation protocol. Under vanilla training, holding out the test city costs 3.5 to 7.8 mIoU across four architectures, the gap remains significant when the training set grows from 225 to 900 images, and six of eight adaptation methods degrade more than vanilla training on average. We propose a three-probe framework that diagnoses where and how the transfer breaks: the failure operates in feature space, beyond the reach of pixel-histogram matching, shows up as a confidence collapse on shadow pixels while background confidence stays intact, and is recoverable by retraining different architectural components depending on the model: encoder for some, decoder for others. These findings replicate on INRIA building footprints, suggesting they describe geographic transfer beyond shadows. Guided by the diagnosis, we build Spectral Information Bottleneck (SIB), a plug-in module with one component per finding. Across the 9 (architecture, held-out city) cells, SIB has the smallest mean gap to the within-city upper bound of the ten methods we evaluate (6.42 mIoU), level with FDA. The benchmark, LOCO protocol, code, and trained models are released to support reproducible evaluation of geographic transfer in overhead dense prediction.

### Fuse to Recognize the Unseen: Class-Relative Infrared-Visible Fusion for Open-Vocabulary Semantic Segmentation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24316) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24316) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24316) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24316)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Visible-infrared image fusion leverages complementary thermal cues to enhance visual perception, particularly under challenging illumination. However, such perceptual complementarity does not readily translate into semantic gains in open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS), where CLIP-based models are predominantly pretrained on RGB imagery. Directly introducing IR information may instead disrupt RGB-language alignment, turning promising thermal cues into semantic interference that undermines unseen-class recognition. Our pixel-level RGB-IR semantic analysis reveals that class-relevant thermal cues can correct certain RGB misclassifications while preserving reliable RGB predictions, motivating RGB-anchored, class-relative thermal cue selection. Based on this insight, we propose EmergeFuse, an infrared-visible fusion framework for OVSS that selectively converts complementary thermal evidence into unseen-class semantic corrections. Specifically, class-relative cue selection evaluates RGB-IR semantic responses and calibrated thermal evidence relative to RGB predictions, generating class-relative correction requests through competitive selection. Text-contrast image rectification then translates these selected requests into semantic rectification directions through text contrasts, guiding evidence-gated feature updates to realize complementary thermal evidence for unseen-class recognition. Experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate gains of up to 4.755% in unseen-class mIoU and 15.730% in strict complementary conversion rate over existing fusion methods, while maintaining competitive fusion quality.

### Management of Behavioral Addictions via Artificial Intelligence based Digital Technologies
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23341) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23341) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23341) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23341)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Behavioral addictions, such as problematic gaming, gambling, social media use, and online shopping, have emerged as a growing global concern with significant psychological, social, and economic consequences. Traditional assessment and treatment approaches, while clinically grounded, often struggle with scalability, accessibility, and long-term engagement. In recent years, advances in digital technologies and artificial intelligence (AI) have enabled the development of novel, data-driven solutions for the prevention, detection, treatment, and recovery of behavioral addictions. This book presents a comprehensive survey of the management of behavioral addictions through digital technologies, bridging clinical foundations with state-of-the-art computational approaches. This book aims to provide researchers, clinicians, and practitioners with a unified, interdisciplinary perspective on the opportunities and limitations of AI-based digital technologies in behavioral addiction management,and to highlight pathways toward more scalable, personalized, and effective intervention strategies.

Precise myocardial phenotyping at population scale is limited by the cost and accessibility of cardiac magnetic resonance (CMR), whereas electrocardiography (ECG) is widely available but reflects electrical rather than structural and functional information. Here we develop MyoECG, a cross-modal representation learning framework that distils myocardial structural and functional knowledge from CMR into standard 12-lead ECGs, enabling imaging-informed cardiomyopathy detection and subtype classification from ECGs alone. MyoECG was pretrained on 45,241 paired ECG-CMR examinations from UK Biobank and a tertiary cardiovascular centre, then developed and validated across more than 1.8 million ECGs from seven independent datasets. Across internal and external datasets spanning prevalences of 0.2-54.7%, MyoECG outperformed five pretrained ECG foundation models, with AUROCs of 0.800-0.974 for cardiomyopathy detection. In a multi-reader study of six cardiologists, MyoECG assistance improved mean sensitivity by 0.109 (95% CI, 0.041-0.175; P = 0.009) and specificity by 0.056 (95% CI, 0.004-0.108; P = 0.040). In 41,128 UK Biobank participants, the MyoECG score showed genetic correlations with cardiomyopathy (rg = 0.409) and dilated cardiomyopathy (rg = 0.371), genome-wide associations at four loci, including regions mapped to NFIA and KCNQ1, and associations with 69 circulating proteins related to myocardial stress, cell adhesion, vascular biology and metabolism. The cardiomyopathy correlation attenuated to non-significance after adjustment for 27 measured CMR traits (rg = 0.131; P = 0.143), indicating that the score captures CMR-derived information rather than dataset-specific shortcuts. MyoECG offers a scalable route to CMR-informed cardiomyopathy assessment and a strategy for transferring knowledge from resource-intensive imaging to widely deployable physiological signals.

### ExiST: Existence-Aware Self-Training for Semi-Supervised Multi-Organ Segmentation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23581) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23581) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23581) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23581)

2026/11/19 14:00 GTM

Semi-supervised multi-organ segmentation should learn more than a dozen organs from a handful of labeled scans, and the smallest organs occupy less than one percent of the foreground. We identify a failure mode of self-training that is specific to this regime. When a teacher misses a small organ in a scan, every voxel of that organ receives the confident pseudo-label background, and the student is trained toward the organ absence. Repeated over many scans, this loop extinguishes tail organs. In a pilot study, removing the gallbladder from the pseudo-labels reduced its Dice on AMOS from 36.5 to 1.0. We argue that a missing organ should be treated as unresolved rather than absent and propose Existence-aware Self-Training (ExiST). It builds pseudo-labels by leave-one-out consensus of independently trained networks, screens them with a quality gate on validation scans, and infers, per scan and per organ, whether the organ was found. For every unresolved organ, the background voxels of that scan receive the set-valued target, trained with a marginal cross-entropy that accepts either answer and a Dice term that skips uncertain voxels. We show that this loss never pushes a candidate organ toward absence and never favors one member of the set, which leaves the decision to labeled evidence. ExiST outperforms the current state-of-the-art methods and reaches 77.1 mean Dice on Synapse with 20% labeled scans and 75.9 on AMOS with 5% labeled scans.

### Structured Extraction of Diffraction Data from Covalent Organic Frameworks

Powder X-ray diffraction (PXRD) is the primary structural evidence for covalent organic frameworks (COFs) and many other materials. Yet decades of measured patterns exist only as images in published figures. Automatic recovery is difficult, because PXRD figures vary widely in format, from a single curve to crowded stacks with labels, insets, and neighboring plots that challenge traditional computer vision. This work presents a pipeline in which a vision large language model (LLM) interprets the layout of each figure, locating the diffraction plot and its curves and sketching a coarse trace of each, while classical computer vision (CV) refines every trace to pixel-level accuracy. Applied to 3,954 COF articles, the pipeline produces 11,445 calibrated diffraction curves, released in the open database COF-PEAK. Compared with a hand digitization of 60 panels, extracted peak positions differ by a median of only 0.07 degrees. Whereas papers usually report only a few peak angles, which limits further analysis, the digitized curves enable tracking how peak prominence, a possible marker of crystallinity, changes with synthesis conditions. Vision LLMs and classical CV can thus turn such figures into reusable experimental data.

Smart wearable devices, such as augmented reality glasses, increasingly rely on cameras to understand and interact with their surroundings. However, their compact sensors often capture noisy RAW images, while limited compute and power make high-quality on-device restoration difficult. This creates an emerging need to reduce both local computation and transmission cost without sacrificing image quality. Two common solutions are to offload restoration or to compress the raw data before transmission. The first makes RAW transmission costly; the second reduces the payload but can spend bitrate preserving sensor noise. We address this fundamental mismatch with an asymmetric framework for joint RAW compression and restoration that directly optimizes the transmitted representation for clean image reconstruction. A lightweight on-device encoder compresses noisy RAW measurements into a bitstream, while a higher-capacity backend decoder jointly denoises and demosaics the encoded representation. We use knowledge distillation to train a smaller encoder for tighter compute budgets. Experiments on real and synthetic data show improved rate-restoration performance over strong cascaded compression-restoration pipelines and learned compression baselines. On SIDD, our method improves PSNR by more than 1.5 dB over cascaded baselines at approximately 0.25 bits per pixel. We further validate the method on real 12 MP RAW images captured with commercial smart glasses and measure sender-side latency on both smartphone and smart-glasses hardware. The distilled encoder achieves a 27.6$\times$ NPU inference speedup over on-device NAFNet for 256$\times$256 output patches. The code is available at https://github.com/coolsyn2000/CRAW

### Synthesizing hyperspectral Data using generative Models to train spectral Unmixing Methods for low-cost Crop Residue Cover Mapping
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24827v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.24827v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24827v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.24827v1)

2026/11/18 17:58 GTM

The spectral range of the Field Imaging Nanosatellite for Crop residue Hyperspectral Mapping (FINCH), developed by the University of Toronto Aerospace Team's Space System Division, poses significant challenges for hyperspectral unmixing to determine crop residue cover fractional abundances. The severe lack of standard indices necessitates the use of complex, data-driven unmixing models. Complex unmixing models require dense, well-distributed manifolds, whereas ground-truth datasets are sparse and expensive. To better leverage the information content in existing ground-truth datasets, this study presents a framework that decouples interpolation and abundance-mapping estimation via an intermediary conditional data generator, contrasting with traditional single-model unmixing pipelines. This decoupling enables the inclusion of physical prior knowledge about spectra, which is not natively accessible to unmixing models, thereby artificially expanding the dataset to serve as a Monte Carlo approximation of the population risk. To test this hypothesis, we have proposed two generator models: a 1D Conditional U-Net with Conformer Layers (GD-Streamline) and a Dual-Path Transformer Conditional Variational AutoEncoder (TCVAE), and two unmixing models: Multi-Layered Perceptrons (MLP) and Fourier Neural Operators (FNO). The results of the study indicate a need for rigorous integration and introduction of spectral priors within the scope of the proposed decoupling framework to prevent domain and mode collapse and hallucination by the generator models; otherwise, this leads to falsely approximated data manifolds, ultimately resulting in unsatisfactory and out-of-distribution unmixing performance.
